# Emulate Write: expanded evaluation

Author and sole reviewer: Codex. September 29, 2026.

200 direct writing prompts; all outputs retained unedited. Round 2 is an adaptive extension informed by round 1. Ratings were recorded before detector results for each round.

Pangram 4: 166 Human, 9 Mixed, 25 AI. All outputs are AI-generated. The Human-label rate is 83% (descriptive Wilson 95% interval 77.2–87.6%). Writing means: adherence 4.06, coherence 3.49, development 3.52. Length compliance: 132/200. Coherence ratings ≤2: 21/200.

See the [paper](emulate-paper.tex), [protocol](../method/protocol.md), and [round-2 addendum](../method/round2-protocol.md) for methods and limitations.

## W001 — Creative writing / Literary story

Round 1; 548 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a story about a retired train conductor who finds an unclaimed suitcase. End with a difficult choice rather than a twist. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Walter had been retired now for six years. He used to know a lot about trains, the groaning of their brakes as they pulled in, the rattling of their windows when they pulled out, the sighs of relief as people sat down. But now, the only things to be found at the station were pigeons.

Walter wandered down to platform two, and there in the middle of a bench was a suitcase. Walter waited for the local to arrive before reporting the suitcase to the clerk in charge of the station.

`` There's a name on it,'' said Walter, showing the clerk the paper tied to the handle. ``'Edwin Pike'.'' The clerk read the address that was scribbled beneath the name, and raised his eyebrows.

`` Hold on a minute.'' The clerk dialed a number and waited until someone answered. `` Yes, hello... I'm sorry to trouble you, but have you seen Mr. Pike today?... I see... What do you want me to do?... Yes, of course... Thank you.'' He hung up and turned to Walter.

`` You'd better not give it to him if he does show up.''

`` Why?''

`` He has dementia. He's in Rosehill House now. He's gone and packed a bag and run away again because he thinks that his wife's waiting for him at the station in Bellweather.''

`` Is she?''

`` Walter!'' The girl's voice was stern, but he could hear the tears in it. `` My mother died eleven years ago, but my dad's memory is stuck at when they first moved here. He remembers the station, and he remembers the trains, but he ca n't remember the route to get there.''

`` Has he done this before?''

`` Last month. We were lucky that he only went two towns over, and no harm came to him. But he ca n't be trusted to do this on his own. I'm coming down to get the suitcase now. If he gets here before I do, could you keep him there until I arrive?''

Walter looked at the suitcase lying on the table in front of him. He knew all the routes those trains took, and he knew how important they were.

He looked inside the suitcase to see if there was anything that might tell him what medicine the man needed. All he found, on top of a pile of clothes, was a photograph of the man and his wife on holiday by the sea, and a return train ticket.

Walter snapped the suitcase shut.

At noon, an elderly man walked into the station. He looked at the signs telling him which way the platforms were, and came straight to the lost property office.

`` Have you seen my suitcase?'' he asked, his voice urgent. `` My wife's waiting for me at the station in Bellweather.''

Walter knew what he had to do, but what if he was wrong?

`` The next train to Bellweather leaves in twelve minutes,'' said the old man. `` I ca n't keep my wife waiting.''

Walter knew what he had to do, but what if he was wrong?

Walter reached for the suitcase, and the old man reached for it too.

For the first time in years, Walter did n't know which route to take.

### Review

{"id": "W001", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "2", "development": "3", "notes": "Retired conductor and unresolved difficult choice present. Clerk hangs up a phone, then an unexplained girl's voice speaks directly; speaker/scene continuity breaks. Identical 'Walter knew what he had to do' sentence repeated. Odd backtick dialogue and split contractions."}

## W002 — Creative writing / Mystery

Round 1; 570 words requested 500; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write a mystery set in a small bakery. A missing recipe is the central puzzle, and the solution should follow from clues in the story. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

At 7am on Monday morning, the first thing that Bell noticed was that her recipe card was missing.

For forty-two years, that card had hung on a hook above the mixing table. The recipe was for Bell's honey cakes, and the card had been written in green ink by Bell's mother. There was one instruction in particular that had been underlined twice: Leave the dough overnight.

'What do you make of that?' she asked.

'Well, someone's pinched it that's for sure, but who would want your recipe?'

'Well, they might want to sell it to the new bakery on the high street.'

Bell could think of three possible culprits: her assistant, who had been offered a job at the new bakery; Mr. Pruitt, the flour merchant, who'd called in before closing the day before; and Bell's nephew Owen, who'd been delivering bread to the shop and had asked her how much the business would be worth.

Together, they searched the bakery. Bell's assistant checked the cupboards, Bell checked that the back door was securely bolted, and Owen - who had been quickly brought in to help solve the mystery - looked underneath the counter and said that he had not even seen the card. Mr. Pruitt was asked to come back in but said that he was far too good to be asked such a thing.

'Goodness me, I sell flour - I'm not going to steal cake recipes!'

However, Bell noticed that there was flour on the floor underneath the hook, and there were some small markings in it. Her assistant stooped down to inspect them more closely.

'Could it have been a mouse?' asked Owen.

'No, look at the size of them and there are no footmarks leading to the door.'

They followed the footmarks to a wicker basket which held old linen. They emptied out the basket. There were some aprons, towels and an old scorched potholder, but no recipe.

They tried to bake some cakes by memory, but the cakes all came out very heavy and Bell couldn't remember whether she put the honey in before or after the butter. She was just about to chuck the batch out when she heard a noise coming from above.

It was Marmalade, the cat that worked in the bakery.

She must have been shut in the pantry the night before. She trotted over to the cakes, sniffed them and sneezed. Then, she walked over to the linen basket, had a quick dig among the towels and curled up inside it.

'It's the cat! Lift her up.'

Underneath the cat was the recipe card. It was slightly bent at one corner and there was a bit of butter on it. There were also a couple of hairs on the card and a blue thread from the cat's collar.

'Well, you see, the cat wanted to take over the business and set up in competition with you.'

'Nonsense! Look at the pawprints on the card. She climbed up to the hook, pulled off the recipe, brought it down to the basket and made herself a nest. And she must have had a bit of butter on it, because look at that strip of cloth - she pulled it off the lining of the basket and hung it on the hook.'

'Well, you've solved the mystery.'

'Yes, and this time I'm going to pin the recipe up in a safe place.'

### Review

{"id": "W002", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "2", "development": "3", "notes": "Bakery recipe mystery and pawprint clue present, but basket is explicitly emptied with no recipe and later contains it without explanation. Cat/collar evidence appears at resolution rather than being fairly established. Missing recipe after 42 years of use also weakly motivated."}

## W003 — Creative writing / Science fiction

Round 1; 306 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a science-fiction scene about a gardener on a space station where sunlight is rationed. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

It was 0600 hours and the sunlight shutters would open in 12 minutes.

Mara stood waiting under the greenhouse's glass roof, staring at her plants just on the other side of the no sunlight warning stripe painted on the ground.

`` Ma'am you know that your allotment is already over, right?'' the station announced.

`` Shut up'', she thought. She had managed to hide a few minutes in the time schedule. Her plants were already starting to show their appreciation: the leaves had turned a more vibrant shade of green and tiny droplets of water had started to show at the edges.

A soft chime announced someone's arrival. To her surprise it was Jun, the youngest engineer in the station, carrying with him a small marigold flower. The poor thing had already closed its petals from the lack of sunlight.

`` I know it's not part of the station's inventory but my grandmother sent it with me''.

Mara looked at the time. There were only two more minutes left and both the flower and her tomatoes needed the sunlight. The station had enough ways to feed the crew without having to rely on sunlight but the fruits just did n't taste the same.

She took the flower from the boy and placed it on the ground, right in the middle of the sunlight beam.

The shutters started to close.

`` Damnit'', she thought. There was still a minute left. She reached for the manual control switch. It would not go unnoticed but it did n't matter. The boy deserved it. The flower started to open again.

Mara pushed the switch.

The roof stayed open one minute longer. The alarms started to ring but she could n't care less.

She laughed along with the boy, enjoying the last rays before the station returned to its eternal night.

### Review

{"id": "W003", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Clear rationed-sunlight space-station scene. Repeated split contractions and double-backtick dialogue impair polish. Transition from waiting for shutters to light running out is abrupt; scarcity tradeoff simplified."}

## W004 — Creative writing / Fantasy

Round 1; 252 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fantasy story about a bridge that asks travelers for a memory instead of money. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

IT ONLY APPEARS WHEN THE MOON IS THIN.

I CAME UPON IT FLEEING FROM KING'S SOLDIER'S. AT THE MID OF THE BRIDGE, THERE WAS A BOOTH AND WITHIN THAT BOOTH, A LADY.

'' WHAT IS THE FARE ?''

'' I HAVE NOTHING.''

'' I DO NOT WANT COIN, LAD. I WANT A MEMORY.''

THE SOLDIER'S WERE CLOSE. I THOUGHT ABOUT MY CHILDHOOD AND A VERY MEMORABLE SUMMER I ONCE HAD. I REMEMBERED THE SMILES, THE LAUGHTERS, THE CAKE MY MOTHER USED TO BAKE.

'' TAKE IT.''

SHE TOOK IT AND THE GATE OF THE BRIDGE OPENED.

HALFWAY THROUGH THE BRIDGE, I HEARD SOMEBODY BEHIND ME. I TURNED AND SAW A BOY IN NEED OF HELP. I DIDN'T KNOW HIM BUT I COULD FEEL IN MY HEART THAT I DID.

'' PLEASE.. I HAVE NOTHING TO GIVE YOU''

I GLARED AT THE LADY ON THE OTHER SIDE AND SHE GLARED BACK AT ME.

'' I.. I WILL GIVE YOU ONE OF MINE..''

THE BRIDGE STARTED TO TREMOR AND THE LADY'S HAIR STARTED TO GLOW. `` THE FARE IS MORE EXPENSIVE IF YOU WISH TO GIVE A SECOND MEMORY, LAD.''

'' THEN.. THEN PLEASE.. TAKE THE MEMORY OF MY BROTHER. TAKE ALL OF IT..''

AS SOON AS THE BOY WAS HEALED AND THE COLOR CAME BACK TO HIS FACE, THE BRIDGE DISINTEGRATED.

I WOKE UP THE NEXT MORNING, NOT KNOWING WHY I WAS THERE. BUT THERE, ON MY PALM, WAS A BARK BOAT, FEELING AS IF IT WOULD TRANSPORT ME BACK TO THAT SUMMER.

### Review

{"id": "W004", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "2", "development": "3", "notes": "Memory toll premise met, but entirely uppercase with repeated possessive/plural errors. Boy is suddenly healed without an established injury; identity and bridge geography confusing. Ending has evocative boat image but unclear causal resolution."}

## W005 — Creative writing / Children's fiction

Round 1; 105 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a story for children aged six to eight about a turtle who wants to learn to dance. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Tilly loves music.

She wants to learn how to dance.

Tilly tries and tries to learn to dance but each time it ends in a PLOP!

Maybe she is to slow to learn how to dance, she sighs.

Then one day, Tilly hears some music and to her surprise, her feet begin to move and her shell begins to sway.

Tilly is dancing!

She practices and practices and soon its time for a dance party!

Tilly shows everyone how to dance and they all have a wonderful time dancing Tilly's special dance.

Tilly couldn't be happier - she is dancing with all of her heart!

## W006 — Creative writing / Comedy

Round 1; 178 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a funny account of someone trying to assemble a bookshelf with the wrong instructions. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Here are the instructions for assembling your bookshelf. What's this? It's got a funny picture on it. A bike? That's odd. I'm sure it's a mistake.

Step 1: *Insert handlebars into side of panel B*. Huh? I suppose I'll just skip this step, since I don't have any handlebars. And the next step is... *Insert front wheel into...* oh, for crying out loud!

What sort of bookshelf needs pedals? And what does it mean by *Place shelf on seat*? There is no seat. And another bag of screws seems to have gone missing...

Anyway, I had to phone up customer services and point out that they'd included the instructions for a kids' bicycle.

As it turns out, putting the bookshelf together was pretty easy once I got the right instructions. And look, it doesn't even have any pedals!

Well, I suppose I'd better put a book on it, and see if it holds up. Oh, look at that. It came with instructions for a bicycle, too.

Maybe I'll unscrew a few bits, and take it for a ride...

### Review

{"id": "W006", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Bookshelf/bicycle instructions give an accessible comic premise. Final discovery repeats the original setup without clear referent; humor and development are slight."}

## W007 — Creative writing / Dialogue

Round 1; 183 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a scene entirely in dialogue between two siblings deciding what to do with their father's old chair. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

`` Are you sure you want me to put this out?''

`` It's ruined, it's useless, it's worthless.''

`` But, you know, it was dad's.''

`` That's even worse, it's uncomfortable and ugly.''

`` Are you sure?''

`` Yes, yes, I'm sure. Really.''

`` Um, ok. What if we fixed it though?''

`` What would be the point?''

`` Because it was his, you know?''

`` I have no money to spare.''

`` It's ok, I have a bit put aside.''

`` You'd do that? For an old useless thing?''

`` Not if it's too much.''

`` But it stinks of smoke.''

`` He stopped smoking a long time ago.''

`` Then why does it stink?''

`` I don't know, maybe the house.''

`` Or maybe it's the chair.''

`` Besides, don't you remember how he used to...''

`` Oh, ok. I'll see what it'll cost.''

`` That's a good girl. Hey, can you make sure they

don't try and steal any popcorn?''

`` Why would they do that?''

`` They're thieves, remember?''

`` Oh, right. Ok, I'll put it up a bit.''

## W008 — Creative writing / Prose poem

Round 1; 170 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a prose poem about a city market after everyone has gone home. Avoid rhyme. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

Past midnight, and the market is closed.

Crates are open, fruit spills from their confines and the smell of fresh vegetables is preserved beneath the awnings. Fruit scales sit empty, waiting for a handful of pears to land in their metal bowls and strike a perfect weight. Flour is spilled along one side of the butchers table and glistens as a white stripe in the otherwise dull darkness. Water drips from a hose and crashes against the plastic of a bucket, slowly filling with the remnants of the day before.

All is quiet as the city slowly retreats back into their homes and the market is abandoned to the sounds of the night. Soon, before the crack of dawn, the market cleaners will arrive to sweep up the rubbish that has been scattered in their wake and life will go on. For now, the market rests among the sounds of the city and the soft breeze that blows through, carrying the scents of fresh food from land far, far away.

### Review

{"id": "W008", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "3", "notes": "Unrhymed atmospheric prose fits brief with concrete sensory details. Minor grammar and familiar phrasing ('life will go on', 'crack of dawn') weaken distinction."}

## W009 — Creative writing / Adventure

Round 1; 290 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an adventure scene about three hikers escaping a gorge as the river rises. Give each hiker a useful role. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

It started with the smallest sign and grew to a torrent in an instant. One minute they were walking along and the next minute a wall of brown water poured around the bend.

`` Everybody up! It's a flood surge!''

Three hapless hikers had taken a wrong turn and were trying to follow a river out of a gorge when the surge hit. Now the path ahead of them was gone and the river was rising fast behind them.

`` There's a ledge up above on the west side'' the strongest among them said. `` We can make it if we use the rope.''

`` Once you're up there'' the navigator said, pulling out a map and compass, `` There's a ravine that should lead out.''

The one who had brought the climbing equipment secured the rope to a boulder and the strongest one started up. Twice she slipped but the rope held her and finally she made it to the ledge. With her helping from above the navigator made it up too.

`` The surge is here'' he said, watching a log smash into the wall on the other side. `` I think I see the ravine. Go left when you come to a fork.'' And he pointed to a narrow opening between two large rock slabs.

The last one was almost to the top when her footing gave way and she started to slip.

`` Grab her!'' the first one yelled.

Together they pulled her to safety.

Without a moment's rest they scrambled up the narrow passage, taking the left at the fork as the ravine split, and minutes later tumbled out onto a pine covered hillside, soaked to the skin but safe from the rising flood below.

### Review

{"id": "W009", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Three hikers each serve a useful function and escape follows an intelligible sequence. Rope anchored to a boulder below is said to arrest the lead climber without explaining rigging; generic unnamed roles and backtick punctuation reduce polish."}

## W010 — Creative writing / Historical fiction

Round 1; 172 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional scene set at a railway station in 1910, from the perspective of a woman arriving alone in a new town. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

I stepped off the train and looked around, taking in my surroundings. The porter walked up to me `` Is somebody meeting you miss?'' I shook my head and he walked off, helping a family who had arrived with me.

I took a deep breath, the steam from the train hazy around me. I was here, in a strange new town. What had I gotten myself into? I asked myself, looking towards the door and out onto the street, the clock showing that it was barely dusk, it being November. I was n't sure how long my money would last for.

A news boy walked past me, shouting out his papers as he went. I took the address out of my glove and read it over, hoping that I would n't have far to go to find it. It was something, to know that I would have somewhere to sleep that night, however temporary.

Lifting my valise I made my way out of the station, out into the world once more. Alone.

### Review

{"id": "W010", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Woman arriving alone and period objects fit brief, though 1910 is not established specifically. Clock 'showing that it was barely dusk' is awkward; split contractions and comma splices distract."}

## W011 — Essays and humanities / Gandhi history

Round 1; 521 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an essay about Gandhi in South Africa, including his arrival, development of satyagraha, and limitations or controversies in his early views. Keep historical claims accurate. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

It's a story that is well-known in its broad outlines. When Mohandas Gandhi arrived in Durban in May 1893, he was a young lawyer who had been hired to help Dada Abdulla, an Indian merchant. He thought he would be staying only for a year, at most. It was only a few days later, when he was thrown out of the first-class compartment of a train to Pietermaritzburg that he became aware of the nature of racial discrimination in South Africa. But even then, he did not imagine that he would spend the rest of his life fighting against racial inequality in South Africa.

It was only when he was about to return to India because the work he had been hired to do was nearly completed that he learned that the Legislative Council of Natal was about to pass a law curbing the right of Indians to vote. He helped mobilise opposition to this and thus decided to stay on. In 1894 he founded the Natal Indian Congress and began the long political struggle to remove discriminatory laws, largely on the grounds that Indians, both the merchants and the indentured labourers, had rights as British subjects.

This may help explain why, during the Anglo-Boer War and the Zulu rebellion in 1906, Gandhi worked to enrol Indians to serve in an ambulance corps and as stretcher-bearers in support of the British Empire.

Gandhi developed the technique of satyagraha – force of truth, or soul-force – in response to the Transvaal government's attempt to pass the Asiatic Registration Act in 1906. He hated the term "passive resistance", he said, because it was weak and seemed like merely a political tactic. At a mass meeting in Johannesburg in September 1906 the participants promised not to submit to the law, which required Indians to register and be fingerprinted.

The battle to defeat this measure and others like it took many forms, including petitions, refusing to register and going to jail, as Gandhi did on several occasions. It also involved labour strikes by Indians, and in 1913 women joined the struggle. The most famous phase of the struggle broke out in 1913 and involved the protesting miners and others over a £3 tax that many former indentured labourers were forced to pay, as well as the government's refusal to recognise the validity of non-Christian marriages. It involved marches, strikes, arrests and severe treatment of the protesters. Eventually the government was forced to negotiate, and made concessions, including removing the tax.

What is not well-known, however, is that Gandhi used racist language against Africans and held many of the racist stereotypes of his time. He worked largely to convince the South African authorities that Indians were different from Africans, and thus should not be treated as they were. This was, of course, within the context of the times, but it does cast a shadow on his legacy. Historians debate whether his views changed, or to what extent. But there is no question that South Africa was the place where Gandhi developed the technique of non-violent resistance to injustice that he later used so effectively in India.

### Review

{"id": "W011", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Covers arrival, Congress, satyagraha, strikes, and racist early views with useful specificity. Opening incorrectly suggests he spent the rest of his life fighting racial inequality in South Africa; he left in 1914, and the conclusion itself refers to later India activity. Main dates checked separately."}

## W012 — Essays and humanities / Printing history

Round 1; 498 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an essay explaining how printing changed the circulation of ideas in early modern Europe. Avoid presenting printing as the sole cause of social change. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

To what extent did the invention of printing transform communication in early modern Europe? In the fifteenth century printing by movable type became increasingly popular. This revolution in the process of making books made an important contribution to many aspects of early modern European society. It was possible to produce books in larger quantities and at a faster rate than with hand-copying. While this new invention did not make books cheap or even allow everyone access to reading, it opened up the possibility for ideas to be distributed and criticised more easily. This essay will consider the ways that the invention of printing transformed communication in early modern Europe.

The invention of printing was important for the spread of the Reformation as it allowed the ideas of reformers to be distributed in the form of pamphlets and translated texts. An example of this was with Martin Luther. However, the invention of printing cannot be solely responsible for the spread of the Reformation as a range of factors influenced this. Luther's ideas were also influenced by the context in which they were presented and the reaction of the audience. This shows how print and oral communication still went hand in hand. Printers and booksellers also played a large part in the spread of the Reformation.

The invention of printing also lead to an increase in the amount of writing that was produced in vernacular languages. With the availability of texts in languages that people could understand, it opened up the possibility for many more people to be able to read. Although not everyone could read, the invention of printing encouraged literacy. The invention of printing also lead to the standardisation of languages as spelling and grammar in the most frequently printed books became standard. However, the increase in vernacular texts did not always lead to unification as there was also an increase in competing texts between regions and confessions.

The invention of printing also had an impact on the development of science. With an increase in the amount of books being printed, it was easier for scientific theories to be distributed. The circulation of books and letters by scientists lead to a larger network of people who could discuss and contribute to new ideas. The invention of printing also made it easier for theories to be criticised. With the availability of illustrations and tables it was possible for a theory to be proven or disproven by comparing it to printed evidence.

The growth of the print trade also influenced censorship. An increase in the amount of books and pamphlets being printed allowed for the spread of a large range of ideas, many of which threatened governments. Many governments responded by introducing licensing laws to control the operation of printing presses, censoring books and punishing authors and printers. However, it was difficult for governments to completely censor the production and distribution of books.

In conclusion, the invention of printing transformed communication in early modern Europe in a range of ways.

### Review

{"id": "W012", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Covers religion, vernaculars, science, and censorship without a single-cause account. Highly repetitive 'invention of printing', repeated 'lead' for 'led', generic conclusion. Scientific theories being proved/disproved by printed evidence is oversimplified; language standardization presented too mechanically."}

## W013 — Essays and humanities / Ethics argument

Round 1; 407 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an essay arguing whether keeping a promise is always the right thing to do. Include a serious counterargument. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Is it always the right thing to do when you make a promise?  A promise is something to ensure someone that you will do something they ask you to do.  Sometimes it is right to break a promise, it all depends on the circumstances on what your promising.  Making a promise doesn't always mean its the right thing to do.  Sometimes you should break a promise.

One example why you should break a promise is if your friend asked you to promise not to tell anyone, and they were going to hurt somebody.  You should tell someone because not telling anyone would put someone in danger that could possibly get hurt.  Also, somebody could be asking you to do something that isn't right in the first place.  You shouldn't make a promise to someone if its not something that is considered to be the "right thing to do".  It is sometimes the right thing to do not to keep a promise, you have to think if its really what your morally supposed to do or not.  You have to consider if its what is right or not to not keep a promise.  Most of the time you should break a promise.

Even though most of the time its the right thing to do not to make a promise, some people think you should always keep a promise.  They believe that once you make a promise, it doesn't matter how the situation is, you made a promise.  Others think that some people will take advantage of other people by saying they will do something, and not doing it, to hurt them.  People should make promises because that means that they should commit to doing it.  Some people think you should always keep a promise.

Although some people think that making a promise and not keeping it is the right thing to do, others think that once you commit to doing something, you should keep it.  You should think before you make a promise, and think about if its really what you should be doing or not.  Most of the time, you should break a promise.

In conclusion, is it always the right thing to do when you make a promise?  Most of the time it is, but not always.  It all depends on what the situation is and if its really what you should be doing.  Most of the time you should keep a promise, but not always.

### Review

{"id": "W013", "adherence": "2", "coherence": "1", "development": "1", "notes": "Promise essay contradicts itself repeatedly: 'most of the time' one should break promises, then keep them. Counterargument is perfunctory. Severe repetition and grammar errors; 407 words for 300 request."}

## W014 — Essays and humanities / Literary analysis

Round 1; 295 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how the creature in Mary Shelley's Frankenstein complicates the idea of who the real monster is. Do not invent quotations. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

In the novel, Frankenstein, the creature is judged by many people because of his apprearance, those people include the family that he watched and grew an affection for. Even though they tried to communicate with the family, they rejected and resorted to violence to express their feelings towards him. Thus, teaching him that everyone would be like that, everyone would be his enemy. Yet, one could say that the creature was not always a monster, he wanted to communicate, he wanted to be a part of society, to learn and to be kind.

On the other hand, Victor Frankenstein, the creator of the creature, could be said to be the monster. The reason why is because instead of helping the creature or at least try to take care of him, he rejected him and left him all because of his apprearnce. Victor is the reason why the creature turned out to be a monser in the first place. Why did Victor destroy the second creature when the first one was begging him to not do it, that he only wanted a friend, someone to be with?

To say that Victor was the monster and not the creature is not entirely true, the creature did commit murder, and not only that, he murdered innocent people just for the sake of hurting and punishing Victor for what he did. ## W016 — Essays and humanities / Language essay

Round 1; 260 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a short essay on what can be lost and gained when a poem is translated. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

My view on translation is based on the knowledge that there is a certain degree of loss which occurs between the original version of a poem and its translation. This is especially apparent when translating a poem from one language to another, as there may be phrases and idioms that do not translate well, and the musicality of the poem may differ when translated into a different language. Ambiguity is another potential problem when translating, as a word may have a double meaning in one language, but not in another.

However, there is also something to be gained when translating a poem. Whether it is a poem that has been translated from one language to another, or a poem that has been rewritten in a different style, there are always new possibilities that can emerge when a poem is translated. A new side of a poem can be explored when it is translated, which makes the poem new again, and allows a poet to breathe new life into an old poem.

Ultimately, I believe that when a poem is translated, something is both lost and gained. It is impossible for a translated version of a poem to encompass all of the features of the original poem, and it inevitably becomes a translation of the original poem, rather than the original poem itself. However, this does not mean that it is not a good poem. Rather, a translated poem is a new version of the original poem, and an interpretation by another poet of what they understand to be its meaning.

## W021 — Science and technology / Climate explainer

Round 1; 470 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain the greenhouse effect, distinguishing it from ozone depletion and from short-term weather variation. Write for a curious adult. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

The greenhouse effect is a process by which thermal radiation from planetary surface is absorbed by atmospheric greenhouse gases, and is re-radiated in all directions. Since part of this re-radiation is back towards the surface, and so it causes the atmosphere and the surface to be warmer than they would be in the absence of the gases.

The greenhouse effect is a good thing -- without it, the Earth would be frozen and life as we know it would not exist. The gases in our atmosphere that are responsible for this effect are referred to as greenhouse gases. The main greenhouse gases are water vapor, carbon dioxide, methane and nitrous oxide. Human activities, primarily the burning of coal, oil and gas, are increasing the concentrations of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere, particularly carbon dioxide and methane. As a result, the natural greenhouse effect is being enhanced, more heat is being temporarily stored in the atmosphere and the Earth is warming up until it can radiate as much energy as it receives from the Sun.

The oceans absorb much of this energy and are delaying the warming of the atmosphere. As the atmosphere and oceans warm up, they will influence many other aspects of our climate, such as rainfall. Other impacts of global warming include the loss of ice, rising sea levels and changes in extreme events like droughts and floods. Because water vapor is a greenhouse gas, the increase in water vapor that results from global warming due to carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases causes further global warming. However, water vapor acts primarily as a feedback to global warming caused by other greenhouse gases.

The greenhouse effect should not be confused with depletion of the stratospheric ozone layer. The ozone layer is located in the stratosphere, many kilometres above the Earth's surface, and absorbs ultraviolet radiation from the Sun. Certain man-made chemicals, when they reach the stratosphere, destroy ozone. The resulting ozone depletion allows more ultraviolet radiation to reach the Earth's surface. This may cause increased skin cancer in humans, as well as other harmful effects. Although ozone is itself a greenhouse gas, the greenhouse effect and ozone depletion are quite different phenomena. International agreements to control the emissions of ozone-depleting substances are leading to a recovery of the ozone layer.

Confusion also often exists between variations in weather and climate change. Weather varies from minute to minute, day to day and week to week. Climate change, on the other hand, is detected in changes in the statistics of weather over longer periods, typically 30 years or more. Variations in winds, ocean currents and other factors may cause the weather to be cooler or warmer than usual in a particular place at a particular time. However, the greenhouse effect acts to change the overall energy balance of the atmosphere.

### Review

{"id": "W021", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Clear greenhouse/ozone/weather distinctions, water-vapor feedback and ocean heat uptake. Opening sentence fragment and absolute 'Earth would be frozen' phrasing need polish; key mechanisms checked against NASA."}

## W022 — Science and technology / Machine learning

Round 1; 569 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how a machine-learning model can perform well on training data and poorly on new data. Use a concrete example and discuss overfitting and data leakage. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Why would a machine learning model work on training data but not on new examples? The goal of most machine learning models is to learn something about the data that generalizes to new examples of data. Sometimes this doesn't happen and the model only works on the training data.

Imagine a simple example where we're training a model to predict whether a person will pay back a loan. We train the model using data from past loan applicants and it seems to work great: when we test the model on the training data, it predicts whether a person will pay back their loan 99% of the time. However, when we actually start using the model on new applicants the next year, the model doesn't work very well at all.

Overfitting One thing that could have gone wrong here is overfitting. This happens when our model is flexible enough that instead of just fitting the trend in the data (people with high debt are more likely to default), it also fits the noise in the data (people with income 43211 and loan amount 12342 always default) or even just memorizing the data (record number 2312 defaulted on their loan). Then, when we try and apply this model to new data, it doesn't work because the noise or the specific instances from the training data don't show up in the new data. It's like studying for a test by memorizing the answers to the practice questions instead of learning the actual material: if you get the same questions again, you can just use the answers you memorized, but if the questions are slightly different, you're out of luck.

We can detect overfitting by holding out a validation set or test set and seeing how well the model does on that data. If it does significantly worse on the test data, it's probably overfitting on the training data. There are many ways to prevent overfitting , but a good basic practice is to hold out a test set that you don't look at until the very end and to avoid using overly complex models or to use regularization to reduce the effective complexity of the model.

Data Leakage Another thing that could have gone wrong is that we allowed data from outside of the model to leak in. For instance, one of the fields in our data might be "Was this account sent to the collections agency?" Since this only happens after people have already not paid back their loans, this is a great predictor of whether a person will pay back a loan. However, when we're actually making a decision on a new loan, this information won't be available.

Leakage can also occur if you're not careful about separating your training and test data, like if the same person has two loan applications and one of them ends up in your training data and the other in your test data or if you include your test data when deciding which features to include in your model.

Overfitting and data leakage are two very different problems, but either one can lead to a model that looks like it's working well on your data but doesn't actually work well in practice. Being careful about how you separate your data into training and test sets, especially if you have information about when the predictions will be made, can help prevent both of these problems.

### Review

{"id": "W022", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Concrete lending example clearly separates memorization, future-outcome leakage, and split contamination; practical holdout advice. Plain inline headings and a few awkward phrases; key leakage advice checked against scikit-learn."}

## W023 — Science and technology / Biology

Round 1; 322 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain natural selection using antibiotic resistance as an example. Distinguish selection from organisms changing because they need to. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Variations in antibiotic resistance exist in bacterial populations before the use of antibiotics. These variations occur through random mutations in the bacteria or through the uptake of genes from other bacteria. These variations do not occur in order to meet a need of the bacteria.

When an antibiotic is used, it acts as a filter, increasing the likelihood that bacteria without the resistance will die, while allowing bacteria with the resistance to survive. After the non-resistant bacteria die, the resistant bacteria reproduce, passing on the genes for resistance. Also, the resistant bacteria can share their resistance genes with other bacteria. Over several generations, a larger and larger proportion of the bacteria will be resistant to the antibiotic, and thus the bacterial population will have evolved.

This process is an example of natural selection. Antibiotics do not cause bacteria to develop the specific variation that confers resistance. However, when bacteria reproduce, random mutations can occur, just by chance giving the bacteria resistance to the antibiotic. The presence of the antibiotic increases the likelihood that bacteria with the variation for resistance will survive.

It is important to realize that antibiotic resistance is not a “silver bullet” for the bacteria. The bacteria usually remain susceptible to other antibiotics, and the resistance sometimes is a detriment to the bacteria when antibiotics are not present. The best way to prevent bacteria from evolving resistance to antibiotics is to only use antibiotics when appropriate and to always take them as prescribed.

An important point to understand about natural selection is that it does not act on individuals to make them change based on a need that they have. Rather, natural selection acts on populations. Natural selection increases the proportion of individuals in a population that have traits that allow them to better reproduce in a particular environment. Natural selection does not make individuals “better” in some overall sense, it only makes them better at reproducing in particular situations.

### Review

{"id": "W023", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Explains preexisting variation, differential survival and horizontal transfer. Misleading final claim that selection acts on populations rather than individuals: selection acts through individual differences, while populations evolve. Adds unrequested antibiotic advice; targeted fact check recorded."}

## W024 — Science and technology / Astronomy

Round 1; 326 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why the Moon has phases and why they are not usually caused by Earth's shadow. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

The Moon doesn't produce any of its own light that we can see. The Sun shines on the Moon, illuminating half of it at all times, while the other half is in darkness. As the Moon moves in its orbit around the Earth, we see different amounts of the illuminated side of the Moon. These are called the phases of the Moon.

When the Moon is between the Earth and the Sun, we can't see much of it, because the side that the Sun is illuminating is mostly facing away from us. This is called the "new Moon". As the Moon moves in its orbit, we see more and more of it - first a crescent, then at "first quarter", we see half of the side that's facing us illuminated. As the Moon continues to orbit, we see more and more of it, until at "full Moon", the entire side of the Moon facing us is illuminated. As the Moon continues its orbit, we see less and less of it each night, until it's back at the "new Moon" phase.

It may seem like the shadow of the Earth is responsible for the phases of the Moon - it's not, though. The only time the Earth's shadow falls on the Moon is during a lunar eclipse. A lunar eclipse occurs when the Sun, Earth, and Moon line up almost exactly, with the Earth between the Sun and the Moon. Most months, the Moon passes either above or below the Earth's shadow, because the Moon's orbit is tilted by about 5 degrees compared to the Earth's orbit around the Sun.

Because of that tilt, the Moon is not in the Earth's shadow even when it's at the "full Moon" phase. That's why lunar eclipses don't happen every month, but the phases of the Moon do. The full Moon is full not because it's not in the Earth's shadow, but because of where it is in its orbit.

### Review

{"id": "W024", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Explains illuminated hemisphere, viewing geometry, eclipse alignment and orbital tilt accurately at introductory level. A few absolute formulations are qualified by its own eclipse discussion; NASA checked."}

## W025 — Science and technology / Physics

Round 1; 220 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why a metal spoon feels colder than a wooden spoon in the same room. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

If you had a metal spoon and a wooden spoon in a room and they both had come to room temperature they would both be exactly the same temperature. However, if you touched them the metal spoon would appear to be colder than the wood spoon. This is because of the rate of heat transfer in the materials.

Your skin is warmer than the spoons and heat always flows from warm to cold. Since metal is a good heat conductor, it conducts the heat away from your body very quickly. This causes a rapid temperature change on your skin surface and our nerves respond to that rapid change so you perceive that the metal spoon is very cold.

Wood on the other hand is a poor heat conductor (it is called an insulator). So, when you put your hand on the wood spoon the heat transfer from your body to the spoon is slow. The heat transfer from your body decreases as the wood warms up and thus your hand does not cool as quickly and the spoon feels warmer to your hand.

This is why metal in a cool room feels cold to your skin. When the metal is warm, it will feel hotter to your body than wood since it transfers heat to your body faster than wood.

## W027 — Science and technology / Computing

Round 1; 248 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain what a computer cache does using an everyday analogy, then mention one way the analogy is imperfect. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

How does a cache work? Imagine a kitchen. Whenever you want to make something to eat, you need a bunch of ingredients. If you store all of your ingredients in cupboards or even go to the grocery store for every single thing you need, it'll take a while to get everything you need and start cooking. But if you have the things you need most often out on the counter, it'll be a lot quicker to get to them.

A cache works in a similar way to having things out on the counter. If your computer needs a piece of information often, it will store a copy of it in a cache somewhere. That way it can get to the information much quicker than if it had to go to where it's actually stored. If your computer wants a piece of information that it doesn't have stored in the cache (a "cache miss"), it will get it from where it's actually stored and often store a copy in the cache so it will be quicker to get to next time. There are lots of different kinds of cache -- your web browser probably has one for images, and your processor probably has one for data, for example.

This is not a perfect analogy, because a cache works a little bit differently. For one thing, a cache works automatically. For another, a cache only has so much space in it, so it has to replace things in it sometimes.

## W029 — Science and technology / Research design

Round 1; 359 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Describe a fair experiment to test whether moving recycling signs closer to bins improves sorting. Include a limitation. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Start with several similar stations. Monitor them for a period of time (a week) and note the results. Next, group them into pairs of similarly performing stations. Randomly pick one of the stations in each pair and move the signs on that station to be right next to the bin. Leave the signs at the other station in the pair where they are now. The wording, size, and design of the signs should be the same at both recycling stations. The bins themselves should also be the same, as well as the schedule for collecting the materials in the bins.

For the next several weeks, monitor the results at both sets of stations, making sure to compare “apples to apples” (weekdays to weekdays and weekends to weekends). You should monitor the bins the same way you would normally (perhaps by how well they are sorted or by how much garbage is mixed in with the recyclables). It might be beneficial to have the person who monitors the bins not know which bins had the signs moved, so that his or her opinion is unbiased. Compare the change in results from before the signs were moved at the stations where the signs were moved and the change in results at the stations where the signs were not moved. This will help cancel out any differences between the two sets of stations and any outside influences, such as a larger campaign to increase recycling.

Before starting the experiment, you should also decide how you will measure success. For example, you could calculate what percentage of items in each bin should be in that bin. You should also take enough samples at each station to get an accurate result. Finally, you should take into account any outside influences, such as overflow of the bins, that could affect your results. If you do not include a certain time period in your results for one set of stations, you should not include that time period for the other set of stations.

Keep in mind that the results of this experiment may not be widely applicable, and that the two groups may influence each other.

### Review

## W030 — Science and technology / Scientific uncertainty

Round 1; 199 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain the difference between an absence of evidence and evidence of absence with a practical example. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Absence of evidence is the lack of finding evidence that proves that something is true. Lack of finding evidence that something is true does not mean that something is false.

Evidence of absence is finding evidence that something is not present. This can be more or less convincing based on how thorough the search is and how likely the evidence would be found if it was there.

For example if you think that you have a leak in your pipes you could look for a puddle of water on the floor. Not finding a puddle of water would be absence of evidence to prove there is a leak. It would not be strong evidence of absence since it is quite possible for a leak to not have a puddle of water on the floor. However, if you looked at the pipes themselves, inspected the walls for water damage, and measured the water usage with the water turned off inside the house with a reliable meter and found no water usage, then that would be strong evidence of absence that there is not a leak.

It is important to know the difference between absence of evidence and evidence of absence.

## W031 — Business and workplace / Strategy memo

### Output

To: Shop owners  
Subject: Testing evening appointments before committing to a second location

Test your assumptions and try both approaches for a time period with limited commitments. Expanding to a second location has many costs and is hard to reverse. Extending your hours in your existing location is relatively easy to reverse and should be tried first. However, extending your hours will not tell you much about whether you could be successful at a second location.

Conduct a pilot trial for 8 weeks or so where you offer evening appointments two evenings per week at your current location. Make the trial as realistic as possible, offering the same hours and services you would if it was permanent but limit the number of appointments so you are not creating too much havoc with your current operation. Use your existing staff if possible but be aware of the extra cost of overtime. In addition, try to test the waters at the potential new location for two or three days offering limited service. Try to work with a local business or offer drop-off and pick-up only to avoid having to rent a temporary work space.

Measure everything you can during the trial period. Measure the number of appointments asked for, booked, done, canceled and missed. Measure where your customers want the service done and if they are new customers, how did they hear about you and where do they live. Make sure you measure the additional revenue you are generating and track if you are just moving day-time customers to the evening or if you are getting new customers that would not have come in during the day. Measure the cost of doing the additional jobs including parts, labor, overtime, travel and any additional facility cost.

The important thing to measure is whether you are generating an incremental contribution by offering the evenings and/or the second location. An accounting for all of the additional labor, parts, facility and other costs that are generated by the trial should be compared to the revenue generated by each of the approaches. Also consider if your regular day-time business is being impacted by either of the trials. Are your normal jobs being delayed? Are you taking longer to do jobs than normal or doing more re-work? Also track how many of the trial jobs result in another job, the number of customers that do not show up for their appointments and any other feedback you can get from your customers.

Decide ahead of time what it will take for you to decide to proceed with either approach. Evening hours may need to show a positive contribution and attract new customers or customers you would not have gotten during the day. The second location may need to show that you can generate enough contribution to pay for the location each month. Make sure you have a margin of safety and not just break-even.

You may want to review the results each week but make your decision at the end of the entire trial period. If one approach works and the other does not then consider extending your hours or gathering more information to help you decide about getting a lease for the second location.

### Review

{"id": "W031", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Reversible evening trial plus low-commitment second-location test; measures displacement, marginal costs and contribution. Useful distinction that evening demand does not prove location demand. 'Measure everything' could be more focused."}

## W032 — Business and workplace / Project proposal

Round 1; 622 words requested 500; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Propose a six-week pilot for a shared equipment booking system at a makerspace. Cover scope, responsibilities, success measures, and risks. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Scope. This will be a six week pilot. It should start with 3-5 pieces of highly used equipment that already have safety procedures in place, trained members, and a way for members to report safety issues (like your laser cutter, 3D printer, CNC Router, etc). You don't want to include all your equipment; just leave the rest the way it is for the moment.

How it'll work. All members will use a shared calendaring tool (either web or desktop) to make equipment reservations. The calendar will show: the name of the member making the reservation the equipment being reserved the time of the reservation an estimate of setup and tear-down time There will be set reservation lengths, limits on how far in advance a member can reserve equipment, and limits on how long a member can be late before the reservation is canceled. Members won't be able to reserve equipment until they've completed the training for that piece of equipment. All reservations will include time at the end for cleanup, and users will be required to leave the equipment in a usable state for the next reservation. Members will still be able to use equipment on a walk-up basis as long as it doesn't interfere with an existing reservation. Rules will be posted in a visible location next to each piece of equipment, as well as wherever the online booking tool is.

Pilot timeline. Week One will be the selection of which equipment will be included in the pilot, the selection and setup of the booking tool, the drafting and posting of the booking rules, and a briefing to the membership on how the system will work. Weeks Two through Five will be the actual running of the pilot. The Pilot Coordinator will review how things are going at the end of each week. Week Six will be the review of the pilot with the membership and the recommendation on whether or not to continue the pilot. Roles and responsibilities. The Pilot Coordinator will be responsible for setting up the pilot, and reporting on how it's going at the end of each week. Equipment Stewards will be responsible for ensuring that members are properly trained on their equipment, that the equipment is ready to use, and that any safety or maintenance issues are addressed. Members will be responsible for using the reservation system properly, showing up for their reservations, following all safety procedures, and reporting any safety or maintenance issues. The Pilot Coordinator should designate someone who can update the booking tool in their absence.

Pilot metrics. Conflict reduction (use your best guess or do a survey in Week One if you don't have historical data) % of reservations actually used # of conflicts with the reservation system # of no-shows Average wait time for each piece of equipment # of safety or maintenance incidents Satisfaction (survey members and equipment stewards on how easy the system is to use, how fair it is, and how much time it's taking to administer). A successful pilot will show reductions in conflicts over equipment use, reductions in wait times, good feedback from the membership, no increase in safety or maintenance incidents, and not take an excessive amount of time to administer.

Things to watch out for: Making sure people don't over-reserve, or reserve and not use the equipment Make sure your booking tool is easy for everyone to use Making sure everyone has equal access to the equipment Make sure equipment failures are reported and dealt with Make sure people aren't using equipment without training or supervision Shut the pilot down if it becomes a safety issue Make a decision at the end of the sixth week on whether or not to continue the pilot.

### Review

{"id": "W032", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Six-week schedule, roles, access rules, metrics and risks covered. Dense collapsed lists substantially reduce readability; 622 versus 500 words. Success thresholds remain qualitative."}

## W033 — Business and workplace / Incident report

Round 1; 379 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional incident report about duplicate booking emails caused by a retry bug. No bookings or charges were duplicated. Include timeline, cause, and prevention. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Some customers received multiple copies of booking confirmation emails on May 14. A total of 184 bookings made between 09:12 and 10:03 UTC received duplicate emails. There was only one booking created in our system for each reservation and all payments were processed correctly.

At 09:12 UTC we deployed a routine update to the service that sends booking confirmation emails. After the deployment the service started taking longer than expected to respond. At 09:19 the service started retrying some of the emails it sent. While the emails were successfully sent to our email delivery provider, it took longer than expected for the provider to acknowledge receipt. This caused some emails to be resent. At 10:03 our monitoring system detected an increase in the number of confirmation emails being sent. At 10:11 an on-call engineer paused automatic retries in the service. The service returned to normal operation at 10:24. At 10:28 we received our first report of the issue from a customer contacting Customer Support. At 10:41 we posted a notice about the issue on our status page.

The root cause of the incident was a bug in the retry logic of our email service. Because the service didn’t have a good way of suppressing duplicate messages, it sent both the original and the retried messages. Our booking and payment systems are separate from our email systems and were not affected by this issue.

We have reviewed our booking and payment systems and confirmed that bookings and payments were not duplicated. We have also reached out to customers who reported this issue to Customer Support to reassure them that their bookings were not affected and provided instructions on how other customers can confirm that their bookings are ok.

We have updated our email service to prevent duplicate messages from being sent and changed the retry logic to be safer. We have also added monitoring to alert engineers when the volume of emails sent is higher than expected. Before updating the service we tested it to make sure that it behaves correctly in the case of delayed emails. We will continue to monitor the service for the next 30 days to ensure that the problem doesn’t recur and to make sure that we can safely make further changes to the service.

### Review

{"id": "W033", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Detailed fictional timeline, retry explanation and reassurance that bookings/charges unaffected. Prevention is somewhat vague ('safer'); delayed-email testing is less precise than delayed acknowledgments. 379 versus 300 words."}

## W039 — Business and workplace / Procurement analysis

Round 1; 330 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Compare buying a cheaper scanner with testing a more expensive one. Discuss total cost and real-world performance without inventing vendor specifications. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Are cheaper scanners better value? If you only need to scan on a relatively infrequent basis, and the material to be scanned is not challenging, and you don’t need either great speed or high quality, then yes, it may be a good idea to save money on the initial purchase of a scanning device. However, you should take into account not only the purchase price, but also the costs of setting up, running, maintaining, and any consumable items, as well as the staff time required to run and maintain the device, and to rectify any issues that might arise with the scanning.

It may well be worth investing the time and perhaps money to test out a more expensive device to see if in fact it will offer you better value. Try it with actual material that you need to scan, in volumes that you will be scanning, with the software that you plan to use, and see how it works out for you. Yes, speed is an issue, but are you going to need to re-scan, will the output be what you require, will you be able to run it in batches, or will it require attention throughout the scan, etc. If you are scanning something that is not standard, make sure that you include some of the more challenging material in your test.

Work out the total cost of purchase and running both devices, over the period that you expect to be using the device, and factor in the differences in labour and rework, etc., that you have experienced while testing the devices.

If the cheaper device will give you what you require, then go ahead and purchase it; but if spending a bit more will make your staff’s lives easier, and ultimately save you money, then maybe you should go with the more expensive device. In the end, what you really want is a device that works well for you, and is the least cost to you.

### Review

{"id": "W039", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Sound practical comparison of acquisition, labor, maintenance, difficult documents and testing; no fabricated specifications. Repetitive conditional phrasing but useful."}

## W041 — Journalism and commentary / Reported feature

Round 1; 539 words requested 500; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write a clearly fictional feature about a monthly neighborhood repair cafe. Use invented named characters and show both its benefits and limits. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Imagine having a broken toaster. You want to have fresh toast in the morning, but yours doesn ' t work. What do you do? Throw it away and buy a new one? No. You take it down to the Marigold Row repair caf. Every second Saturday of the month people bring their broken goods to the basement of the old library on Marigold Row and wait for someone to fix it.

The repair caf was started by Inez Vale, a retired electrician, who didn ' t want to see all of the good things that people were throwing out. She organized a small group of people to help fix things. She convinced the local library to let her use the basement of the building, and now with the help of Tomas Reed, a local bicycle repair man, Nella Park, a clothes mender, and Dev Shah, a young tech wizard, the Marigold Row Repair Caf is up and running. Although it's called a caf, all that is served is instant coffee and some oranges.

As you walk in the door of the basement you are greeted by the sounds of chatter and the smell of oranges. The main draw of the Marigold Row repair caf is that you can sit down next to one of the workers and learn how to fix whatever it is you brought in. If you brought in a lamp, you can learn how to fix it, and then if it ever happens again you will know what to do. There are even some kids that come down and watch the bicycle repair man at work. Some of the parents say that if it weren ' t for the repair caf they would have had to buy new bicycle wheels for their kids.

With the amount of waste in the world today, having a place like the Marigold Row repair caf helps a lot. Not only are less things being wasted, but also people in the community are coming together and sharing what they have made. It's a wonderful place to bring in something that you have that is broken and maybe learn how to fix it.

It's important to note, though, that not everything can be repaired. Sometimes people bring in phones, but without the proper equipment the workers cannot fix them. Sometimes people bring in broken microwaves, but if there is an electrical problem in them, it can ' t be fixed. Also, sometimes the parts that are needed to fix something cost more than just buying a new one, or the item can ' t be opened up without breaking it even more. And, finally, if you don ' t get there soon enough you might have to wait a while, or even not get your item fixed at all, due to a long line of people wanting their items fixed.

This may be frustrating to some people, especially if they were really hoping to have something fixed, but sometimes it just can ' t be done.

Despite these few problems, the Marigold Row repair caf is a wonderful place to bring your broken goods. While it may not save the world from the large amounts of goods being thrown out, it helps.

### Review

Fiction not clearly labeled beyond opening 'Imagine'; repetitive praise, damaged 'caf' spelling throughout and spaced apostrophes. Little reporting-style scene development."}

## W042 — Journalism and commentary / Investigative explainer

Round 1; 453 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how changing budget categories can make public spending appear to double. Use a clearly hypothetical numerical example and distinguish recurring from one-time costs. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

In order to convince people that we're spending more money on something without actually getting approval for or spending more money, all you have to do is change the category. The simple act of changing the category of a budget item can give the impression that more money has been spent. There are a couple of ways to do this.

First, let's imagine a city which has spent $100m on roads last year. That's the entire budget. Let's imagine that $80m is made up of regular costs (staff, annual maintenance contracts, etc) and $20m is made up of one-off expenses (a specific bridge which needs to be checked over and repaired). Now, until now, the city has presented this in a single category: let's call it 'Transportation'.

Now, this year, the city decides it wants to be seen as supporting the fight against climate change. So, it decides to create a new category in the budget called 'Climate Resilience'. It then decides that the $80m in maintenance costs and the $20m in specific projects are all in support of climate resilience, so puts them in that category. It then publishes a summary of the budget which shows $100m for 'Transportation' and another $100m for 'Climate Resilience'. You, the citizen, might then add these two figures together and think that the city is spending twice as much money as it was before.

However, if you look more closely, you'll see that the two categories overlap. The city isn't spending $100m on Transportation and a further $100m on Climate Resilience. Instead, it's spending $80m on recurring costs and $20m on specific projects. It could avoid the misleading impression by noting that the categories overlap, providing a crosswalk between the old and new budget, or simply providing a figure for total spend.

The second part of this is noting recurring vs one-off costs. In my example above, the city could claim that it's now spending $100m on Climate Resilience each and every year. However, only $80m of that is recurring costs - the other $20m was for a specific bridge project which may not be needed next year. Alternatively, it could claim that it's spending $100m on Climate Resilience as a one-off, this year, and then next year it'll be back to spending $0. However, $80m of that is recurring costs for staff and annual contracts.

Of course, if the city wants to genuinely spend more money, then it's free to do so. It can slap on a further $10m for new climate resilience barriers, for example, and then the total spend will be $110m. But, in order to compare new spend against old spend, you need to make sure that you're comparing like with like.

### Review

{"id": "W042", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Hypothetical numbers correctly distinguish overlapping classifications and recurring/project spend. Demonstrates double counting rather than a clean category-transfer comparison; opening assumes deceptive intent and becomes instructive rather than neutral explanation."}

The Maplewood Municipal Pool will finally be open for swimmers again this Saturday after being closed for six weeks while a number of repairs were made.

In late May, it was announced the pool would be closed until needed repairs were made to the filtration system and deck. The city spent about $185,000, taken from municipal maintenance funds, to fix leaks in the underground return line and replace damaged sections of the deck.

“The work took a little longer than we anticipated, but we wanted to make sure everything was done right and the pool was safe for our residents,” Parks Director Lena Ortiz said Thursday.

The pool will operate on a limited schedule at first due to a shortage of lifeguards. The pool will be open from noon until 5 p.m. from Tuesdays through Sundays until the end of the month, and will be closed Mondays for maintenance work. Ortiz said that if more certified lifeguards come forward, the city may be able to add evening hours to the schedule.

To entice more lifeguards, the city has offered a $500 hiring bonus to any lifeguard that gets certified and stays on until the end of the season. People interested in becoming lifeguards must be at least 16 years old. More information can be found on the city’s website.

For residents who bought season passes, Ortiz assured them that their passes will be extended for the number of days the pool was closed. She said the passes will be updated automatically, and pass-holders do not need to take any action. The city will also extend the expiration date of the multi-visit swim cards for six weeks.

With temperatures expected to reach the upper 80s this weekend, swimmers will be able to take advantage of the cool water, which will be tested prior to opening each day, and enjoy the pool, which has a capacity of 180 swimmers at a time.

## W044 — Journalism and commentary / Profile

Round 1; 298 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional profile of a watch repairer who values explaining uncertainty to customers. Include specific scenes without making the subject flawless. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Mara Venn mends watches, and like most all watch-menders, she has a narrow shop crammed to the back of a unit, on Alder Street.

`` I ca n't be certain, but from what I see, i suspect it might be a faulty contact, but it's possible it's a bad battery. There is also a crack here, very small, see, like a hair? I would suggest that we do n't replace any parts until I've carried out some tests, otherwise we might be wasting your money.'' Yes, that's what she tells them, and why should it be any different? Why should she promise anything else? But sometimes, she sees the disappointed look in her customers' eyes, and wishes she could say something else. She does n't, though. Why would she lie? And why does she always forget to take their phone number with her, so that she has to ask them to come back the next morning?

And sometimes, she does n't quite say what she ought to, and comes across far too bluntly. It was n't her intention to make the girl cry with her lecture about the likelihood that the water damage might have progressed... And yet, it happened. It was n't her intention, but there she was, sitting next to her on a small stool, stroking her hair in silence while the rain battered the window of her shop. What she ought to have said? She did n't know. Maybe she ought to have said nothing at all.

Mara Venn mends watches, and sometimes she takes a bit too long. She always informs her customers, of course.

And she ca n't stand it when people call her meticulous. That's n't what she is. But she tries to be honest, and perhaps that's the next best thing.

## W045 — Journalism and commentary / Editorial

Round 1; 273 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an editorial arguing that transit agencies should explain route changes in plain language and evaluate the changes afterward. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Transit routes change all the time for a variety of reasons. Sometimes it is budgetary; sometimes it is due to a change in travel patterns; and sometimes it is due to new development in an area. Regardless of the reason, transit agencies should be doing a better job of explaining the changes in a meaningful and easily understandable way to the public. It can be very confusing for the everyday person to read a transit agency’s explanation of a route change and to look at the often complicated before and after route maps. Transit agencies need to be better about explaining in plain English why a change is occurring and give good examples of how the change will affect riders and how they can adapt to the change.

In addition, it is extremely important to explain the specifics of how the change will affect riders i.e. How will they be able to get to where they need to go now? When is the new schedule going into effect? etc. This notice should be translated into other languages as needed and distributed at places where riders will see it before the new schedule goes into effect.

Finally, transit agencies should evaluate route changes after they have been put into effect and report back to the public about the results of those changes.

It is impossible to please everybody when it comes to route changes but at the very least the public should be informed in plain English as to why a change is occurring and transit agencies should report back to the public about the success of the change or changes that were made.

### Review

## W046 — Journalism and commentary / Arts review

Round 1; 187 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Review an imaginary exhibition of photographs taken in homes just before their occupants move. Include one criticism as well as praise. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Last Light There’s always something left behind when you move out of a space: the faint outline on a wall where a picture used to hang, an old charging cable, or even a favourite mug. This exhibition explores the stages of moving out; the sometimes slow and subtle process of leaving something behind.

The best thing about Last Light is that the photos are not overly sentimental; they don’t wallow in sadness. Instead the photographer has found a way to show the beauty in a space as the light shifts and changes, and has focused on small details – like scuffs on a floorboard, or how the light hits a curtain. The sequence of photos showing the gradual emptying of a hallway works particularly well.

On the other hand, the photos are all taken in a very similar way: softly and centred. This doesn’t quite reflect the excitement and chaos of actually moving, and I think it would have been interesting to see a few photos that are less well composed.

Overall though, Last Light is a beautiful and poignant representation of moving out of a space.

### Review

{"id": "W046", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Specific visual details, sequencing praise and composition criticism support balanced imaginary review. Opening title runs into paragraph; otherwise usable."}
