## W049 — Journalism and commentary / Interview Q&A

Round 1; 310 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional Q&A with a community librarian about the value of libraries beyond borrowing books. Include one challenging question. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Stuff they want you to think: What do you do? I help people borrow books.

What we actually do: I help people use computers, fill out forms, find health information, practice for interviews, go to story times, learn new languages, join craft groups, study, etc.

Why we do it: Because not everyone has access to the internet or a printer or a quiet place to work. Because there are lots of people out there who can help people with their problems, and if we aren't the right people, we can at least point people in the right direction.

Stuff they want you to think: Aren't libraries useless now that all information is available online?

The reality: Librarians are still needed to help people sort through all the information available online and find the good stuff. Also, not everyone has access to a computer or the internet.

Stuff they want you to think: Why should we pay for libraries when so many people don't use them?

The reality: You know all those people you're paying to have roads and fire departments and police departments and schools for? Yeah, most of them don't use those things either. But it's still a good idea to have those things available to people who do, and the same goes for libraries.

Stuff we wish they'd think: If you don't know what you're looking for, that's okay. Come in anyway. We have a ton of stuff here that you might want, and even if you don't, that's okay too. You can ask us questions, or participate in one of our programs, or use the restroom, or just sit here for a while. This is a public space, and it belongs to you and everyone in our community, whether or not you borrow books from us. But we really hope that you do borrow books from us.

## W050 — Journalism and commentary / Opinion column

Round 1; 244 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a nuanced column about the pressure to turn every hobby into an income source. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

There’s something very appealing about the idea of turning a hobby into a money-making venture. Whether it’s to supplement your income or a way to live the dream, it seems wonderful that you can make money doing what you love. Make some beautiful pottery and sell it, love playing guitar? Teach some lessons.

But in a culture that’s obsessed with making money from everything we do, it seems like there’s pressure on us to turn every hobby we have into a business. If you’re not making money from your hobby – you should be! In fact, there are plenty of online platforms that make it easy for you to do so. In a culture like this, it’s hard to just do something for fun and not feel like you should be making money from it.

And it’s not like you can make money from a hobby and then go back to doing it purely for fun – once you’ve brought money into the equation, it’s no longer just a hobby. Sure, you can still do it, but you’ve made an exchange – you’re doing something for money, and that changes things.

So before you turn your hobby into a business, perhaps you should ask yourself what making money from it might mean for you. And, equally, what making money from it might mean for your hobby. Because some hobbies are meant to be unproductive. We shouldn’t need to produce anything from everything we do.

### Review

## W051 — Personal communication / Travel narrative

Round 1; 493 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a first-person travel narrative in which a missed express bus leads to a memorable local journey, but the destination is obscured by clouds. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

On my way up to Alishan I missed my express bus connection by a matter of seconds. As I ran up and out onto the plaza of the Chiayi bus station, I could just make out the tail lights of the express turning the corner, taunting me as it receded into the distance. I went back inside apologetically and explained my predicament to the ticket clerk. “ Not a problem,” she said with a sympathetic smile, “there’s a local bus that will get you there, it will just take longer.”

I bought a paper ticket and sat and waited for my local bus, which belched a cloud of blue smoke as it turned into the station. It was an older bus, with slightly cloudy windows and a plastic peach hanging from the mirror, but I was still surprised that it was full. There were people sitting in every seat on the way up the mountain, each one of them had a basket, bag, or bundle of greens with them as they made their way along.

The bus stopped constantly for people to get on and off, and with every stop a village whizzed by, in and out of my peripheral vision. Soon the passengers noticed me as the only foreigner on the bus and started to chat with me, one kind elderly lady in a purple sun hat even offered me a piece of guava from her bag and tried to correct my tones when I tried to speak Mandarin.

The ride up was long and as the bus snaked its way up the mountain through tea fields the environment on the bus was very lively. People gave up their seats for the elderly, a boy who couldn’t have been more than ten standing in front of me was almost engulfed in his backpack, and the driver even stopped by a road side stand to buy a coffee and steamed buns for himself and one of the ladies on the bus. Despite my initial impatience, the ride was both pleasant and exciting.

We made it to Alishan around noon and the scenery was beautiful. Clouds were rolling through the trees and the air had cooled down considerably, it was nice to finally have something other than concrete and building on either side of me. The view was even better now that the kind lady in the purple hat had left me with one of her oranges.

The only problem was that the clouds had made their way up the mountain as well and despite being at Alishan I couldn’t see a thing. The mountains that I had traveled so far to see were hidden and the forest beyond the station was only visible in bits and pieces.

Although I was a bit disappointed that I wasn’t able to see the view (I had intended on taking the early bus for just that reason), the ride up was an experience in its own right.

### Review

Run-on sentences, 'village whizzed by' at every stop, and 'view even better' immediately before inability to see create awkward continuity. Real-place travel logistics not independently verified."}

## W052 — Personal communication / Personal statement

Round 1; 483 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional personal statement for a museum studies applicant whose volunteering taught them the value of careful documentation and acknowledging uncertainty. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

In my work cataloguing local history, I have often struggled with determining what to include in an object’s description and what to leave out. This confusion was especially prevalent in the first month of working at the Bellweather Local History Museum. On my very first day, the Collections Manager gave me a box of photographs donated by the daughter of a former local textile mill worker. One of the photographs included in the box had “Mill girls, 1911” written on the back. According to the donor, one of the young women depicted in the photograph was her grandmother, although she could not confirm with complete certainty.

My instinct was to include this information in the photograph’s catalogue record, but I was worried that if I did not know for certain that the date and people listed were accurate, it was not right to include it as a factual statement. I asked the Collections Manager if it would be alright to include what information we did know in the catalogue record. I proposed that I add a note that we were unsure of the accuracy of the date and people listed, but that based on the inscription on the back of the photograph and the donor’s account, that was the best information we had at that time. I would also include as much information about the photograph as I could, including the building in the background, so that at a later date, we could potentially find more information. She agreed that this was the best way to handle the situation, and later, I found that the mill actually opened in 1914, not 1911.

This experience changed the way I looked at documentation and cataloguing in museums. I now realize how important it is to be extremely thorough when describing an object, as well as to acknowledge any uncertainty about its origin or interpretation. When transcribing documents, I make sure to include misspellings as they originally appeared, and when there is a gap in information, I note that in the catalogue record. The process has also become a collaborative one with other volunteers, as we all work to create the most complete record possible for future use.

Through my work as a volunteer at the museum, I have realized that there are many limitations to the research that I am able to conduct, but also many opportunities for creative and responsible research practices. I hope to continue this work when I study museum studies, and become more knowledgeable about how museums are responsible for documenting and caring for their collections, both historically and ethically.

I hope to be a part of a museum that values thorough and complete documentation, even when there is incomplete information available. Although the photograph I initially struggled to catalogue is still not identified, I am proud of the way that the record represents what we do and do not know.

### Review

{"id": "W052", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Specific photograph/inscription episode demonstrates attribution and uncertainty rather than merely asserting values. Some repetition but convincing connection to museum studies and careful documentation."}

## W053 — Personal communication / Learning reflection

Round 1; 255 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a personal reflection on learning to draw and becoming more comfortable with mistakes. Use a specific incident rather than generic encouragement. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

I’ve avoided drawing hands for a while now.

I tried to draw my left hand last tuesday. I tried and erased. Tried and erased. At first, my hand looked like a mitten. Then it had extremely long fingers and a misplaced thumb.

When I accidentally drew a line too dark, I almost gave up, but I thought, “Well, what can I do with this? How can I make this mistake work for me?” And that dark line worked out to be a nice little shadow under my pinky finger, so I kept on drawing.

The hand that came out on the other end was a little lopsided, but it was very much my hand. And some of those “mistakes” I made along the way turned out to be just what the picture needed. I love that my mistakes are still there – they’re a part of the process and I’m going to keep them, scars and all.

This is something I’ve learned: it’s not enough to just draw. I also have to approach the drawing process with the right attitude, the right mindset. I have to go in open to being surprised, to the unexpected. I have to be open to finding a new way to look at something, to using a “mistake” to my advantage. Sometimes I get frustrated if I can’t draw what I’m looking for, but I’m learning to embrace the frustration, to work with it instead of against it. It’s pretty exciting to see where the drawing process takes me now.

### Review

## W054 — Personal communication / Family anecdote

Round 1; 310 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a warm family anecdote about a grandfather who labels every jar in his shed, including one containing parts of a long-gone bicycle. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

I will never forget the smell of my grandpa's shed. All the cedar wood shavings, mixed with the smell of machine oil and peppermint candy. There were numerous shelves full of glass jars. Some were full of things like buttons or short nails or screws. Either way, each of them had a label. Even the empty jars had labels on them.

He used to play a game with my brother and I when we were little, he would have us guess what certain jars were for. One jar was filled to the brim with ``important bits,'' and another was full of ``not important, but you never know when I'll need this.''

One day, I came across a jar that had little bits of metal inside. I could see a few washers, a little bell, and some other little silver things. I could n't quite make out what the label said, so I went to ask him. It read: ``Old bicycle, mostly.'' I was confused, since I could n't remember him ever having a bike. All I could remember was an old, blue bike that always seemed to have a little bit of a flat in the front tire. That's the one he used to push me around on when I was little.

``Why do you keep this?'' I asked.

``Well, that there bike has been very useful. It's helped your mother to school, your uncle to the creek, and has helped me push you around the yard when your legs were n't long enough to pedal,'' he answered.

He then proceeded to show me how to put something on the label myself.

Even now, the shed is still there, full of its jars. Each time I go to visit, I get a kick out of reading all of the labels and hearing the stories that go along with each one.

### Review

Dear diary,

Today, I cleaned my desk.

I wasn't sure if there would be anything left once I got rid of all the junk that had accumulated on it over the last few months, but there was.

There was quite a bit of sorting and filing to do (mostly into the recycling bin or the garbage), and I kept quite a few things that I had no reason to keep, other than the fact that it would take more time to get rid of them. In the end, though, I managed to clean the entire thing off without doing any of the work that I had told myself I'd do once the desk was cleared off.

It's funny, because I sat down at the end expecting to actually get some work done, but I didn't.

Instead, I placed my hands onto the unfamiliar expanse of emptiness and felt how smooth it was. I put my forehead down on the cool surface and just basked in the feeling of a clean desk. It was one of the most peaceful times of my life.

Maybe tomorrow I'll get something done, but for now, I'm just going to sit and enjoy what little peace I can find in my life.

What can you learn from an empty desk?

## W061 — Education / Lesson plan

Round 1; 657 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a 45-minute lesson plan for twelve-year-olds on distinguishing observation from inference using a closed box. Include timings and an assessment activity. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

By the end of this lesson, students will be able to distinguish between observations and inferences, support their inferences with evidence, and generate multiple inferences for a single set of evidence.

Materials: For this activity, you will need a box that securely closes and is not see-through. Place a few small objects in the box (e.g., a wooden block, a metal spoon, etc.). You may want to have a second box for students to look at. You will also need to make copies of the observations and inferences sheet for each student, and give each student a pencil. Be sure to tell the students that the box will not be opened during this lesson.

0:00 – 0:05 | Starter Activity: Show the students the box that you brought in for this lesson, but do not let them handle it. Ask them what they can tell you about the box. Allow a few students to respond, and write down a few of their responses. After hearing several responses, ask the students if they are telling you what they are seeing or hearing, or if they are trying to explain what they are seeing and hearing. Explain that what they are doing is making observations and inferences about the box.

0:05 – 0:12 | Teacher Model: Model for the students the process of making observations and inferences by investigating the box yourself. You can gently tilt the box and listen to it, etc. Think aloud, and be sure to differentiate between what you are observing and what you are inferring (e.g., I am hearing a rattling sound. I am inferring that there are a few things inside of the box). Ask the students why you don’t know for sure what is inside the box. Make sure they understand that you need to describe what you hear before you infer what is making the sound.

0:12 – 0:27 | Team Activity: Break the students up into groups of three or four. Have the students take turns tilting the box gently and listening to it. They are not allowed to open the box, try to force it open, or throw it. Each student must make at least three observations and at least two inferences. You may want to have them use the observations and inferences sheet to help them keep track of what they are doing. Make sure each student supports his or her inference with evidence. Remind them that there may be more than one inference that can be made for a set of observations.

0:27 – 0:35 | Class Discussion: After all the groups have completed their investigations, ask each group to share one observation and one inference. Write these down on the board in two columns, one for observations and one for inferences. Make sure the students understand that observations should not include inferences, and that inferences should be supported by evidence. Have the groups compare their observations and inferences and discuss where they agree and disagree. Make sure the students understand that it is OK to have different inferences, as long as they are not treated as facts.

0:35 – 0:42 | Individual Assessment: Have the students complete the evidence check on their own. This will require them to write down two observations, two different inferences, and a sentence describing how the observations support the inferences. Then have them tell you whether the statement “I think that there is a coin in the box” is an observation or an inference. Collect the evidence checks to determine how well the students are able to distinguish between observations and inferences, as well as how well they support their inferences with evidence.

0:42 – 0:45 | Exit Activity: Have the students complete the sentence “One thing I know from making observations is ____________________. One thing I am inferring is _________________________________________.” Use their responses, as well as their evidence check, to determine which students need additional practice with observations and inferences.

### Review

{"id": "W061", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Timings total 45 minutes, closed-box task and individual assessment align with objectives. 657/500 words and repeated 'make sure'; worksheets referenced but not supplied. Usable structure despite verbosity."}

## W062 — Education / Study guide

Round 1; 580 words requested 500; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write a practical study guide for students preparing for exams that require explanations. Include retrieval practice, spacing, and a realistic weekly example. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Many exams will contain questions which ask you to explain, compare or show how / why something happens. It can be difficult to know how to prepare for these types of questions. Here are some ideas to help you prepare for these types of questions.

Turn your learning into questions and then answer them for yourself. Close your book or notes, get out some paper and then answer the questions you have set yourself. This could be about a particular topic which you feel you need to know or it could be a question which you think might come up in your exam, such as ‘Explain the process of photosynthesis’. Try to include as much detail as you can when answering the questions. Then check your answers back with your notes or textbook and put any changes you need to make, in a different colour. This process is called retrieval practice and is much more effective than rereading and highlighting your notes.

As part of your revision practice explaining or comparing something which you need to know. Try to use the Claim, Evidence, Explain structure in your answers. When you have finished writing your answer, read it through and see if you think it is as good as it can be. Does it need editing? If yes, make those changes, but don’t copy everything out again, only the bits which you are changing.

It is important to space out your revision over a few days. Research shows that it is more effective to study a topic for shorter periods of time on different days rather than doing it all in one go. When you have revised a topic, try to revise it again the next day and then again in three or four days time. Then revise it again the following week. If there are bits which you are struggling to remember you may need to revise these more frequently.

Let’s imagine that you have a history exam next Friday. On Monday, after school, spend 25 minutes answering questions which you have taken from your notes for the work you have done that week. On Tuesday, spend 20 minutes testing yourself again on some of the same questions and write out an explanation on a cause for a particular event. Check your answer with your notes and make any changes you need to. On Wednesday spend 15 minutes looking at any questions you got wrong previously and then write out a comparison between two events from memory. On Thursday spend 25 minutes answering two questions which you think might come up in your exam. Put a time limit on yourself for this task. Check if you have used Claim, Evidence, Explain for each of your answers. On Friday morning, before your exam, try to remember as much as you can about the topic, but don’t try and learn a whole unit of work at the last minute!

Break your revision down into small sessions and be very specific about what you are going to revise in each session. Try to keep your phone out of the way whilst revising and use a timer to help you to stay focused for each session. Revise regularly and spread it out over a few days. Use retrieval practice when you revise and always check your answers back with a reliable source. You will soon be an expert at explaining all about your topic and will be able to do so in your exam.

### Review

{"id": "W062", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Concrete retrieval, feedback, spacing and realistic Monday–Friday schedule. Some repetition and overconfident 'soon be an expert' ending; general efficacy claims checked separately."}

## W063 — Education / Classroom debate

Round 1; 429 words requested 300; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write a balanced argument about letting students choose between an essay, presentation, and model when demonstrating learning. Explain when choice is inappropriate. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Do you think it is appropriate to allow students to select how they will be assessed? For example, if you have set an essay, should you let students opt to do a presentation or make a model to show what they have learnt?

Allowing students to show what they have learnt in different ways can be beneficial, a student may be able to show what they have learnt better in a presentation or a model than in an essay. Giving students a choice will motivate them to work hard to show what they have learnt and it will also encourage them to be creative. However, letting a student show what they have learnt in a different way may not be as effective in showing what they have learnt and a student may pick the option that they think will be easier, therefore not showing what they have learnt. Giving students a choice of how they want to be assessed can lead to unfairness, a student may appear more knowledgeable just because they are a good public speaker compared to another student who is nervous when speaking in public.

Giving students a choice of how they want to be assessed is appropriate if you are wanting to assess the general understanding of a topic and each of the assessment options will be equally effective in showing what a student has learnt. Teachers should also make sure that each assessment option includes the same requirements and that a student has learnt what was intended for them to learn, this can be done by having a quick chat with the student or asking them to write a short reflection on what they have learnt.

Giving students a choice of how they want to be assessed is not appropriate if you are wanting to assess a students ability in a specific area, for example if you are wanting to assess a students ability in writing an essay you cannot let them do a presentation instead. If a teacher is wanting to assess a students speaking skills then they must complete a speaking assessment and if a teacher wants to assess a students practical skills then they cannot design something instead of actually doing it. Giving students a choice of how they want to be assessed may also not be possible due to safety, accessibility, resources or if it is a formal assessment such as an exam.

Overall, allowing a student to choose how they want to be assessed is only appropriate if it is a valid way of showing what they have learnt.

### Review

{"id": "W063", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Valid distinction between understanding and a specifically assessed skill, with fairness/access constraints. Extremely repetitive 'what they have learnt', grammar errors, 429 versus 300 words."}

Written by Teigan Page This lunch box was made at the beginning of the 20th century. It would have been used to carry lunch to work, as back then a lot of people worked away from home. It has been heavily used as you can tell by the handle, it is very smooth where somebody would have been holding it. It is made out of wooden boards and is a rectangular shape. It has a hinged lid with a metal clasp which is used to hold the lid closed. On the lid you can see many scratches which would have come from use. The inside of the lunch box is darker than the outside which again must have come from use.

You can tell that it has been used a lot and it must have been a very useful thing to have for going to work. It would have kept lunch safe inside as being that it was made out of wood, it would have stopped it from getting squashed etc. I think that this lunch box would have been used regularly.

Although we know that this lunch box would have been used to take food to work, we don't know who it belonged to or who made it. We also don't know what sort of food it would have held. We don't know if it belonged to multiple people or if it was just one person's lunch box. We don't know where this person would have gone to work or when they would have ate their lunch. Although this lunch box gives us some information about what people would have used to carry their lunch to work in, it doesn't give us a lot of information.

## W065 — Education / Student feedback

Round 1; 233 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Give feedback on a student argument for two extra library hours each weekday. Praise its focus, ask for relevant evidence, and explain why weekend access belongs elsewhere. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Overall I think you did a very good job of staying on topic and being clear on what you are trying to get done, which is 2 extra hours each day of the week for students to use the library.

However, I do think that you should expand on the idea and include some relevant data in your argument in order to show why the library needs to be open two extra hours. For example, maybe you can include some data on the amount of students that visit the library right before it closes each day or how many books are checked out. You could also include data on how many students would actually benefit from this or even if there is enough staff members and money to actually keep the library open during these hours. Including this data will help strengthen your argument and will help you achieve your purpose of trying to convince the reader to give students two extra hours in the library each day.

Also, I think you should not include your idea of giving students access on the weekends. This should be proposed in a different way because it is not part of the two extra hours you are trying to get. If you include this in your argument it will make your ideas less clear and will probably detract from the purpose you are trying to achieve.

## W069 — Education / Assessment rubric

Round 1; 315 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a presentation rubric prioritizing claim, evidence, and reasoning over visual polish. Do not penalize nervousness by itself. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

To what extent do you think your presentation demonstrates each of the four performance indicators? Use the 4-point scale to rate yourself.

Content: Claims, evidence, and reasoning (90%) Claims (30%): 4 - Progresses a clear and focused claim addressing the topic. 3 - Progresses a clear claim addressing the topic with minor lapses in focus. 2 - Progresses a broad or partly unclear claim that may laps in focus or is not maintained throughout the presentation. 1 - Progresses no claim or one that is extremely difficult to understand.

Evidence (30%): 4 - Supports claim(s) with specific, credible evidence and identifies sources/examples when appropriate. 3 - Supports claim(s) with relevant evidence, but may need stronger support in some areas. 2 - Support claims with limited or uneven evidence that may be only loosely related to claim. 1 - Provides little evidence that is relevant to the claim.

Reasoning (30%): 4 - Clearly explains how evidence supports a line or reasoning, uses sound reasoning to connect claims and evidence, and examines implications and/or counterarguments. 3 - Reasoning is mostly clear, but may not always clearly explain how evidence supports claim(s). 2 - Incomplete explanation or simply states conclusion without supporting reasons. 1 - A listing of ideas with little to no explanation of how claims and evidence are related.

Organization and Access (10%) 4 - Sequences claims, evidence, and reasoning so that the presentation is easy to follow; uses effective delivery and/or text/visuals to enhance clarity and allow easy access to ideas. 3 - Organizes ideas so that the presentation is generally easy to follow, but may have minor lapses in sequence or some other elements that limit ease of access to ideas. 2 - Organization and/or presentation limit ease of access to ideas at times. 1 - Organization and/or presentation make it difficult to follow ideas.

Don't grade down for nervousness, accents, speaking style, etc.

### Review

## W070 — Education / Teaching reflection

However, I am not so sure if the students went away from this lesson with the intended learning goal. While most students were working together, I am not certain that they were working towards the learning goal or if they were just trying to complete the assignment and make it look good. The students were talking but they were not applying the main concept to their work. Although the students all completed the assignment, it does not mean that they all learned the concept.

I would alter this group activity by providing a way for me to know that the students learned the concept. I would have each individual student complete a short assignment that would require them to apply what they just learned to a question and have them explain how they got their answer in a sentence. The students would also be provided with a checklist that includes what they need to have completed in order to be successful in this assignment. That way, I will know if each student learned the concept and if there is any re teaching that needs to be done.

## W071 — Marketing and product writing / Product launch plan

Round 1; 641 words requested 500; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write a launch plan for a fictional compact reading lamp with a physical switch and replaceable bulb. Avoid unsupported performance claims. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Your new product, a small reading lamp, has a couple of key features that set it apart from the competition. It has a physical switch, not a touch-activated switch and the bulb can be replaced when it wears out. You’ve done your research and know that there are a lot of people out there that are looking for a lamp with these features. You want to ensure that your advertising is consistent with your message about the key features of the lamp. Before the launch, make sure you have all the facts about your lamp. What type of bulb does it take? Is there a maximum wattage? How exactly does the switch work? What are the dimensions of the lamp? What is the lamp made of? How is it cleaned? What safety certifications does the lamp have?

Six weeks before the launch date, take some pictures of your lamp and write some captions to accompany the pictures. You might want to show the lamp in a few different settings to show how versatile it is. You should also record a video showing how easy it is to change the bulb and how the physical switch works. Draft a FAQ sheet about the lamp so that you have answers to potential questions that customers might have.

Two weeks before the launch, start to roll out some information about the product. Post some of the pictures that you took online, on your web site, in an email, on social media, etc. Write a short blurb about the product and where people can find out more information. Send the product to some reviewers. Make sure you give them the same information sheet that you have posted online so that the message is consistent. Let them know that you aren’t expecting good reviews, but that if they do publish a review, you’d appreciate them mentioning that it was a sample. Keep an eye out for questions about the product and add any of them to the FAQ sheet.

When the big day comes, post the information about your product on your web site. Include the price, dimensions, what type of bulb it uses, how it is cleaned, what the warranty is, how it will be shipped to customers, etc. Make sure you include some pictures on the product information page. You should also include information about how to replace the bulb. Send out one email to your email list letting them know that the product is now for sale. Post some information on social media highlighting the key features of the lamp. At this point, you should only be selling the lamp through your online store and maybe one or two other places. If you are selling it in bookshops, make sure they have a guide on how to set it up and some information to answer common questions.

For the first month, keep an eye on how the product is selling, how many people are returning it, if you are getting any support requests about it, how many people are viewing the product information page on your web site, and what customers are saying about it. Also, keep an eye out for common questions that people are asking and incorporate them into your FAQ sheet. Don’t rely on click-through rates or social media stats to tell you if people like the product. If you run out of product, make sure you have a good idea of when it will be back in stock before you tell people. If you find a problem with the product, stop promoting it until you can resolve the issue and keep your customers informed.

After the first month is up, evaluate how well the product launch went and make changes if needed. Make sure that you are still sending a consistent message about the key features of your product.

### Review

{"id": "W071", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Actionable phased launch, specifications, sample disclosure, support and outcome metrics; avoids invented performance numbers. Assumes demand research already done and unique competitive features; 641/500 words and repetitive wording."}

## W072 — Marketing and product writing / Brand story

Round 1; 399 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a believable brand story for a fictional notebook company founded because attractive notebooks would not lie flat. Avoid claiming notebooks transform lives. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

I kept a notebook in my bag for years – whether to scribble grocery lists, jot down meeting notes or draw a chair I liked on the street. The notebooks I bought always ended up fighting me though, despite how great they looked on the shelf and how good the paper seemed to write on. The minute you opened them, the covers would spring back closed, making it hard to write in them.

I always found myself having to hold them down, prop them up with books or find another way to keep them open, which made it hard to write comfortably. After speaking to friends about this problem, I found that it was something a lot of people struggled with but had just adapted to – writing at the gutter of the notebook, propping it up with something or just putting up with it.

Working in packaging design, I found myself spending weekends testing out different constructions. I ordered a lot of paper samples, made some rough covers, worked with a bookbinder to create a few prototypes and figured out a combination that worked for me – allowing the book to stay open whilst also feeling nice and sturdy in the hand.

In 2018 I launched Common Fold with one notebook, in 3 muted colours and a local printer, who I’m still working with today. It’s been a small but incredibly fun project to work on, always testing out the prototypes in the same way you would test paper at your desk to see if it worked for you.

The first edition of the notebooks weren’t perfect, so I tweaked a few things (including changing the covers and the paper) to improve them, as well as adding a dot grid version which has been really popular with customers and more recently a lined edition. The range is small, but I hope to grow it slowly over time with customer feedback.

Common Fold remains small for the time being, with limited runs of notebooks, but I am committed to the idea of making a high quality product that focuses on the finer details which makes a difference to the way you use it. It’s a notebook for people to write in – whether that’s taking notes, making lists, planning something, sketching or jotting down a thought you want to remember.

I hope it ends up in your bag too!

### Review

{"id": "W072", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Believable origin/prototyping/revision story and no life-transformation promises. 399/500 narrowly below tolerance; minor agreement errors and generic final appeal."}

## W073 — Marketing and product writing / Case study

Round 1; 365 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a clearly fictional case study about a makers club trialing shared booking software for two workstations. Include a limitation and an adoption difficulty. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Our club is a makers club (the name is made up for the purposes of this post - Harborlight Makers Club), with 12 members, and two pieces of equipment that we often need to book in advance for classes and other work - a laser cutter and a large format printer. Up until this spring we had been using a paper-based calendar to reserve sessions on the equipment.

This spring we decided to give your system a try for 8 weeks, to see how it would work for us. It's been great having someone else (thank you!) set up the time that we can book, and it's been great to be able to book time via cell phone or computer when I'm not at the club. Messages and reminders are great, too. It's also been very helpful being able to limit how much time someone can book (we chose two hours per machine per week), and having it free up if someone doesn't use their slot.

Overall it has been a good experience; it's been helpful to be able to easily see when the machines are booked, and we've had fewer instances of people booking over each other than in the previous two months. At least one member has been able to arrive with the right material for their project, thanks to the reminder.

One issue we've encountered is that the software doesn't allow us to indicate when a machine is not available for use - for example if someone is still cleaning up from the previous session, or if a machine is in for maintenance.

Another issue is that not everyone in our club uses their email very often, and several found it hard to use on their older cell phones. Some people still prefer to use the paper calendar that we keep, so we've had a volunteer setting up accounts for people, and also keeping the paper calendar up to date as a backup.

At the end of the trial period we voted to continue to use the software, and are setting up an orientation and a shared tablet that will be available near the machines for people to use to book sessions.

### Review

If we assume that the two sketchbooks have exactly the same type of paper and binding, there are a few differences based on size:

The benefits of a pocket size sketchbook are: a. You are more likely to have it with you at all times b. You can pull it out in crowded spaces more easily c. It will encourage you to make quick sketches, notes, and small studies d. You may find that the small size of the pages makes it easier to work on, as the project seems smaller The drawbacks of a pocket size sketchbook are: a. The benefits of a large sketchbook are: a. You have more room on the page to make a large drawing b. You can draw larger, more expansive compositions c. It is easier to make detailed studies and diagrams d. You can experiment with size more easily e. You can draw with a more relaxed motion The drawbacks of a large sketchbook are: a. It is less likely that you will have it with you at all times b. It takes up more room on a table or on your lap c. It can be difficult to draw in when you are standing up or traveling

Additional things to consider: a. A pocket size sketchbook will fill up much quicker than a larger one b. Drawing in a large sketchbook can capture the feeling of a scene, while a small sketchbook will force you to make choices about what to include.

If you are looking for availability and portability, go for the pocket size. If you are looking for space to draw, go for the large one. It may be best to keep both available at all times, so that you can take advantage of the benefits of each.

## W081 — Practical guides and reviews / Travel packing

Round 1; 527 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a practical guide to packing for a five-day trip with one small bag, including choosing clothes and avoiding unnecessary duplicates. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Five Day Trip Packing List How To Pack For A Weekend The most effective way to pack for a five day trip is to start with the bag. You need to know how big it can be in order to pack accordingly, especially if you're flying as many airlines have restrictions on the size of luggage that you can take on board. It's always best to leave some room in your bag in case you want to buy anything on your trip or pick up anything that you need while you're there. It's also useful to have a small backpack or a soft luggage bag that can be squashed into small spaces. Next you need to think about what to pack, starting with clothing. Check the weather forecast and also what you'll be doing on your trip.

Choose a colour scheme for your clothing, ideally two or three neutral colours and an accent colour. Plan what you'll wear for each activity but make sure that the items can be mixed and matched so you can make different outfits from them. For a normal trip you should pack three tops, two bottoms, one extra layer and one outfit for a special occasion. You should also pack one pair of shoes, however it's best to wear these with you on the plane or train to save space in your luggage. If you need to pack another pair of shoes then wear the other pair. It's best to choose comfortable shoes that can be worn for a range of activities so you don't have to pack more than one pair. You should also wear a jacket if you need one.

Try and choose items that can be mixed and matched and that you can re-wear. Pick clothing that doesn't need special care, that is easy to wash, dries quickly and is wrinkle resistant. You may want to pack a waterproof but try not to bring anything bulky that you may not need.

You should also pack five pairs of underwear and socks, one set of clothing to sleep in and if you plan on exercising while you're away, pack an outfit for that, otherwise you should be able to do your exercise in normal clothing. Don't pack any extra outfits in case you need them.

Lay out all of your items to make sure that you haven't packed anything that is similar, such as two similar jumpers or shoes. Only pack one set of toiletries and make sure that they are small. Also check to see if your accommodation provides any toiletries or a hairdryer so that you don't need to bring them with you. Pack any other items that you need such as a water bottle, charger etc.

Finally, pack your luggage, putting the heavier items at the bottom and any items that you will need while travelling on top. Roll your clothes up and put them in packing bags to keep them separate and make the most of the space available. Pack any important documents, valuables etc. in an easily accessible place. Finally, check your luggage one last time and take out anything that you don't really need.

### Review

Stray 'Five Day Trip Packing List How To Pack For A Weekend' heading, muddled shoe instructions and rigid 'normal trip' quantities reduce polish and adaptability."}

## W082 — Practical guides and reviews / Photography tutorial

Round 1; 508 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how a beginner can photograph a household object using window light and a sheet of white card. Include common mistakes and how to check the result. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

If you don't have a small studio with professional lights, don't despair. You can create some excellent images with some basic materials and a little knowledge. For this lesson, choose a small object, clean and with some shape. Put it on something nice, close to a window but not in direct sunlight, and turn off the nearest lamps.

Traditionally you'll see your subject a couple of feet away from the window, but experiment with this as you go along. It's better to have the window to the side of your subject than behind you. If the sunlight is hitting your subject, move it further away or use a sheer curtain to soften the light.

Place the piece of white card to the right of your object. Make sure that you place it so that it is just out of the frame of the image, because you'll want it to bounce some light back onto your subject. The beauty of this is that you can control it simply by moving it closer to or further away from your object. Keep some shadows, because they give the image depth, but try and get an even lighting to the whole piece. Check that you're not including the white card in reflections, though.

Next, sort out your background. It's best if it's nice and tidy. When you take your first photo, try to leave a little space around your subject. Get the camera as close to the level of the object as you can, but try a few slightly from above, too. Make sure that you keep your phone steady when you take the picture. On most phones you can tap on the screen to set the focus, so touch your subject to do this. You should also be able to adjust the brightness of the image using the sun icon and slider. Try a few shots, just tweaking the brightness as you go along.

Now take a look at the common errors below. The most common are photographing your subject in direct sunlight, having lamps on in the room and putting your object too close to the window. When using your white card, the usual mistake is putting it too close to your subject and making the image too flat. Other errors to avoid are messy backgrounds, not keeping the horizon straight, not focusing properly and having reflections in your image. One last thing to look out for is that, although you can change the white balance, your camera won't turn a plain background white if it isn't getting enough light.

Each time you take a photo, look at it on your camera/phone to make sure it's in focus and not over-exposed. Check that you haven't cut any of the object out of the image. Also try to see if the shadow looks OK. Compare a couple of the photos that you've taken with and without the white card, and choose the one that you think best displays your subject. Try to look at the image on a larger screen, too.

### Review

{"id": "W082", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Practical light, background, focus and comparison checks. Tells reader to put reflector on right without establishing window on left; fixed geometry and blanket too-close-to-window warning need qualification."}

## W083 — Practical guides and reviews / Gardening guide

Round 1; 272 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how to assess sunlight, wind, and drainage before starting a balcony garden. Avoid prescribing one watering schedule for every plant. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Where is the Sun? Before you buy plants for your balcony garden, spend a few days observing when it is sunny and when it is in the shade. Check at various times of the day to see how much direct sun reaches your balcony. Even if your balcony seems shaded, nearby pale colored walls may reflect a lot of light. The amount of sun may also vary at different times of the year. Once you have a good idea about the amount of light that reaches your balcony, you can choose plants that will thrive in specific spots on your balcony.

How windy is it? The corners of your balcony and the area above the roof tops may be quite windy. At various times, check how strong the wind is. This will help you decide where to position the containers so that they are not blown over.

What is the drainage like? Make sure that your balcony has good drainage. The floor should be slightly sloped so that water runs towards the drain. Make sure that the drain is not blocked. You should not use saucers under your containers unless you can make sure that excess water does not run onto your balcony. Be a good neighbor and make sure that the water does not run onto other people's balconies. If you are in doubt about the drainage or the weight your balcony can support, ask the building manager.

How often will you have to water? This will depend on many factors including the wind, the amount of sun, the type of containers you use, and the types of plants you select.

## W084 — Practical guides and reviews / Home organization

Round 1; 293 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an entryway organization guide for a shared household, focusing on real habits rather than maintaining a perfect appearance. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

1. Arrange it to fit in with your lifestyle, not somebody else's. Spend a week observing what comes into your house and where things are placed down. Put storage solutions in the best place for your family.

2. Work out where 'shared' items need to live -- coat hooks, a place for keys, a basket for items that need to go out of the house. If you have enough hooks, you could label them or give each member of the family a section. Have a couple of spare tote bags for things that need to go out of the house (library books, returns etc.).

3. Don't expect perfection. People are going to bring in shoes, so have a mat they can stand on to remove their shoes, and a bin to put them in. People will bring in wet things, so have somewhere for their brolly. Try to make it as easy as possible for your family to keep it as tidy as possible.

4. Agree on a few rules that the whole family can adhere to -- putting keys somewhere, bags on hooks, sorting through the mail once or twice a week etc.

5. Give it a quick reset when you take out the rubbish or do your weekly shop. If it's taking too long to do this, something isn't working, and you may need to make some changes to make it more practical for your family. If things are constantly piling up somewhere, that's a sign that what you have there at the moment isn't working, and you need to make some changes.

Remember, the aim here is not to have a perfectly tidy entry, but to make it a little bit easier to leave and enter your house in peace!

## W085 — Practical guides and reviews / Cooking method

Round 1; 249 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how to roast mixed vegetables, accounting for different cooking rates, overcrowding, and how to judge doneness. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Heat the oven to 425F degrees (220C degrees). Cut vegetables into similar sized pieces based on when they should go into the oven. Hard vegetables like potatoes, carrots, squash, beets, etc. take a long time to cook. Softer vegetables like zucchini, bell peppers, asparagus, broccoli, etc. take a shorter time to cook. Toss the vegetables with enough oil to lightly coat them, and season with salt and pepper. Arrange the vegetables in a single layer on a rimmed baking sheet, making sure to leave space between the vegetables. If you put them too close together, the vegetables will steam rather than brown. Use 2 baking sheets if necessary.

Place the dense vegetables in the oven first. After about 15-20 minutes, turn the vegetables over and place the remaining vegetables on the same baking sheet (or a separate one if preferred, in which case check them earlier). Cooking time will depend on the size and type of vegetables you are using. Turn the vegetables over once to help ensure even browning. Don’t turn them over too often, though.

The vegetables are ready when they brown and get crispy on the edges and are tender when you pierce them with a fork or small knife. Potatoes and carrots should be tender, but not mushy. If the outsides of the vegetables are browning but the insides are still too firm, turn the heat down a little bit and keep cooking. Remove the vegetables from the oven as soon as they are ready.

### Review

## W088 — Practical guides and reviews / Troubleshooting

Round 1; 207 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain what to check when a shared printer accepts a job but produces no pages, and why repeatedly resubmitting can cause problems. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

Is your printer on or offline? Does it have enough paper in the correct paper tray? Is the correct paper size and type loaded? Does your printer have any errors on the display such as paper, jams, toner or maintenance? Does it have jobs sitting in the printer that are waiting for something, like a different paper size or a secure release code?

Look at the print queue on your computer, are you sending your document to the correct shared printer? Is the printer server or network connectivity in question? Are other people having the same problem? Check with your printer administrator to see if they can look at the shared print queue and see if there are any issues with the print spooler. Try to print something once you have resolved any issues.

The most important thing to not do, is keep sending your document to the printer. You may just be sending it into a queue that is having delays for some reason and you are just using up the resources on the print server. When the queue starts to work again, you may have 10 copies of your document print out, which can waste paper, supplies, and cause issues if the document is confidential.

## W089 — Practical guides and reviews / Repair decision

Round 1; 306 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a balanced guide to deciding whether to repair or replace an ordinary household object. Discuss uncertainty and usefulness, not just purchase price. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Should you fix or replace it? When something at home breaks, you often have to make a decision: should you have it fixed, or should you replace it? There are a number of things to think about when deciding what to do: How important is this thing to you? How often do you use it? How much will it affect your life if it isn't fixed? Is it easy to borrow or find a replacement while it is being fixed?

Do you have a warranty? How much will it cost to fix or replace? Is it easy to get parts for it? Is it easy to find someone to fix it? Is it easy to know what is wrong with it, or will you have to pay extra to find someone to work out the problem? Is it likely that there will be other problems that weren't noticed before, making it more expensive to fix?

How long do you expect it to last for if you fix it? Is it old and likely to break down again soon? If you decide to replace it, how will you decide which new one to buy? Are all new products the same? Or are some better quality, or more reliable, or easier to fix, or more energy efficient, or easier to get parts for?

What are some of the benefits of fixing things, rather than replacing them? What are some of the dangers or difficulties of fixing things, rather than replacing them? What should you do if something is dangerous?

If you are not sure what you should do, what could you do to make a decision that isn't very risky? What are some of the things to think about when you are deciding whether to fix or replace something? How will you decide which is the best option?

### Review

## W090 — Practical guides and reviews / Beginner hobby

### Prompt

Give a beginner advice on starting a small stamp collection, recording uncertainty, and avoiding the assumption that age alone determines value. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Here are some suggestions for starting your collection: Consider collecting stamps of a specific subject or region. Start small. You may be interested in collecting stamps depicting birds, space related stamps, or even stamps from a certain country. For beginners it is best to start with a few inexpensive stamps from a reputable dealer, local club, or even from a collector who may want to help you start a collection. Whatever stamps you collect, handle them carefully, preferably with stamp tongs, and store them in a dry location in stamp albums or archival quality stock pages.

Keep track of your stamps. It is a good idea to have a place to record information about your stamps. Consider noting the country of origin, date of issue, description, condition, source of acquisition, cost, and your source for this information. It is better to note that something is “possibly” a certain way rather than to guess when you are not sure. Take a picture of your stamps with your cell phone so you can refer to them later.

Be aware that old stamps are not necessarily valuable. There are many factors that go into determining the value of a stamp. Many very old stamps are quite common. Some newer stamps are quite valuable. Be cautious of prices you see on the internet. Collect stamps because you enjoy it, but do your homework before spending a lot of money!

## W091 — Civic and community writing / Policy brief

Round 1; 486 words requested 500; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Write a policy brief proposing a six-week evening-library-hours trial. Assess new access, costs, and displacement of daytime services rather than just visitor counts. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Public libraries extend hours to meet public need. My proposal is to allow a six-week trial period at one branch of your choice (selected in consultation with the community) to extend opening hours for eight hours a week for six weeks (48 hours) during evening hours chosen by a short survey. The eight hours could be split over two evenings a week, when the library would stay open until 9pm. This would not affect your daytime opening hours or services in any way.

The key here is that this would target users who are not catered to by the current opening hours. To prove this, you would need to take some measurements. You would need to take a four-week baseline of current opening hours before the evening hours begin to assess how many people use the library in daylight hours, how the library is used, staffing levels and how often it closes. You would need to record how many people use the library in the evening hours, how many of these are distinct and how many of them are not catered for by current opening hours. This can be assessed by a short survey and anonymous counting of library users. You also need to record how the library is used during these hours and how accessible the library is for people using it in the evening (disability access, safety, etc).

You will also need to record the entire incremental cost of opening the library during these evening hours, to make sure that it is open with enough staff to operate safely. You should record the total cost of opening the library for these hours as well as the cost per hour.

It is also important to record whether the evening opening hours are affecting daytime users. You can do this by comparing the effect of the evening hours on opening hours, staffing, cancelled programs, service use, etc with your four-week baseline, as well as by comparing these to a similar library that has not extended its hours.

At the end of the six-week trial period, you should record how many people the evening opening hours have catered for, how the library has been used by these people, how much the extended opening hours have cost and whether extending the evening opening hours have had an effect on daytime library users. If the evening hours have catered for people not served by current opening hours, haven't adversely affected daytime users and you can find the money in your budget to continue it, you should extend the evening opening hours. If the library isn't being used very much in the evenings, you should investigate whether this is due to the days you've chosen to open, a lack of outreach, the location of the branch, etc. Whatever your decision, you should publicise the result of the trial and take on board people's opinions on what you should do next.

### Review

{"id": "W091", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "5", "notes": "Strong baseline/comparator, new access, incremental cost and displaced-service measures. Opening promises no daytime impact before measuring it, contradicting later caution. Six-week intervention plus separate baseline is clear."}

## W092 — Civic and community writing / Evaluation summary

Evaluate a fictional four-week community shuttle pilot with 480 passenger journeys and high use on market mornings. Costs exceeded estimates; avoided car trips were not measured. Aim for 500 words.

It is good to see the Shuttle service has had a good take up during the four week trial period. The timing of the Shuttle service seems to have been a big factor especially on Market mornings which is to be expected so perhaps a more focussed service at these times would have worked better if running a service at other times of the day did not have as good a take up. A total of 480 passenger journeys over a 4 week period gives an average of 120 per week so if this could be sustained then it has to be a good thing.

My concern is the cost of running the Shuttle service as it seems to be higher than expected so unless this can be controlled then it will make it more difficult to make it a sustainable service. It would be useful to know the actual cost of running the Shuttle service and what it was expected to cost. It would also be useful to know the number of passenger journeys each day to see if it correlated with the cost.

Another thing to consider is the number of car journeys the Shuttle service has replaced as this was probably one of the main reasons for introducing the service in the first place. It is difficult to say how many car journeys it has replaced but to assume that each passenger journey was a car journey is probably incorrect although it may have stopped some people from using their car. Statements such as "The service will help reduce carbon emissions and traffic congestion in the town" may be a little premature at this stage.

Overall I think the Shuttle service has had a reasonable impact on the community so perhaps extending the service for a while longer to gather a little more information would be a good idea before making a final decision on its future. Knowing the following information may be helpful:- a) How many people used the service on a weekly basis and if they used it week after week. b) Why people used the service, c) Did they find the service accessible, d) What the expected weekly cost was and what it actually cost to run the service, e) How much it should realistically cost to run and how this can be achieved. f) How many people used their car instead of using the Shuttle service.

Once this information is available then a more informed decision can be made on whether the service is a success and whether it should be continued or not.

I would like to ask that the city consider providing a few options for quiet play. Not all children enjoy climbing or spinning or playing in a busy, noisy area. It would be great to provide a small sensory garden, water/sand table, musical options, or other quiet areas for play. This would allow children who may have a need for this type of play to enjoy the playground, and it would be great for any child or adult that just needed a break from the busy, loud area as well. It would be a great asset to allow a wider population of people to use the playground and feel welcomed.

Also, it would be great to add some shade and seating for the adults/grandparents/children to enjoy the playground in a more comfortable manner.

## W096 — Civic and community writing / Budget explanation

Round 1; 268 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain a community garden budget's distinction between operating costs, one-time improvements, expected donations, and reserves. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

In the Community Garden Budget, we break down funds into different types of expenses, so that it's clear how money is being spent and when.

1) Operating Costs: These are regular, ongoing expenses that the garden will have in order to operate. In our example, these include water, seeds, soil, tool repairs, insurance, permit fees, etc. Operating expenses are the things you'll need to spend money on every season or year in order to keep the garden running.

2) One-Time Improvements: These are investments the garden will make in itself in order to improve it. They may be fairly expensive, but are not meant to be recurring expenses. One-time improvements might include building new raised beds, building a shed, creating accessible paths, installing an irrigation system, etc. After these improvements are made, they should not need to be repeated for many years, if ever.

3) Expected Donations: These are donations that you expect to receive, but which you can not count on. They may be cash donations, in-kind donations (e.g. compost, lumber, tools) or in-service donations (i.e. people donating their time to work in the garden). Don't count on these donations in order to pay for the bills - if they don't come through, you'll need to find another source of funds.

4) Reserves: These are funds that have been set aside for unexpected expenses or future expenses. They should not be spent on regular expenses or be used to balance the operating budget. Rather, they should be used in cases of unexpected expenses, like a sudden increase in the water rates or the need for new equipment.

### Review

{"id": "W096", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Four budget distinctions clear with examples; expected donations not treated as assured funds. Reserve prohibition too absolute for all policies and 268/200 words; opening calls all categories expenses although donations/reserves are not expenses."}

## W099 — Civic and community writing / Impact report

Round 1; 306 words requested 300; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Write a cautious impact summary for a fictional tool library with 140 members and 620 loans. Do not infer money saved or waste avoided directly from loans. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

We have a 620 loans and 140 members at our (fictional) tool library. How many tools have we saved from being bought, and how does that help the environment?

It sounds like a simple calculation, 620 tools that might not have been bought - except that isn’t quite right. For one thing we don’t know how many of those loans would have ended in a tool being bought, people might have had tools they could have used, or they might not have done the job that needed the tool at all! We also don’t know how many of our members already have tools, and how often they have borrowed things instead of buying them. It could be that all our members have drills and saws, and the 620 loans are for things they didn’t already own.

There are also all sorts of other issues to consider if we want to look at the environmental impact. If we don’t know how many tools are in circulation how can we know how many we have saved from being produced in the first place? Or from the emissions of transportation and manufacture? Or from going in the trash at the end of their life? It’s very hard to say.

So what can we say? Well we know we have 140 members and 620 loans - that’s a great start! We know people are using what we have, and we can find out more by asking them - are they borrowing things they wouldn’t have otherwise, are they bringing things back time and time again? We can also look at the tools we have, are they being repaired or replaced, and again ask our members how having access to the tools has helped them. This is a great start - we just need to remember not to assume too much!

## W100 — Civic and community writing / Community mediation

Your neighbors may have different expectations about acceptable noise levels in the garden. You may need it to be quiet earlier in the evening so that you can read, rest or enjoy having your windows open. They, on the other hand, may want to use the garden for talking with visitors or just to relax after a hard day's work. While neither of you is necessarily wrong, the lack of clear guidelines about what is acceptable and what isn't may be causing problems when one of you perceives the other as being too loud.

There are areas where you are able to find agreement:

It might be possible to compromise by agreeing that after 8 pm on week nights, you will both keep the volume of conversations down, refrain from playing music (or playing it very low - no amplification) and end any get togethers by 9 pm. On weekends, you might allow for longer get togethers until 10 pm, as long as the person hosting the gathering gives the other person advance notice so that he/she can plan accordingly. Try out the new guidelines for four weeks and then reevaluate to see if either of you needs to make any modifications. If you have a problem with the new guidelines, bring the problem up directly with the other person in a calm manner and work together to resolve it.

## W101 — Creative writing / Literary story

Round 2; 547 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a story about a night cleaner in a concert hall who discovers an unfinished letter under a seat. Build toward a difficult decision without revealing a hidden celebrity connection. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

It was 2am and Nessa was doing her usual rounds cleaning up the concert hall.

She worked her way methodically through the rows from back to front. Usually she would find lots of things dropped under the seats; coins, cough sweets, gloves, you name it. But this time when she knelt under seat G-14 her mop got caught on some folded up paper.

The paper was creased in quarters and soft around the edges, it had been folded like this for a while. There was no envelope and no name written on the outside. Technically Nessa should have taken the letter to the lost property desk, but curiosity got the better of her and when she picked it up the letter opened up in her hands.

`` Dear Ruth,'' Nessa began to read, `` I have tried to write this letter so many times and I really do need you to know that when you left I told everyone it was your decision to go, it was easier at the time than admitting that I was the one who...''

Nessa quickly folded the letter back up again.

The concert hall seemed a lot bigger now that it was after midnight. A work light was still on over the stage, shining down on a forgotten music stand, alone in the darkness. Nessa could just picture someone huddled up in seat G-14, writing this letter by the pale glow of the stage light, leaving before they ran out of courage to deliver it themselves.

Nessa decided to take the letter to the security office, Mr. Vale was busy at the desk logging up a lost scarf when she arrived.

`` What are you going to do with it?'' Nessa asked.

`` Well its just a piece of unsealed paper, I suppose Ill keep it for a month and if no one claims it then it'll go in the confidential bin.''

`` Even if there is an address on the back?''

`` You know the rules Nessa, if there is a name and address then Ill contact the owner. We ca n't read lost property and we ca n't deliver it, people leave all sorts of stuff behind you know.''

Nessa knew that Mr. Vale was right but it did n't make it seem right.

She could clearly make out an address scrawled in small neat writing on the back of the letter. In her mind she saw it sitting on a plastic tray for thirty days before ending up shredded in the confidential bin. Or maybe she saw someone desperately awaiting the contents of this letter. Or maybe she imagined the recipient reading a painful confession that they did n't want to know.

Nessa thought of a letter she had in a kitchen drawer at home that she had been meaning to send to her brother for three years.

`` Do you want me to log it in?'' Mr. Vale asked holding up a clear evidence pouch.

Nessa looked back towards the darkened rows of seats, the concert was over and the writer was gone. The words were now her burden to carry.

`` Yes please,'' Nessa said as she slipped the letter into the evidence pouch, `` and if anyone asks what it says then dont tell them.''

### Review

{"id": "W101", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Clear night-cleaner setting, unfinished confession, and privacy decision without celebrity twist. Address would trigger contact under stated policy, yet narrator imagines automatic destruction without resolving that option. Run-ons, missing apostrophes, and backtick dialogue weaken polish; concrete personal parallel supports motivation."}

## W102 — Creative writing / Mystery

Round 2; 580 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fair-play mystery about a village seed library whose rare bean seeds disappear. Plant two relevant clues before the solution and avoid a supernatural explanation. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

It must have happened during the night, for when I arrived at work on Tuesday morning, the packet of Ashcombe Speckled Beans was gone.

`` It could only have come out of the seed library's cabinet,'' I said, `` and that was definitely locked when I left.'' There was a window at the back of the cabinet, but that was securely locked as well. Besides, even if someone had got in that way, there was nothing else missing.

`` They were rare beans,'' Tom Bell, who kept the books for the library, said, `` so someone must have gone out of their way to steal them. Perhaps they had a key.'' He looked enquiringly at the rest of us.

`` They were the only remaining strain we had locally, so we should make every effort to find out what's happened to them,'' said Mrs Vale, our oldest volunteer.

Well, that went without saying. It was quite a tussle to find out who had a key; it turned out that Tom had the key with him all the time, and Mrs Vale had one as well. I had a spare in the village office. It was a relief when we'd questioned the two gardeners, because neither of them had keys. They weren't the type to lie, either. Checking the visitors' book, it seemed that only Tom had been into the library on the Monday to count the packets and Mrs Vale had popped in to water the window boxes.

There were two strange things about the whole affair. Looking into the cabinet, there was a patch on the shelf where the beans had been kept, quite free from dust. Yet there was a thick layer of dust all around it, but undisturbed, so it seemed that the beans had not been taken recently. That didn't make much sense to Tom, for he'd counted them the day before.

The second thing was when Mrs Vale asked whether anyone had checked if the beans had been tested for germination.

`` Tested?'' Tom asked, looking puzzled. `` What do you mean, tested?''

`` Well, you know, for germination,'' Mrs Vale said, her mouth tightening a little. `` I remember reading somewhere in the notebook that they had been tested.''

We looked in the notebook, but there was nothing about germination. All it said was that the beans had been stored in a cool, dry place, written in Mrs Vale's hand. I remembered that the cabinet was somewhat damp when it rained, and that Mrs Vale had often told me how dry her pantry was, with the little stove next door.

We didn't accuse her of anything, but asked if we could look in her pantry, which we found just above the flour tin. There were only a dozen or so, but Mrs Vale looked very uncomfortable when I found them.

`` I was going to return them, only the cabinet seemed so damp, and I was afraid that they'd go rotten in there.''

`` But why not tell us?'' I said.

`` Well, you know the regulations; there must be at least one packet in the library at all times, so I thought I could return them before anyone noticed. Then I worried in case they hadn't been tested properly, and I'd have to tell someone.''

Tom was quite happy to take them back to the library, and we comforted Mrs Vale by telling her how we'd be planting them in the community plot.

### Review

{"id": "W102", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "2", "development": "3", "notes": "Provides clues before a naturalistic solution, but claims a dust-free patch with undisturbed surrounding dust shows the packet was not taken recently, an unsupported inference. Germination clue and withholding rationale are contrived; pantry sentence has wrong referent. Mystery resolves but evidence does not securely support it."}
