### Output

`` 16:00:00, starting hourly countdown: 00:59:59''

`` You need to send the pressure readings. ``

`` The pressure readings ca n't wait a few minutes. My daughter's birthday is tomorrow, and I promised I'd call her. ``

`` Well, you ca n't promise her more than you've got. You can message her in 5 minutes. ``

`` I've been messaging her for six weeks. She wants to hear my voice. ``

He glanced out the porthole at the black void beyond, as a stream of bubbles rose idly by. The comm console chirped, indicating that only 53 minutes remained.

`` You know, if those pressure seals give out we could lose the upper lab, maybe even the entire station. ``

`` And if we lose this uplink, they wo n't know. ``

`` They'll get the readings. ``

`` Is that all you care about, your precious readings? ``

`` It's not just the readings; these findings may be enough to secure continued funding for the project. ``

`` So it's either my daughter's birthday or your career. ``

She paused for a moment.

`` How long will it take? ``

`` Ten minutes? ``

`` Fine. I'll queue up my recording for next time. ``

`` You what? ``

`` You heard me. Take your ten minutes and tell your daughter you love her. ``

He smiled as the countdown clock hit 46:12 and pressed the record button.

## W104 — Creative writing / Fantasy

Round 2; 329 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fantasy story in which a town's bell can erase one regret each winter. Follow a baker deciding whether to use it, and make the cost clear. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

It's a tradition in Bellweather, every winter, under the blue moon, a bell will ring for those who wish to rid of a regret. Just go to the tower, ring the bell and tell the bell your regret. The next day, the regret will no longer exist.

So will the memories.

My regret is.. Let's just say it happened 12 years ago. My brother was running from the burning house and the fallen wood managed to pin him down. I was strong enough to lift it but I chose to run for help. By the time I came back with the neighbours, the roof had collapsed onto him.

Every winter I try to go to the tower, but I never can bring myself to do it. So this year I took a bread with me and gave it to the man in charge of the tower. He accepted it and let me go into the tower.

I went all the way to the top where the bell was and put my hand on it.

`` My regret is.. I let my brother die''

The bell shivered and started to ring.

And I saw his face start to fade away, how he laughed, his crooked teeth when he grinned, I quickly removed my hand from the bell.

And it stopped ringing.

If I did n't have the regret, I might not do the things I do for him. I might not remember him as much, if at all. The bell might be able to erase my regret, but it ca n't erase my love for him.

So I went down the tower and saw the people waiting for the next day to come.

I went back home, and my regret was still there. Just like his name.

And the next day the town woke up to a nice snowfall and I opened my bakery and let the smell of honeybread fill the streets. And maybe in that smell, he's laughing.

### Review

{"id": "W104", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Baker and regret-memory tradeoff are present with concrete emotional stakes. Rules say effects arrive the next day, yet memories fade immediately and stopping the bell reverses them without explanation. Several run-ons and nonidiomatic phrases weaken otherwise effective ending."}

## W105 — Creative writing / Children's fiction

### Output

Once upon a time there was a fox who wanted to build a kite. He found some sticks, some paper, and a piece of string.

`` Hey, I'll just do this myself," he said.

So the fox began to build the kite. First he put the sticks together and then he put the paper on, but when he pulled the string one of the sticks broke!

The fox was very sad. He tried to tie the stick back together, but each time he tried to tie it a knot the string kept slipping. He tried to pull the string tighter but then the paper ripped.

Just then his friend, the turtle, walked by.

`` Would you like to help me, turtle?" asked the fox.

`` Of course," said the turtle. The turtle got some reeds out of her basket and showed the fox how to tie the stick to the reed using strong grass. After that they patched up the paper together.

Before long the kite was done and the two friends went up to the top of the hill to fly it. The kite went sooo high!

`` I'm really glad I asked you for help, turtle," said the fox.

`` I'm really glad you did," said the turtle.

They took off, paddling to the other side of the river, making sure to avoid any obstacles in the water. It took some time, but they slowly made their way below the supplies, edging closer and closer to the rocks.

Using his strength, and with the help of the ranger and the woman, he got the box into the canoe and placed it safely behind him.

## W110 — Creative writing / Historical fiction

'Only one, sorry.' She brought her foot up to stomp the sand from it before bringing the postbag down again to deliver the next. In this miserable place it was a wonder that post still came through at all. The damned wind was always blowing sand all over the place and the beach was hardly a glittering jewel with the grey sky and small breakers taking on the groynes constantly.

'Is there anything for you Mrs Pritchard?'

It was the handwriting, it was always the handwriting. The heavy capital letters with the hooked, rounded final letters. It was just like the letters that came for her mother when her brother was in prison.

'It's for Mr Vale in room 7 miss.'

A man stepped from the doorway, the sounds of a gramophone and chatter flowed out into the cold night air. He was a slight looking man, dressed without a collar and looked like he had just come in from outside. His hair was damp as if he had gone for a stroll along the seafront. He stopped dead when he saw the envelope in her hand.

'Is it, is it from the office?'

She could have lied. It would have been easy. Instead she simply handed it to him.

He looked at the postmark and brought the letter up to his chest.

'Why thank you miss.'

She turned and left, the salty wind blowing in her face.

## W112 — Essays and humanities / Printing history

Round 2; 556 words requested 500; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Write an essay explaining how inexpensive newspapers affected public discussion in nineteenth-century Britain. Discuss literacy, transport, advertising, and limits on access without treating printing as the only cause. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

The statement that 'Inexpensive newspapers were solely responsible for the widening of the public discussion' is partly true. Yes, newspapers had an impact on the increase in public discussion in the 19th century, but many other factors were also responsible.

Firstly, the increase in literacy amongst the working classes helped to increase the readership of newspapers. A greater proportion of the population had been exposed to some form of education during the 19th century, whether this was through Sunday school, adult education classes or through self improvement societies. The fact that more of the population could read by the end of the century than at the beginning, led to an increase in readership of cheap newspapers. However, the fact that literacy rates were still not high at the beginning of the 19th century, limited some people's access to newspapers. On the other hand though, many people would read to others who could not read for themselves, or share the paper with others, so illiteracy did not always exclude people from reading a newspaper.

Secondly, the improvements to the transportation network helped to make newspapers more accessible, as they could be distributed further and faster. From the beginning of the century, the railway network grew rapidly and helped to bring newspapers from London and other large centres of newspaper production out to the provinces. The improvements to the road network also helped people to receive newspapers quicker. However, in more remote areas of the country the lack of an efficient transportation network meant that distribution of newspapers was not as convenient. Improvements to the telegraph network from the middle of the 19th century also helped to distribute news quicker, though not actual newspapers themselves.

Also, the rise of advertisement helped to make newspapers more inexpensive, as this was a source of income for the newspaper. A greater number of people reading a newspaper was good for advertisers, who wanted to reach a large audience. In turn, the greater amount of advertisements in the newspaper allowed them to be sold more cheaply to members of the public, as the cost of the newspaper was covered by the income made from advertisements. However, the fact that income was needed from advertisement made the newspapers more commercial, and so could influence what was written in the newspaper. The repeal of the stamp duty on newspapers in 1855 and the paper duty in 1861 also helped to make the rise of the cheap press.

However, the fact that a newspaper still needed to be bought limited the access for some, especially for the poorest members of society. Also, the limited access that some people, such as women, had to education limited their access to newspapers and public discussion. Also, the fact that the newspaper often represented a specific point of view, and the fact that who owned the newspaper could influence what was written in it, limited the views that the newspaper gave on social and political issues. The official charges, such as the stamp duty and the paper duty, before they were scrapped, meant that newspapers were not always cheap. Also, the laws that were in place at the time limited some of what could be written in the newspaper.

Overall, inexpensive newspapers did help to increase the amount of public discussion, but many other factors also played a role.

## W116 — Essays and humanities / Language essay

Round 2; 276 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a short essay about what can be lost and gained when translating humor. Give a concrete invented example and explain the translator's tradeoff. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Humor is one of the trickiest things to deal with when translating from one language to another. This is due, in part, to the fact that a lot of humor depends on the sound of the words, the grammatical structure of a sentence, or even cultural aspects that may not exist in the target language. Thus, if the translator is too literal, the humor will be lost on the reader. On the other hand, if the translator modifies the text so that it becomes funny in the target language, it may not end up sounding the same as the original.

For example, let’s imagine that you are translating a comic from English to Spanish and come across the following: “Can you make dough?” “Well, I can make money, but I’ve never baked bread in my life.” The joke here relies on the fact that dough has two meanings in English: 1) a mixture of flour and water used for making bread and 2) an informal term for money. If you were to translate this literally into Spanish using the word masa , the reader would have no idea what you were talking about.

One possibility to deal with this would be to use the word pasta , which in some Spanish-speaking countries is used to refer to money: “Pasta, sí. Pero solo la que se come.” However, in doing so, the text may not sound as the original.

Thus, the translator must sometimes choose between staying faithful to the source text or staying faithful to the intent of the author. Sometimes, this means that the joke simply will not be as funny in the target language.

### Review

{"id": "W116", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Concrete dough/pasta example illustrates sound, cultural meaning, and fidelity tradeoffs; acknowledges regional usage. Adaptation changes the reply and needs clearer explanation of what is gained. 276/200 words substantially exceeds length; otherwise coherent."}

Being patient is allowing things to happen in their own time, without becoming frustrated. Being passive is giving up, not doing what you should be doing. I found the recipe in my mom's old book. At the top, in grandma's distinctive blue ink, it says "two cups of flour, a little salt, and just enough milk." Underneath that it says, "bake until it's ready."

That's it. No temperature, no time, no name. There's a stain of butter at the top and a little drawing of a star. I know she measured it like this when I was little and helped her in the kitchen. I could watch her work, but she never let me near the knife.

I tried to make it last Sunday. The dough was too wet at first, so I kept adding flour until, like grandma used to say, it felt like it knew where it was going. When it came out of the oven uneven and crisp on the edges, my mom took a bite and told me what it was.

We talked about it for a while, whether or not she put sugar in it, how she always seemed to make enough for whoever was around. The recipe doesn't tell me how much of anything to use, but between the lines, I can see how much room she made at her table.

## W121 — Science and technology / Climate explainer

Round 2; 523 words requested 500; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Explain the difference between the greenhouse effect, global warming, and urban heat islands for a general reader. Describe mechanisms and avoid implying that every hot day proves a global trend. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

WHAT is the Greenhouse Effect? The Greenhouse Effect is a process by which thermal radiation from a planet's atmosphere warms the planet's surface to a temperature above what it would be without its atmosphere. Solar radiation reaches Earth's surface and warms land and ocean waters. That energy is radiated back toward space as infrared radiation (heat). Some of this energy is absorbed by greenhouse gases in Earth's atmosphere, such as water vapor, carbon dioxide, and methane, among others. That energy is subsequently radiated in all directions. This process effectively reduces the rate at which Earth's atmosphere loses energy and causes it to be warmer than it would be in the absence of an atmosphere.

Human activities, such as burning coal, oil and gas, as well as deforestation and agriculture, are enhancing the natural greenhouse effect, leading to global warming.

WHAT is Global Warming? Global warming is the increase in average temperature of the Earth's surface and lower atmosphere since the mid-twentieth century, and is expected to continue. Global warming is caused primarily by the enhanced greenhouse effect due to the increase in greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. In common speech, "global warming" may also refer to observed increases in surface temperatures since the mid-twentieth century (as reflected in the land-ocean temperature index), and may even include longer-term temperature changes such as those occurring during the mid-twentieth century cooling and the preceding Little Ice Age. Although the entire troposphere is warming, the general term "global warming" is often used to refer to the warming of the land and ocean surface temperatures, which are most relevant to humans. Warming may cause other changes, such as increases in extreme heat events and a shift in the amounts and patterns of rainfall and drought, as well as changes in the amount of ice and snow stored in the mountains and on the land, and warming of the ocean.

WHAT is the Urban Heat Island Effect? The Urban Heat Island Effect is a region (usually urban) that is significantly warmer than its surroundings (usually rural). This phenomenon may be caused by human activity and the replacement of natural land cover with large areas of dark surfaces (such as roofs and asphalt) that absorb more sunlight during the day and radiate more heat than natural vegetation. The urban heat island also results from the reduced availability of water for evaporation in cities, since vegetation is removed and covered over with solid surfaces. Heat produced by vehicles, air conditioning, and industry also contributes to the urban heat island effect. In addition, cities often have a reduced air flow which prevents the dispersion of heat. The effect varies depending on weather conditions, land use, and urban form. It can be mitigated through the use of green roofs, parks, trees, and other cooling technologies.

HOW are these things related? The greenhouse effect is caused by gas. Global warming is caused by the greenhouse effect. Urban Heat Island Effect is caused by global warming.

HOW do we know it's not just a particularly hot day? It's similar to knowing that someone is sad versus them just being in a bad mood one day.

### Review

{"id": "W121", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "2", "development": "2", "notes": "Initial mechanisms are mostly relevant, but final summary falsely says urban heat islands are caused by global warming, contradicting its own land-cover explanation. Climate-versus-hot-day answer is an uninformative mood analogy; description of global warming includes cooling confusingly. Serious conceptual errors despite detailed opening."}

## W122 — Science and technology / Machine learning

Round 2; 593 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain training, validation, and test sets using a system that predicts bicycle demand. Discuss temporal leakage and why repeatedly consulting the test set can mislead evaluation. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Let's illustrate this with a case study. Let's say that you work for a bike sharing company and you'd like to predict the bike demand in each neighborhood for each hour of the day. You have historical information about the number of bikes rented, as well as information about the date, the meteorological conditions, holidays and so forth. In order to evaluate your system, you will split your data into 3 sets:

Training set: it's the set of data that you will use for training your model. This is the data that your model will use to "learn" the factors that influence the number of bikes requested. The parameters of your model will be adjusted to the data present in this set in order to minimize the error on this set (for example, by minimizing the difference between the actual number of bikes and the number predicted). You should also use this set to fit any transformation that you want to apply to your data (for example, to normalize your data).

Validation set: this is the set that you will use to make choices in your modeling. You will use it to choose the best algorithm, to determine the complexity of your model, to select the features that you want to use, etc. Your choices during the development of your model will be driven by the performance that you obtain on this set. Therefore, you are indirectly "adjusting" your model to this set, which will give you a slightly optimistic vision of the performance of your system.

Test set: this is the set that you will use to evaluate your model at the end of your development phase. You should use it to give a final estimation of the performance of your system. In the case of forecasting the bike demand, it is preferable to separate your data temporally, with the oldest data being used for training, more recent data for the validation set, and the most recent data for the test set. This way, you will be closer to the real problem that you want to solve (using the past to forecast the future). Depending on the problem, you can also use several time periods in a row to evaluate your system (for example, train on the past and forecast the next period, then train on the past and forecast the next period, etc.)

Watch out for temporal leakages: If you separate your data randomly, you might end up with a situation where your training set contains data from a Tuesday, and your test set contains data from the next Wednesday. Those two periods are very close to each other, so the conditions will be almost duplicated between your training and test sets. This will also be true for your validation set. Another thing to watch out for is including a feature that takes information from the future (for example, the number of bikes requested for the day if you are forecasting for a particular hour of the day, or a rolling average that takes the next periods into consideration). Finally, as I mentioned above, you should only use your training set to fit the transformations that you apply to your data.

Avoid using the test set iteratively: As you are developing your system, use your validation set to evaluate your choices. Only use your test set when your development is finished, as each evaluation and modification based on your test set will transform it into a validation set and will give you an optimistic estimation of the performance of your system.

### Review

{"id": "W122", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Good separation of fitting, model selection, final evaluation, future-feature leakage, and training-only preprocessing. Tuesday-training/Wednesday-test proximity is not inherently leakage, so that example overstates temporal dependence; otherwise clear and specific."}

## W123 — Science and technology / Biology

Round 2; 356 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how vaccination can create immune memory. Distinguish antibodies from memory cells and avoid suggesting that every vaccine prevents every infection. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Vaccines work by exposing your immune system to something that your immune system can use to learn what a target germ looks like. It could be a weakened germ, killed germ, part of a germ, or something that instructs some of your body’s cells to make a protein that the germ makes.

When your immune system sees this, immune cells work together to create a immune response. Some B cells turn into plasma cells, which make antibodies. Antibodies are proteins that target and bind to certain things. Antibodies can neutralize germs by blocking them from getting into your cells, flag them to be destroyed by other immune cells, and do other things. Antibodies are not memory cells, and eventually the amount of antibodies in your blood will go down (although some plasma cells can stick around for a long time and keep making antibodies).

Other B cells turn into memory B cells, and some T cells also turn into memory T cells. Now, if your body sees that germ again, the memory cells can turn into other immune cells that respond faster and stronger to the germ. For example, the memory B cells can turn into new cells that make antibodies to target the germ, and the memory T cells can help other immune cells target the germ or help destroy your own cells that the germ has gotten into.

This faster immune response can often stop you from getting sick, or make you less sick and not as long if you do get sick. Some vaccines are really good at stopping you from getting sick, others are really good at stopping you from getting severely ill but less effective at stopping you from getting infected/spreading the germ, and others are really good at stopping you from getting infected but not always, etc. That is why sometimes you need a booster shot, to remind your immune system what the germ looks like.

Antibodies are more the “action” part of your immune system (although they are not cells), and memory cells last a long time to help your immune system respond faster to things in the future.

### Review

Repetitive germ phrasing and a rambling effectiveness paragraph reduce precision; booster explanation is simplified. Central mechanisms checked against CDC."}

## W124 — Science and technology / Astronomy

Round 2; 290 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why Earth has seasons and why the northern and southern hemispheres have opposite seasons. Address the common misconception about distance from the Sun. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

The seasons are caused by the 23.5º tilt of the earth.

As the earth revolves around the sun, sometimes the northern hemisphere is tilted towards the sun. When this happens, sunlight hits that side of the earth more directly (it also has more hours of daylight during that time of the year). This makes it warmer on that side of the earth. Six months later, the southern hemisphere is tilted towards the sun, and now it receives the majority of the sun's light and energy, and it is warmer there. When a hemisphere is pointed away from the sun, it receives less intense radiation from the sun (it also has less hours of daylight during that time of the year), and it is colder. This causes winter. In between these two times is spring and autumn.

When it is summer on one side of the earth, it is winter on the other side. This is why in June, it is summer in the United States, but winter in Australia. In December, it is winter in the United States, but summer in Australia. In March and September, both sides of the earth are equally as far from the sun, so the day and night lengths are the same.

Something that many people believe is that the seasons are caused by the eliptical orbit that the earth travels. At certain times of the year, the earth is farther from the sun than it is at other times. However, the seasons are determined not by how far the earth is from the sun, but by the tilt of the earth. At any one time, the northern and southern hemispheres are the same distance from the sun, however they are usually in different seasons.

### Review

{"id": "W124", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Correct central tilt/daylight mechanism and opposite-hemisphere examples. Incorrectly explains equinox day lengths through equal distance to the Sun, then claims hemispheres always have the same distance. Those geometry shortcuts undermine the requested misconception correction; NASA check recorded."}

## W125 — Science and technology / Physics

Round 2; 215 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why a thick blanket can keep a person warm without generating heat. Discuss insulation and clarify why wrapping an unheated object does not automatically make it hotter. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

A blanket will keep you warm by inhibiting heat loss. Your body generates a certain amount of heat as part of its metabolism. If there was nothing inhibiting heat transfer then this heat would escape your body and you would be at the same temperature as your surroundings. By placing a blanket over yourself you trap a layer of air close to your body which cannot circulate freely. Air is a relatively poor conductor of heat and so by having a layer of air which can not convect the rate at which heat is lost from your body is reduced. The blanket also reduces the amount of radiant heat lost from your body.

The blanket itself does not heat you, you remain at roughly the same temperature. It simply stops your body heat from escaping.

If you wrap something which is not generating heat in a blanket it will not heat up. The blanket is not a source of energy. If the object was cooler than its surroundings it would heat up more slowly than it would have done had it not been wrapped in a blanket. Likewise if it was warmer than its surroundings it would cool down more slowly. Nothing heats up unless there is a source of energy to heat it up.

### Review

{"id": "W125", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Insulation and cooler-object warming are explained well, but claim that an unblanketed metabolizing person would equal ambient temperature ignores heat balance. Says heat loss is stopped rather than reduced and initially says an unheated object will not warm before qualifying it. Useful core with overabsolute statements."}

## W126 — Science and technology / Statistics

Round 2; 255 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why a correlation between umbrella use and traffic delays does not prove umbrellas cause delays. Discuss a plausible confounder and what additional evidence would help. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

While there may be a corrolation between umbrella use and traffic delays, it doesn't mean that there is a causal relationship. It could very well be that a third variable, not umbrella use, causes both the increase in umbrella use and the traffic delays. For example, rain. It could be that rain makes people use umbrellas, and the rain makes it harder to see, makes the roads more slippery and makes people drive more careful, leading to traffic delays.

Of course there are plenty of other possibilities as well.

If we want to actually test for a causal link between umbrella use and traffic delays, we would need to find two places (or two time periods in the same place) where all of the variables are the same except for the amount of umbrella use. If in both places it rained equally much, if the traffic was equally high, if the roads were in equal shape, if there were an equal amount of car collisions and road closures due to other reasons, etc., but in one place more umbrellas were used than in the other, and only then we could see whether umbrella use makes a difference. Of course this would need to be done with a large amount of data, from many places and days. Better would be an actual controlled experiment, but that would probably be too difficult to do without causing more traffic delays itself. Until then we can't conclude anything from the mere correlation, other than that there is a correlation.

### Review

{"id": "W126", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Rain confounder and matched comparisons clearly explained with experimental alternative. Equalizing every variable is unrealistic and needs a caveat about residual confounding; misspelling and long sentence reduce polish. 255/200 words exceeds tolerance."}

## W127 — Science and technology / Computing

Round 2; 176 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain a database index using a library analogy. Include one way the analogy is limited and one cost of maintaining indexes. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

A database index is like a library catalog. Without the catalog, you would have to walk around the library and check every book to find the one you want. With the catalog, you can look for the title or author and be directed to a reference where the book can be found, allowing you to walk straight to the shelf on which it sits. A database index has a similar effect; it stores some of the information from a table, along with pointers to the rows from which the information was taken.

Note that there are some differences between a database index and a library catalog; specifically, a database will use indexes automatically, and only for certain queries.

Indexes also need to be updated whenever the data they index is changed, and can take up a significant amount of storage space. Because of this, using too many indexes can slow down the updating process and use a large amount of diskspace. For this reason, indexes should only be used when needed to speed up frequent queries.

### Review

## W128 — Science and technology / Ecology

Round 2; 213 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why restoring a wetland involves more than planting vegetation. Mention water flow, species, maintenance, and how success might be observed over time. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

The next step is the actual restoration of the wetland. There are a number of things that are required at this stage. First, the water needs to be brought in to the wetland. The water levels need to be correct, and the right amount of water must flow through the wetland at the correct times of the year. This may mean restoring the natural water channels that were there before the wetland was drained, or removing barriers to water flow. It may also mean changing the drainage on the land so that it floods at the right time of year, and does not dry out or flood all of the time.

Secondly, the correct species of plants need to be planted. These need to be native species, suited to the local environment, and also suited to the type of wildlife you want to attract to the wetland.

Thirdly, the wetland will need to be maintained, to keep it healthy and viable.

Finally, the project needs to be monitored, to see if it has been a success or not. Evidence of success will include the wetland staying in good condition, and improvements in the local environment, including increased wildlife, better water quality, and a reduction in the amount of invasive species in the area.

## W129 — Science and technology / Research design

Round 2; 396 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Design a small study testing whether reminder postcards increase attendance at a community workshop. Explain allocation, outcome measurement, consent or privacy considerations, and a limitation. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Getting started: Contacting potential participants Contact people who register for a community-based workshop, indicating that they are ok to be contacted. Inform them that you will be testing whether a reminder postcard increases their likelihood of attending, and that they do not have to take part if they prefer not to. Tell them that all registrants will receive the usual confirmation of registration and information about the event.

Randomly assign each person to one of two groups. The simplest method is to use a computer to generate a random list. Several days prior to the event, send one group of registrants a postcard with a brief reminder about the event. The second group of registrants does not receive a postcard, but will receive the usual information about the event. If the number of registrants is large, and they vary by where they live or when they registered, it may be best to randomly assign people within each location and registration date. The postcard should simply include information about the event, such as date, time, place, and a contact person.

Determining whether participants attended: The most direct way to measure the impact of the reminder postcard is to compare the proportion of registrants who attended the event in each group. Event staff can record which registrants attend, either by name from a registration list or by a registration code. Even if participants in the postcard group do not receive or see their postcard, include them in the group to which they were randomly assigned. It is useful, however, to know the number of undeliverable postcards. Although a small pilot project may not be able to reliably detect a small impact of the postcards, it can at least provide some indication of whether a larger project would be worthwhile.

Protecting participant privacy Collect and store only the information you need for the project. Keep the list of contacts separate from the information on who attended the event, limit access to the information, and eliminate it when it is no longer needed. Give people the opportunity to opt out of future mailings. Avoid including any personal information on the postcards, since others may see them.

Limitations of this approach This approach only includes people who registered for the event and agreed to be contacted. These people may differ from the general population in how they respond to a postcard.

### Review

## W130 — Science and technology / Scientific uncertainty

Round 2; 278 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain why two well-conducted studies might obtain different estimates of the same effect. Distinguish sampling variation from differences in populations or methods. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

It could simply be due to chance. The two studies provide different estimates of the effect. The estimate of the effect is not the same as the effect itself; it is based on the particular set of patients (or other units) in the study. If we could repeatedly sample from the population of interest and conduct studies in exactly the same way, the estimates of effect would vary slightly from study to study. This is called sampling variation. It is reduced by increasing the sample size but cannot be eliminated entirely. By considering the confidence intervals , we can get an idea of the range of estimates that are reasonably likely, given the data from each study.

There may be other explanations for the differences between the two studies. The two studies may have sampled from different populations. For example, the patients in the two studies may have been of different ages or had different risk factors for the condition of interest, and the effect of the intervention may differ for these different groups of people.

The methods used in the two studies may have differed in important ways. The outcome may have been measured differently, for example. Other aspects of the studies may have been different, such as the way in which the intervention was given, or other methodological features of the study.

Think about how the study results compare, both in terms of the estimates of effect and the uncertainty around them. If they differ more than you would expect due to sampling variation, think about whether there is a sensible explanation for the difference in terms of differences between the study populations or methodological differences.

### Review

{"id": "W130", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Clearly separates sampling variation, population heterogeneity, and methodological differences. Confidence intervals described loosely as likely estimates rather than parameter uncertainty under assumptions. Repetitive closing and 278/200 words exceed length request."}

## W131 — Business and workplace / Strategy memo

Round 2; 465 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a memo for a bicycle repair shop deciding whether to offer mobile repairs or extend weekend opening. Propose small reversible tests, relevant costs, and decision criteria without inventing market-size statistics. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

What would you do? Here's what I'd do

If you have a vehicle and available staff you could try running a limited mobile service for a few weeks. Limit the service area and the type of work, maybe limit it to one or two days and a limited number of jobs that need to be booked in advance with a deposit to limit no shows. Limit the distance you are prepared to travel and make sure you have the parts available to limit repeat visits. Keep detailed notes of enquires, jobs booked, jobs done, jobs cancelled, travel time, job time, parts availability, repeat visits etc etc. Then work out the cost of running the service including staff time, mileage, parking, vehicle costs, tools, consumables, payment costs etc and decide if it was worth doing.

Similarly you can extend your opening hours for a limited time on a rota basis without incurring overtime and without affecting normal shop opening. Advertise the extended hours and keep a record of the business generated, did it capture people who would have come in during normal hours or were they additional? How much work was done, how much was sold, how much work was taken on but not done, what were the incremental staff costs, employer costs, utility costs, payment costs, security costs etc. Then decide if it was a useful exercise or not.

The key thing is decide before you start what the outcome has to be to justify continuing and how much you are prepared to spend on finding out. Work on incremental contribution from both labour and parts rather than just revenue. Compare and contrast the two on a number of basis, incremental contribution, incremental contribution per staff hour, your time, managers time, customer satisfaction, repeat business, disruption to normal running etc. Don't forget to differentiate between one off costs and running costs.

The key thing is to keep it small scale and reversible, you don't need to buy a van or sign a lease or commit to any changes until you know it is worth doing. If possible do them separately, if you do them together make sure you advertise them separately and use different booking codes so you know which business came from which source. Also make sure you allow for seasonality, weather, holidays and any other influences that might affect your normal business.

If at the end of the day each trial generates a positive incremental contribution at a level you can support and there is evidence to suggest that there is enough on going business from satisfied customers then you can make a decision to continue, if not pull out whilst you still can. Remember its better to know now than wait until you have committed to something that you cannot reverse easily.

### Review

{"id": "W131", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Useful reversible trials, displacement checks, incremental contribution, and precommitted thresholds. Informal advice rather than memo, repeated limit phrasing, and unsubstantiated assumption that extending hours avoids overtime without affecting normal coverage."}

## W132 — Business and workplace / Project proposal

Round 2; 524 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a six-week proposal for replacing a small charity's shared filing system. Include roles, migration checks, access permissions, training, measures of success, and risks. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Moving to a new system The simplest solution to the problem is to move to a secure document management system, which can be accessed from anywhere. This should be done in a methodical way, with a small project team, including a sponsor (trustee or senior manager), project coordinator (senior manager with responsibility for operations), technical lead (system administrator or external supplier) and representatives from each team within the charity. The charity’s data protection lead should also be involved. Although it is important to involve staff in identifying their needs, there should be only one person making the final decisions about the folder structure and who has access to what.

Week 1 The project coordinator should review the current folder structure, to identify who ‘owns’ each folder, who needs access, the types of documents involved and any current issues. They should also identify which documents need to be kept, particularly any personal, financial or safeguarding data. Week 2 Research and select a new system. There are many document management systems available, so you should identify what you need and then look for one or two that fit that need. You should choose a system that provides security, including individual user accounts, multifactor authentication, version control, audit logs, good recovery facilities and encryption. The project team should then decide on the folder structure, file naming convention, document retention period and how staff should request access to the new system.

Week 3 The technical lead should set up the system and test it. They should set up the appropriate permissions, so that each member of staff has access only to the documents they need. For example, finance records should be accessed only by the finance team and safeguarding records only by those specifically designated. The technical lead should set up individual user accounts with multifactor authentication and not allow the use of common accounts. They should also set appropriate permissions for each user, and not allow files to be shared with external users unless appropriate.

Week 4 Migration should take place in a series of batches, starting with the most organised and actively used folders. Files should be pruned at the same time, with duplicates and out of date files removed (this should be done in conjunction with the data protection officer). The original system should be made read only and kept for reference during the migration. Care should be taken to make sure that the number and size of files is the same on both systems, and that the appropriate permissions are in place. A full record of the migration should be kept.

Week 5 Training should be provided to all staff on the new system. This can be done in a series of short sessions, tailored to each group of staff and supported by a one-page guide to the most common operations. Week 6 Once the migration is complete, the original system can be retired, after a suitable period with it held for reference as a read-only system.

Consider how you will measure the success of the move to the new system. What could go wrong and what can you do to mitigate any risks?

### Review

Ends by asking for success measures and risks instead of providing them. Simultaneous pruning conflicts with unchanged file-count/size checks unless reconciliation is explained; retirement timing vague."}

## W133 — Business and workplace / Incident report

Round 2; 424 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional incident report about duplicate appointment reminders sent during a software update. Give a consistent timeline, distinguish confirmed facts from hypotheses, and describe corrective actions. No appointments were lost. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

On the 14th April 2025, Northstar Clinic’s scheduling service (this is a made up name for the purpose of this example) experienced an issue where some patients received duplicate appointment reminder messages during a scheduled software update.

At 08:00 our engineers started a scheduled software update of our patient reminder service. At 08:17 the service had completed its restart, and began processing the queue of patient reminder messages it needed to send out that morning. Between 08:19 and 08:36 some of our patients received two appointment reminder text messages, usually within two minutes of each other. At 08:41 our support agent identified that some patients had received multiple reminder messages. At 08:48 our operations team suspended all SMS reminder messages. At 09:12 our engineers had completed rolling back to the previous version of the service, and began processing the reminder message queue again after verifying its integrity. At 10:05 we completed our reconciliation of all appointments with your appointment management system, and have confirmed that all appointments are still scheduled correctly.

Our logs indicate that the software update caused the service to retry sending part of its message queue, without recording that it had already sent those messages. We are still investigating why it didn’t record that the messages were sent. Our engineers believe it may have been caused by a timing conflict with the database connection being reset, but we have not yet confirmed this. There is no indication that any appointment or contact information was changed by this issue.

We have sent, or will send by 11:30 today, a follow up text message to the affected patients to explain what happened, and confirm that their appointments are unchanged. We have provided your support staff with a script they can use to respond to your patients. We’ve also asked your clinic locations to check in with their patients to confirm their appointments are still correct. No action is required from your patients.

We have made a change to our service to stop it from retrying messages when it restarts. We are also implementing additional checks to make sure that it compares the message queue to its record of which messages it has sent before it sends messages again. We are currently testing some other changes, which will make our software update process more gradual, and alert us if the service does send duplicate messages in the future. Our engineers will complete their root cause analysis by the 18th, at which time we may have a better understanding of what exactly caused the issue.

### Review

{"id": "W133", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Coherent timeline and clear confirmed-versus-hypothesized cause; appointments preserved. Retry-disable fix needs assurance that legitimate retries will not be lost; client voice shifts. 424/300 words substantially exceeds target."}

## W139 — Business and workplace / Procurement analysis

When to rent or buy a projector For a small organization that is unsure of how many events they are likely to put on in the future, the flexibility of being able to just rent a projector for an event, and not having to commit to buying one, can be a good idea. On the other hand, buying a projector may be cheaper in the long run. To compare the two options you need to think about all the costs involved in buying a projector, including any accessories you'll need, maintenance and repair, replacement, and any financing or insurance you decide to take out. When comparing the cost of renting a projector you may need to consider the cost of having it delivered and picked up or setting it up, or even the cost of extending the rental period or of getting a projector at short notice.

A projector hire company may be able to provide a reliable service, but there is always the risk that they may not have the type of projector you want when you need it, or they may be delayed delivering it. On the other hand, if you have your own projector it will always be there when you want it, but you'll be responsible for making sure it is in good working order.

You may be able to get good technical support from whoever you hire a projector from, but it's something you should check out. If you buy a projector you'll need to consider the warranty and how easy it will be to get it fixed if something goes wrong, and how long it is likely to last.

If you hire a projector you don't need to worry about where you are going to store it. If you buy a projector you'll need somewhere safe to put it.

To decide whether to rent or buy a projector you need to consider how often you think you'll need one in the future, compare this with quotes you get for hiring one, and think about all the other costs of owning a projector. You'll also need to think about what type of projector you need, how big an image you need to project, how bright it needs to be, where you'll be projecting, how you'll transport the projector, who'll be using it, and what will happen if it breaks down. If you're very unsure of how often you'll need a projector in the future, it may be best to hire one for the time being until you have a better idea.

Those items must be labeled with the name of the event, the name of the person putting them in, and the date they will be removed. Event items need to be removed promptly after the event is over.

Please note that there will be a consistent reminder process in place before removing food. On Friday mornings a reminder will be posted next to the refrigerator and to the individuals who have food in there, if possible. On Friday afternoon, any food that is still there will be labeled with the date and put into the other location (if we have one). That food will have until the next business day to be picked up, at which point it will be thrown out if it isn’t picked up or if it has gone bad. If food is in there and we aren’t sure who it belongs to, we will post a notice before throwing it away if at all possible. If you need more time or you are storing food for an approved event, please contact the kitchen coordinator.

## W141 — Journalism and commentary / Reported feature

Round 2; 590 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a clearly labeled fictional feature about a neighborhood clothing swap held each season. Include concrete scenes, named fictional participants, practical benefits, and logistical limitations without claiming measured environmental savings. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

This is a fictional feature

On a Saturday morning, a crowd of individuals from a housing community gathered to participate in a clothing swap. People were sorting, hanging up and labelling their clothing donations for others to peruse. The different articles of clothing were sorted into categories. There were tables to lay out the clothing on, there were baskets for scarves and other small items and there was a small area for people to put their shoes.

The event was organized and run by the residents of the housing community and it was held four times a year to coincide with the changes in seasons. People were able to bring in clothing that they had outgrown or no longer needed and in exchange they could take home whatever they liked from the clothing that others had brought. No money was exchanged at the swap and people were not required to bring anything if they did not wish to. There were some simple guidelines for what could be brought to the swap. Clothing could not be stained or broken and underwear was not permitted. People were only allowed to take what they needed.

Participants at the swap seemed happy with what they managed to find. ``I needed a new cardigan for my daughter'' explained one woman ``her old one had become too small and this will last her until the next swap.'' A retired elderly woman was trying on a jacket and deciding whether or not she wanted to keep it. ``I'm not sure if this is my style yet'' she said with a smile ``but now that I've tried it on, I know that it is.''

People attended the swap for a variety of reasons. Some needed clothing for themselves or for their children. New residents attending their first swap often needed a coat to help them through the upcoming season. Other participants brought in clothing to be rid of it or to simply see what was available. Through the swap, people were able to meet their neighbours and create new friendships within the community.

The swap was a small affair with a limited amount of racks for people to hang their clothing on. Often, certain items such as winter coats and clothing in popular sizes would be taken very quickly while other items such as formal wear and clothing in unusual sizes would not be taken at all. Clothing that was not taken at the swap could not be kept for the next one. Often the organizers of the swap would contact a shelter to see if they wanted any of the clothing. Any clothing that the shelter did not want would be brought to a collection point and any clothing that could not be collected would be disposed of.

As the swap was a very small operation, it was subject to a number of limitations. There were a limited number of resources available for the swap and it was not uncommon for something to go wrong with the event. In the past, there have been problems such as volunteers falling ill or the venue leaking. However, the organizers of the swap have managed to overcome these problems and make the swap a success despite the limitations that it has.

The participants at the clothing swap seemed very happy with how the event went and they are already looking forward to the next one. No matter what the clothing swap may have for them next time, it is sure that they will walk away with something that they need.

### Review

Repetitive event description, awkward quotation reversal, and final certainty everyone finds needed clothing conflict with size shortages."}

## W142 — Journalism and commentary / Investigative explainer

Round 2; 530 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Explain how changing the denominator can make the same public-service performance look better or worse. Use a clearly hypothetical numerical example, show both calculations, and distinguish misleading presentation from evidence of misconduct. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

It’s all in the denominator How can two different performance rates for the same service both be correct? Let’s start with an obvious point, a performance rate is a fraction, it has a numerator and a denominator. The numerator is the number of cases meeting the performance target. The denominator is the number of cases you want to measure the performance for.

Example Let’s take an example in a hypothetical call centre serving the public. In a given month, 100 people ring the call centre for assistance, and 90 of them get connected to an agent within five minutes. The remaining 10 people get impatient and hang up without getting through to an agent.

If you define the denominator as the number of people calling in to the call centre, you can report that 90% of callers get through to an agent within 5 minutes.

But if you define the denominator as the number of calls answered by the call centre, you can report that 100% of calls are answered within five minutes. In both cases, the numerator is 90.

Is one of these performance measures incorrect? Or is one of them misleading? You could argue that the second performance measure makes the call centre look better than it is, and therefore it could be considered misleading.

However, there are many legitimate reasons why the denominator might be different from the total number of cases a service receives. Perhaps some cases are duplicate records which need to be excluded. Perhaps some cases are outside the authority’s jurisdiction. Perhaps some applications are withdrawn by the applicant before they can be processed. The important thing is that whatever the definition of the denominator, it should be defined ahead of time, applied consistently, and disclosed to the public. And when the denominator changes, that needs to be disclosed as well, so the public know that performance in the previous year isn’t directly comparable to the performance in the current year.

Probably the best approach would be to disclose both performance measures – “90% of callers get through to an agent within five minutes, and 100% of calls answered by our call centre are answered within five minutes.” You could also report the number of calls that were abandoned, and give some definitions to clarify how you’re calculating the performance measures. Generally it’s useful to report counts along with performance measures, as it gives the public some additional information to help interpret what the performance measures mean.

Finally, is it wrong to choose the denominator that makes your service look the best? That’s a hard question to answer. It may simply be an error, or it may be a reasonable technical definition that you’re applying, or it may be that your industry has a standard definition for the denominator that you’re following. On the other hand, if you’ve deliberately changed the definition of the denominator to hide the fact that your performance has slipped, or if you’re not disclosing important exclusions from the denominator, or if you’re not applying the definition consistently, or if you’re claiming an improvement in performance that isn’t supported by the underlying data – then yes, that would be wrong.

### Review

{"id": "W142", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Hypothetical 90-of-100 versus 90-of-90 example is correct and interpretation distinguishes exclusions from misconduct. Useful consistent-definition and disclosure advice. Collapsed headings and some repetition reduce polish; division is verbal rather than displayed."}

## W144 — Journalism and commentary / Profile

Round 2; 305 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional profile of a watch repairer teaching an apprentice. Use specific observed actions and a moment of uncertainty; avoid portraying the subject as flawless or inventing awards. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Every morning at nine, she unlocks the door.

`` No, Joel, not like that. Slower... yes, just like that.'' She watches the boys young hands carefully remove the back of the clock. `` Good, that's a start. Now, watch here.'' She carefully removes her own tool and shows him how its done. `` See? You have to be careful, not too fast, but steady. It has to be straight.'' She removes the back with a practiced pop and sets it aside.

Over the years, she's taught the boy a thing or two. He's a quick study, very careful with his work. He picks things up easily, and learns her little techniques. `` Now be careful with these, they go everywhere.'' She laughs as he drops one of the tiny screws, and they both scramble to find it under the bench, moving baskets and tools out of the way until they spot it gleaming on the floor.

Its not always easy, though. Sometimes, even she has trouble. `` Hmm.. what could be wrong with this one?'' She pauses, staring at the tiny innards of the wristwatch. `` It cant be that... no, theres something there, but Im not sure what yet. Lets have a look together, hmm?'' Carefully, she studies it alongside him, letting him fix it when they find the problem. `` Thats it! Great job, Joel!''

Every night when they close, he winds the clock above the door. Every night, she shrugs when he points out that its not quite right. `` Well, we'll see about that tomorrow.''

## W145 — Journalism and commentary / Editorial

Round 2; 282 words requested 200; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Write an editorial arguing that a council should explain changes to recycling collections clearly. Address residents without internet access and suggest a way to assess whether the explanation worked. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

To ensure that everyone is informed about changes to the recycling collection service, the council needs to communicate effectively. Without effective communication, people may be confused and put their recyclable materials into the general rubbish or their recycling may be rejected. Either of these outcomes would have a negative impact on the council’s ability to meet its environmental objectives.

To communicate effectively, the council should explain clearly (and in plain English) what changes are being made to the recycling collection service, with plenty of examples. This information should be available on the council’s website, however not everyone has access to the internet so it is important that the council uses a range of communication methods to make sure that everyone is informed. The council could print and distribute leaflets to all houses in the area, include the information on the recycling collection calendar, put up posters at the local library and community centre and provide a phone helpline for people to call if they have any questions. It would also be a good idea to provide this information in a variety of formats and languages to make sure that it is accessible to everyone.

The council should also find out whether their attempts to communicate have been effective. They could conduct a postal, telephone or online survey of a representative sample of residents to find out if people know about the changes. They could also look at the number of missed recycling collections and the rate of contamination and compare these figures to previous years.

If it appears that people are still confused about the recycling collection service, the council will need to try communicating the message again in a clearer way.

## W149 — Journalism and commentary / Interview Q&A

Round 2; 343 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional interview with a community radio producer about local reporting. Include a challenging question about editorial independence and a substantive answer. Keep questions and answers natural. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

How do you stay independent when your sponsors are the people you’re reporting on? Me and a fellow journalist at Harbor FM have been putting out a local news show for the past six years, and I get a lot of tips from the community. It can be anything from local council, local schools, health, etc. I think this is important because even the small things can affect a community.

Is there any way of verifying the tips so that I know they’ll be good for a show? I try to get multiple sources and make sure that I’m getting all sides of a story. It doesn’t always turn into a story though.

The kicker is, our show is sponsored by some local businesses and our local council. How do I make sure that our audience knows that we’re independent and not influenced by our sponsors? It's okay to be upfront with your audience that there may be pressure to not report on certain things because of your sponsors. In my opinion, it's your job as a journalist to not give in to that pressure. I had a similar experience where a business that was regularly advertising on our show asked me to drop a story that I had done about unsafe work conditions at one of their sites. I refused and they stopped advertising with us, and while that put a dent in our budget, I think it would have hurt us long-term to change the story and lose the trust of our audience.

That said, it can be really tricky when one of your major sponsors is also one of the main things you report on, like in your case with the local council. It might be helpful to find other ways to fund your show so that you aren't reliant on them, and also be very transparent with your audience about the relationship.

One of the hardest things about reporting locally is that you’re often going to run into your sources at the grocery store. Be kind, but not timid!

## W150 — Journalism and commentary / Opinion column

Round 2; 206 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a nuanced opinion column about turning every walk into a fitness target. Acknowledge the appeal of tracking while defending room for unmeasured enjoyment; avoid claiming tracking harms everyone. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

There's something enjoyable about seeing how far you've walked. Monitoring the number of steps you take can be good to set goals, to look back at your weekly graph and see that you're doing well, or for general motivation and competition.

However, the times you take longer to walk home because you're out with your dog and you're both having a nice time sniffing things; the walks you take where you choose a different route and see something new; the times you take a stroll with a friend -- do these need to be quantified? Does enjoying a walk mean that it needs to be measured? Sometimes, quantifying a walk makes it into a task rather than an enjoyment.

This isn't to say that you shouldn't use fitness apps and enjoy monitoring how much you walk. Rather, it's nice to have the choice: sometimes you can enjoy a nice, brisk walk and feeling satisfied at how far you've gone; other times you can enjoy a walk simply for how nice it is, without feeling the need to measure it.

While our technological devices can measure how far we've walked, they can't measure how much we enjoy a walk. It's good to enjoy walking for both reasons!

## W151 — Personal communication / Travel narrative

Round 2; 572 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional first-person narrative about missing a ferry and spending an unexpected afternoon in a harbor town. Include a specific conversation, a practical adjustment, and mixed feelings rather than a perfect travel lesson. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

As I round the corner and sprint down the final hill towards the ferry I see it pulling away from the dock. I skid to a halt, the force stopping my momentum, my backpack thuds against my spine as I turn to face the direction I came from. I watch the people on board wave to the man at the pier, he waves back and then notices me standing there watching him. A sorry smile on his face as he waves a hand in my direction to signify *'yeah sorry about that'*.

'When is the next one?' I ask

'4'o clock' He says pointing to a chart behind the counter.

'Fuck! Is there any way to get on earlier? Maybe you know someone who owns a boat?' I say desperately

'Well if you know someone who owns a tugboat, I'd love a ride on it too, but I'm afraid these things run on a schedule, bud'

'Yeah, schedule...right' I say in a defeated tone, a laugh escaping my lips, reluctantly I walk up to the window and buy a ticket for 4'o clock.

Frowning at my ruined plans for the day I turn and head into the town to while away the time until the next boat. It's only 10 in the morning and I had planned on being on the bus heading back home by now.

The town is quite nice, I eat in a little cafe by the harbour, lots of cute shops and fishing nets piled up in corners. The lady who owns the cafe is quite nice, very no nonsense, I enjoy chatting to her about my sandwich, which surprisingly is amazing, the cheese in it makes all the difference.

'Are you waiting for someone?' She asks wiping down a table.

'Why do you ask?'

'Because you look like someone who is waiting for someone. And you know what they're like? Never turn up when you're waiting for them.'

I chuckle at her comment. 'Actually I am waiting for something, I missed the ferry to the island and the next one isn't until 4'o clock'

'Sorry to hear that, they're always late that one so you may have a longer wait than you think.' She says with a wink.

I finish my lunch and head out again, the weather has turned a bit and its started to rain, my phone is almost out of juice and I don't dare go out walking along the coast as it's quite steep and the weather doesn't look like it's going to improve. I do some last minute shopping and find somewhere to charge my phone up, in the end I end up in the local library, which is nice, I manage to plug in and read a book for a while. It's always good to adapt your plans if they don't quite go to plan, you have to be able to change with the flow.

Eventually the rain clears up and I go for a short walk along the pier and watch a few kids flying kites, it's nice.

At 4 I hop on the ferry and head back, I'm glad to be on my way again, although I am still a bit annoyed that my plans have been disrupted, but on the whole I'm glad I got to see a bit more of the harbour town, although I'm glad I won't be coming back here and waiting all day again.

### Review

Pervasive run-ons and repeated nice/glad language; direction and return-home logistics unclear, with unrequested generalized travel lesson."}

## W152 — Personal communication / Personal statement

Round 2; 450 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional personal statement for a conservation training program from someone who volunteered cataloging a local archive. Show a concrete mistake, how they corrected it, and what they still need to learn. Do not invent prestigious awards. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Why am I interested in archiving? Why do I want to take archiving more seriously? I ask myself these questions often. When I first volunteered at the Cedar Street Historical Archive my intentions were pure, I wanted to help preserve the history of my town. I thought I would be reading a lot of old letters, but, as time has gone on, I have come to understand that without proper cataloging, the entire collection would be useless.

I was primarily tasked with cataloging a box of photographs that had been donated by a former local newspaper. I wrote down all the information I could find about each photograph, such as a title, date, location, and condition, and input it into the archives database. I had a lot of material to go off of as each photograph had come in an envelope with information scrawled on it, as well as a handwritten inventory of the entire collection. As I was cataloging the photographs I made an error. I cataloged a photograph based only on the information that had been written on the envelope and not on the inventory.

A week later, while I was helping a researcher who was interested in photographs of when the towns town hall was renovated in the early sixties, I noticed a photograph that seemed to match what they were looking for. It was the photograph that I had previously mis-cataloged. After looking at the envelope again I noticed that it had been reused and that underneath the more recent writing there was an older piece of an envelope attached that I had not noticed before.

I immediately notified the archivist that I had been working with and we amended the record of that photograph to make it more vague so that it would not mislead any future researchers. After I had made this mistake I realized how important it is to be very thorough and careful when it comes to cataloging. If what is written in the archives database is incorrect, then it can have a detrimental impact on the integrity of the collection.

While I think that I have a fairly good understanding of what goes on in an archive, I know that I have much to learn when it comes to conservation. I feel that I am not very knowledgeable when it comes to how to best preserve an object, or what factors are most influential when considering an objects stability. This is something that I am very interested in learning more about, and know that I must become more educated in if I want to pursue a career in archiving.

I think that I can be a valuable asset to the field

### Review

Correction only makes record vague without explaining evidence/provenance retention; repeated rhetorical framing, grammar errors, and weak abrupt final claim reduce effectiveness."}

## W153 — Personal communication / Learning reflection

Round 2; 343 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a first-person reflection on learning to bake bread after several failed attempts. Describe a specific adjustment and distinguish improved skill from simply feeling more confident. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

I used to struggle with bread for a long time. No matter what I did, my loaves would turn out dense with a pale crust and a gummy center. Every time this happened, I would blame some variable, like the flour or my oven or even the weather, and I'd try to change something when I baked the next loaf. But the next loaf would often turn out badly too, and since I was always changing multiple variables, I never knew what I was learning.

Slowly, I started taking notes and changing only one thing at a time, and eventually, I figured out that I just needed to let the dough rise for longer. Now when I make bread, I always let the dough rise longer than the recipe says because I realize that the timer in the recipe is just a guide. Now I know to look for other clues that the dough has risen long enough, like the fact that it's grown in size, or that it seems airy, or that when I poke it, it slowly springs back. And if the dough doesn't act like it should, I realize that maybe it's because my house is cold today and the dough needs more time.

With my latest loaf, I realized that my bread was better, but I wasn't necessarily a better baker. I was more confident, certainly, because I wasn't as nervous when I mixed up the dough, and I believed that if I baked again, I could probably make a good loaf. But that's not the same as skill. My skill increased because I could bake a good loaf, explain how I did it, and do it again.

I might still add too much flour when I knead the dough, but now I know that I do that, and I know that next time I knead dough, I'll work on not doing that. It's funny how learning to bake bread has taught me so much about learning in general, and how even small changes can make a big difference.

### Review

{"id": "W153", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Specific note-taking and dough-readiness clues distinguish process knowledge from confidence. Always rising longer contradicts conditional observation, and paragraph first denies better skill then claims increased skill. Otherwise detailed reflective progression."}

### Output

Have you ever wondered why your auntie kept all those small bags?

Well, when I was younger, I wondered it too. When I was a teenager I remember asking her about it, thinking it was one of those weird habits people get as they get older. I remember asking her about it, and she told me to throw some of them out.

Well, a few years ago, when I came to stay with my auntie again, the super of the building had just gotten out of the hospital and was moving slow. Well, his daughter was helping him with some groceries, and was struggling with the bags, so my auntie grabbed a few of her small bags.

She was able to divide up the groceries and put them in the small bags and make it easier for the super to get them into his place. Well, when they were leaving, the super pulled out a small bag of his own to give to my auntie, and said he was going to get her some muffins when he got around to making them.

And she put the bag in with the rest of them.

Later, on the way home, I asked her if she had always saved the bags for the neighbours.

And she said no, that when she first moved in, the super's wife used to bring her soup when she was working late, and she used to put it in a bag so it would be easier to carry on the bus. And, from what she said, it seemed like it was nothing, like it was just the natural thing to do. And, ever since then, when my auntie starts to smooth out a bag, I make sure not to throw it in the recycle bin.

### Review

## W159 — Personal communication / Diary entry

Round 2; 366 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a diary entry about spending a rainy day at home without completing a planned list of chores. Let the narrator's feelings change through small experiences; keep it private in tone rather than addressing readers. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

It is raining today, it started before I got out of bed. I had made a list of things I was going to do, things I should do. There's laundry to do, I need to vacuum, buy some groceries and I really should clean the bathroom.

At first, I revelled in the fact that I didn't have to be anywhere. I made some coffee, sat by the window and watched the birds scurry about trying to keep dry. I took my time with my coffee and decided I'd get started after breakfast.

After breakfast I did start, I did put the laundry on and then I found a book I had forgotten I owned and before I knew it, an hour had gone by and all I had done was read. I felt a little guilty like I had let someone down, but no one was there.

While the laundry dried, I listened to the weather report and found out that it was going to rain some more. The clothes dried easily and I folded all of them except for the towels, I could do that later.

At noon, I was going to go out and get some groceries but I couldn't be bothered to put on my wet shoes so I had some toast and the rest of the cheese for lunch.

Now it is late afternoon and the list still sits there on the table. I haven't cleaned the bathroom, I haven't vacuumed and I still need to get groceries but instead of feeling bad about it, I feel like I have done something good for myself. It was a nice day to do nothing, a nice day to be kind to myself and realise that the world will still be there tomorrow if I need it to be. It won't fall apart just because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, at most it'll be a little dusty and I'll have to use wet towels until I get around to folding them.

For now, I'm going to turn on my little lamp and wait until tomorrow, when perhaps I'll do something. Perhaps not, but that's ok too, because today was enough.

### Review

Towels were explicitly dry but become wet until folded, a continuity error. Repetitive self-kindness conclusion and 366/300 words exceed tolerance."}
