## W161 — Education / Lesson plan

Round 2; 525 words requested 500; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Create a 45-minute lesson for twelve-year-olds on distinguishing observations from explanations. Include timed activities, a low-cost object-based exercise, and an individual assessment that actually tests the distinction. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

What will the students do? How long will it take? 0-5 min The teacher will show the students a paper helicopter and drop it from shoulder height. Ask the students to tell you what they can report about it. Collect a few answers from the class. Then tell the students that the information they can gather with their senses or with a tool is called an observation. Then tell them that when you try to explain what you observed it is called an explanation. Tell them that even if your explanation is correct it is not considered an observation.

5-9 min The teacher will give an example of an observation and an explanation. For example, the teacher can roll a coin on a table and say the observation is that the coin rolled for two seconds and the explanation is that the coin stopped rolling because the table was too rough for the coin to keep rolling. Once the students can pick out the difference between the two with the teacher's help they will work on their own for a few minutes to come up with their own explanations and observations using the sentence starters "I observed..." and "I think this happened because..."

9-24 min Working in partners students will be given a paper helicopter and they will drop the helicopter from the same height three times. They will record their observations and only their observations. The students will be working with scrap paper so they can attach a paperclip to the end of the helicopter to help it drop. The teacher may want to pre-cut the helicopters for the students. Once the students have observed the helicopter for three drops they will write down their observations. They will write down one explanation for why they think the helicopter spun as it dropped and they will write another explanation for what they could observe to help test their explanation. Remind the students that even if they think their explanation is right they cannot write it in the observation column of the lab sheet. For example they may not write that the blades are catching the air as it goes down.

24-30 min Once the students have finished the lab sheet they will exchange notebooks with another pair and circle one observation and underline one explanation. Then as a class the students will discuss what makes a good observation and what makes a good explanation.

30-38 min The students will be given four statements two observations and two explanations and they have to mark the observations with an O and the explanations with an E.

38-45 min The students will be given a new situation and they will work alone to mark the observations with an O and the explanations with an E. They will also be asked to write one more observation they could make for that situation. The teacher will collect the assessments to see which students mastered the skill. Each correct answer will be worth one point. For example the teacher may say that there was a puddle on the playground at 9:00 in the morning and at 12:00 the ground was dry.

### Review

{"id": "W161", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Timings sum to 45 minutes; low-cost helicopter activity and individual classification fit objective. Actual assessment statements and explanation alternatives are not supplied, so teacher must finish instrument. Collapsed headings and awkward wording of testing predictions reduce usability."}

## W162 — Education / Study guide

Round 2; 645 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a study guide on learning unfamiliar vocabulary using retrieval and spacing. Provide a realistic five-day schedule, explain feedback, and avoid promising instant mastery. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Step One: Make a List Choose a handful of words that you would like to learn. It is actually better if you have a smaller set of vocabulary words to learn (6-10) because you will spend more time learning each word. Copy down each word, along with it's pronunciation, part of speech, meaning, and an example. (You may also want to copy down a translation of the word or some kind of cue that will help you remember the word's meaning.)

Step Two: Use Retrieval Practice Extensive research has shown that one of the best ways to learn something is to practice retrieving it from your memory. This is sometimes known as "practice testing." It is more effective to spend time quizzing yourself than it is to simply read over your vocabulary list. You should practice retrieving the words in a variety of ways. First, cover up the meaning of the word and try to say what it means. Then, do the reverse: look at the meaning and try to say what the word is. Be sure to say the words out loud, and even better to write them down. It is also helpful to practice using each word in a sentence.

Step Three: Space Your Practice! Research shows that it is most effective to practice over a period of several days so that you have some time elapse between practice sessions. You should spend about 10-15 minutes a day learning your vocabulary words. Below is a possible study schedule that you could follow. Day One: Learn your vocabulary words by making up study cards or notes (as outlined above). Go through each word, read it, say it, and describe its meaning. Then take a short break, and go through the words again, trying to retrieve the words without looking at your notes. Be sure to check your answers at the end and to note which words you had difficulty with.

Day Two: Start by giving yourself a quiz (without looking at your notes) on all of the vocabulary words. Be sure to try to retrieve the words in both directions (from word to meaning, and from meaning to word). Check your answers right away, and then study any words that you didn't remember. Go through those words again a few minutes later to be sure you've learned them. Spend more time studying the words that you had trouble with, but also check the words you did remember.

Day Three: Again, start by testing yourself, but try a different format. You could, for example, match words to their meanings, or fill in the blanks in sentences that include the words you are learning. Be sure to create new sentences using each word, and check to make sure you are using the words correctly.

Day Four: Spend a little less time studying today. Start by trying to remember the words without any prompt, and then prompt yourself by looking at the meanings of the words. Spend more time on words that you continue to have trouble with. If the words have multiple meanings, be sure to learn those as well.

Day Five: Give yourself one last quiz to see how much you have learned! Again, check your answers and make note of how well you remembered each word. Be sure to continue to review any words that you are still having trouble with over the next few days and weeks.

An important part of practice is to get feedback about how well you are doing. Be sure to check your answers, especially when you are not sure how to answer a question. It may not take long for you to feel like you've learned the words, but be sure to practice the above steps. In just five days, you will have learned the words much more effectively than if you had just read over the list!

### Review

{"id": "W162", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Concrete five-day retrieval, spacing, feedback, and continued review plan. Matching can rely on recognition, and final five-day effectiveness promise is too certain for individuals. 645/500 words exceeds tolerance; some repetitive instructions and possessive error."}

## W163 — Education / Classroom debate

Round 2; 381 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a balanced classroom debate brief on allowing notes during exams. Distinguish tests of recall from tests of application, consider fairness, and offer a policy that depends on learning objectives. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Should students be allowed to use notes on tests? I believe that it depends on what the test is looking for. For instance, if the test is asking for recall based information, such as vocabulary, then the students should not be allowed to use notes. Students need to be able to remember certain things that are important for future classes, or for the real world.

On the other hand, if the test is looking for application based information, then I think that the students should be able to use notes. There are many jobs in the real world that require use of reference material, and it is important to be able to apply information rather than simply regurgitate it. Additionally, this would decrease the advantage that people who are able to remember large amounts of information have on a test.

Another thing to consider is the idea of what is fairest to the students. On one hand, if the test is closed note, then it may be unfair to students who have trouble memorizing large amounts of information, or students who have trouble studying at home due to distractions. On the other hand, if the test is open note, then it may be unfair to students who are unable to find the information that they need quickly, or students who are not clear on what is and is not allowed to be used.

I believe that the best test policy is to make a decision based on what information the teacher wishes the students to know for the test. If it is based on recall of basic information, then the test should be closed note. If the test is based on application of information, then it should be open note, though only allowing a certain amount of material, such as a reference sheet or certain course material. Additionally, a test may have a portion that is open note, and a portion that is closed note, testing both recall and application.

Whatever the policy is, it should be made clear to the students ahead of time, and it should be fair to all students. Additionally, it should not be based on whether or not the test is difficult, but what the teacher is looking for the students to learn from the material.

### Review

{"id": "W163", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Clear recall/application distinction, fairness considerations, and possible split exam policy. Repeats thesis and makes closed-note rule somewhat categorical; 381/300 words exceeds target tolerance."}

## W165 — Education / Student feedback

## W167 — Education / Child-friendly explanation

Explain to an eight-year-old why different maps show different things. Use a concrete example and explain symbols without referring to an image that is not supplied. Aim for 150 words.

Maps are special drawings of places.

Maps usually only show one thing at a time. Think about your town. One map might show you all of the roads. Another map might show you the weather. A third map might show you where the parks are in your town.

Maps use symbols to show places. A map of your town might use a blue squiggly line to show a river. It might use a picture of a tree to show where a park is. It might use a star to show a school or other important places. If you do not know what the symbols mean, look for the key. The key, sometimes called the legend, will tell you what each symbol means. Maps also use color to show what the symbols are. For example, the color blue might show water. The color green might show plants.

All of these maps are different because they are meant to do different things.

## W169 — Education / Assessment rubric

Round 2; 450 words requested 300; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Create a rubric for a short science presentation. Allocate 80% to explanation and evidence and 20% to organization. Give clear performance descriptors and state that nervousness or accent alone should not lower marks. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Scoring Guide Oral Science Presentation Area of Assessment Area and Weight Total Score Strong Secure Developing Beginning

I. Explanation and Evidence: (80 points) 72-80: The student clearly and accurately explains the science and key ideas, using age-appropriate language. The student also connects key ideas and explains the importance of these connections. The student provides specific evidence (for example, examples, data, or observations) from reliable sources that supports their claims. The student also explains how the evidence supports their claims. 56-71: The student accurately explains the main ideas of the science with minor gaps or errors in explanation. The student includes relevant evidence or examples, and usually explains how those support their claims. Some ideas and/or evidence could use more explanation. 40-55: The student's explanation demonstrates a partial understanding of the science, with important ideas missing or significant errors in explanation. The student's use of evidence to support their claims is limited, vague, or not clearly connected. 0-39: The student's explanation demonstrates little understanding of the science. Claims are not supported or are based on evidence that is missing or not relevant, or the explanation includes significant misunderstandings of the topic.

II. Organization: (20 points) 18-20: The presentation has a clear opening and ending, and the information is presented in a logical sequence. The student uses appropriate transitions and effectively concludes the presentation. The presentation fits within the time limit, and, if applicable, any visual or other materials used by the student serve a clear purpose. 14-17: The presentation has a beginning, middle, and ending, and the information is presented in a mostly logical sequence, with minor lapses in one or more of the following: transitions, staying within the time limit, or use of materials. 10-13: While some organization is evident, the presentation either lacks connections between ideas, is repetitive, or lapses frequently in one or more of the following: transitions, staying within the time limit, or use of materials. 0-9: The presentation lacks organization, either because it does not follow a clear sequence of ideas, does not have a clear ending, or lapses frequently in one or more of the following: transitions, staying within the time limit, or use of materials.

Scoring Notes: Score each area separately, and then add them together for a total score of 100. Within each range of scores, assign the number of points based on how consistently the oral presentation matches the description for that range. Score the content of the student's presentation, not how the student speaks. Do not deduct points simply because the student is nervous or has an accent, unless their speaking makes it difficult to understand their explanation. If you use this criterion for scoring, apply it consistently for all students.

### Review

## W170 — Education / Teaching reflection

Round 2; 215 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a teacher's reflection on a lively group activity that produced weak individual understanding. Use a specific example involving fractions and propose a next lesson and a check for learning. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

Today, my students had a blast doing a hands-on lesson about fractions. They were able to work in small groups to compare fractions, and create equivalent fractions, by using paper strips. They shared, they talked, and they felt very confident with their work.

Then, they were given an exit slip to do individually to assess their learning. Several of the students who did the activity together couldn’t even tell me why 1/2=2/4. One student even said 2/4 was bigger because 4 is bigger than 2! So even though they may have been able to do the activity successfully in a group, it was obvious that they had just copied someone.

To help my students, I will re-teach the lesson briefly and have them create and label their own drawings to show equivalent fractions. After I give them a brief explanation and show them how to justify their answers, I will give them a few to do on their own, and then they can talk about them with a partner.

I will then give them another short assignment to do on their own to make sure they have grasped the concept. For example, they may have to draw a model to show that 3/6=1/2 and explain in a sentence how they know this to be true.

### Review

## W171 — Marketing and product writing / Product launch plan

Round 2; 538 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Create a modest launch plan for a fictional reusable lunch container. Include testing claims before publication, clear specifications, feedback collection, customer support, and success measures without fabricated sales forecasts. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

Well, that depends on the product and the type of business. Let's say that it is a new food container that is reusable. Maybe you would want to start out small, put it on your website and have a few local stores carry it. But, before you take orders make sure you have a product page that has all of the product details, pictures, etc., how to care for it, price, shipping information, and how a customer would make a request for a return or warranty service. Also, make sure that you do not include any claims that you have not verified.

For the product details, I would list the information that you have but use a model name or number so that if you have more than one container you know what product you are referring to. Let's say that the name of this container is the "LoopBox 900" (made up name for this answer). It has a capacity of 900 ml, is 19 x 13 x 6 cm, and weighs 280 grams. You would also want to list if it comes with a lid or not, if it is dishwasher safe, if it can go in the microwave, if it can be used in the freezer, what it is made of (stainless steel that is approved to come in contact with food, with a silicone gasket that can be removed for cleaning), etc. Do not make any claims that it is leakproof, stain resistant, or anything else unless you have done the testing to be able to make that claim or you state how it can or cannot be used.

Speaking of testing, you should test the product and your claims before you publish the information. Make sure that you are meeting all of the requirements for the areas that you will be selling the product in. You can document your testing and make updates as needed. You will want to have the documentation to show that your claims are true and that you have done the due diligence to provide a good product to your customers.

Also, consider getting some testers to use your product and give you feedback on it. You can use some of the customer feedback on your product page (with their permission). Be sure to also provide a way for your customers to give you feedback.

Finally, make sure that you have your customer support set up prior to launching your product. You need to respond to the customer's needs and quickly address any issues that come up. If there is an issue with the product that is a safety concern or seems to be a wide-spread issue, you would want to stop promoting and selling the product until it can be addressed.

You should also periodically review how the product is doing. For the first few weeks, you may want to do it weekly and then maybe monthly after that. Review things like the number shipped, the number of returns, the defect and complaint rates, your customer support response and resolution rates, your customer survey results, etc. You will want to set some metrics prior to launching the product so that you can see if you are meeting your goals.

### Review

{"id": "W171", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Modest staged launch with specifications, claim testing, feedback, support and metrics. Fictional product details are appropriate but could be more clearly labeled illustrative; generic repeated make-sure language and no actual threshold values reduce specificity."}

## W172 — Marketing and product writing / Brand story

Round 2; 493 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional brand story for a small workshop making reading lamps. Build around a practical design problem and successive prototypes, acknowledge a remaining tradeoff, and avoid life-changing promises. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

The first lamp Mara made for herself didn’t work. It was too bright.

Mara lives in a small space, and the light flooded into the room when she wanted to read a book while her partner slept. She tried shrouding the lamp with a scarf, but it didn’t work. She tried wrapping the lamp in baking parchment, and the light was a bit better, though her reading space smelled like toast.

Mara runs a small metalworking shop with her brother, Eli. Together they brainstormed solutions to her problem, and decided to see if they could work on building the perfect reading lamp.

This ended up taking a while. The first prototypes didn’t work, as the lamp had to be perfect for a very specific purpose. Subsequent prototypes had issues with being able to extend far enough without using springs, which meant they required a counterweight, and it ended up being too big for what Mara needed.

Slowly, they iterated on the design, keeping a close notebook of all the little details that ended up being important: the placement of the switch, the length of the light, the warmth of the shade. They ended up creating a perforated shade, diffusing the light from a warm white bulb, a warmer bulb than usual with a reflector to direct more light onto the page, and a joint to hold the shape of the lamp, without using a spring or counterweight.

The fourth version of the lamp eventually had issues with the joint not being tight enough to hold the weight of the shade, and had to be changed to be slightly tighter, which meant that tightening it was a bit of a pain. After a few conversations on how to fix this, they settled on a large washer and a thumb screw, which could be easily tightened and loosened by hand, but was a bit more visible and would require occasional tightening.

Working through all of the kinks of the design, they settled on the final design for their lamp, called the Fold (named for the little fold in the arm of the lamp). Each one is made in their shop, made of steel and carefully bent into shape, and finished with a matte finish so that it doesn’t get finger prints. The switch on the end of the cord is right by the base of the lamp for easy access, and the whole thing is a work of art.

The Fold isn’t the perfect lamp for every situation, but if you’re looking for a good lamp to read by, or one to use when you’re working on something that requires you to write in a notebook, you’ll love it. And if it needs to be adjusted in a few years, you’ll be able to do it easily.

In the meantime, Mara and Eli are still taking repair jobs, and are keeping their first lamp prototypes on display in their shop, including the slightly toasted one.

### Review

{"id": "W172", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Specific light-spill problem, mechanical prototypes, thumb-screw tradeoff and workshop continuity give coherent brand story. Some clumsy mechanism descriptions and absolute fingerprint/appeal claims need tightening; no life-transformation promise."}

## W173 — Marketing and product writing / Case study

Round 2; 363 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a clearly fictional case study of a neighborhood club trying an online sign-up sheet. Describe the previous process, a short trial, a useful change, and one remaining difficulty. Do not invent precise productivity gains. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Cedar Lane Neighborhood Club used to hold a monthly community dinner in the back room of their library. People used to sign up to help with the event by writing their names on a sheet that was passed around at the beginning of every meeting. If people weren’t at the meeting and wanted to sign up, they called the person in charge of the dinner and either left a voicemail or talked to another member of the club. Then the person in charge of the dinner had to copy down all the names that were given to them and try to remember what everyone said they were helping with.

This way of getting people signed up to help wasn’t working very well.

So the Cedar Lane Neighborhood Club decided to try using a very basic online sign-up sheet for at least two of their monthly events. When the next meeting rolled around, the person in charge of the dinner showed everyone how they could use the online sign-up sheet. They still had a paper sheet for people who wanted to use it, and the person in charge of the dinner wrote down those names on the online sheet.

The Cedar Lane Neighborhood Club made some changes to their online sign-up sheet and now they give a little more information about what each task is. For example, if someone needs to bring a dessert, there is a note on the sign-up sheet reminding them to also bring utensils to serve the dessert with. And if someone signs up to help set up, they are reminded to arrive early and to help set up tables and chairs.

Some members of the Cedar Lane Neighborhood Club still don’t use email very often, so they aren’t able to use the online sign-up sheet. And they don’t want to start using one. So the person in charge of the dinner still has to accommodate people who call in to sign up or who sign up using the paper sheet. But at least now they have something to help them out a little bit. And even though it isn’t perfect, it is better than what they were using before.

### Review

{"id": "W173", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Before/trial/change/remaining barrier are present without invented gains. Fictional status is not explicit, repeats club name and signup process, and equates infrequent email with inability to use an online sheet. 363/300 words slightly exceeds tolerance."}

## W174 — Marketing and product writing / Landing page

Round 2; 267 words requested 300; Pangram: Mixed.

### Prompt

Write landing-page copy for a tool that helps community groups coordinate borrowed equipment. Explain who it is for, three concrete steps to get started, and the limits of what it guarantees. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Do you have equipment that your group would like to share with the community? Whether it’s a neighborhood association, tool library, school club, mutual aid team, arts group, or something else entirely, CommonKit is here to help you manage your gear.

Add the items you want to share, see when they’re being requested, and schedule pickups and returns. It’s an easy way to see what’s available without having to buy it yourself or beg people in your group chat to stop taking stuff.

It only takes three steps to get started: 1. Create your group space and invite your members 2. Add your items and include any relevant information, like quantity, condition, storage location, or borrowing guidelines. 3. Encourage your members to request items and schedule pick-ups and returns!

CommonKit does its best to help you organize and schedule items, but we can’t guarantee that an item will be available, in good condition, returned on time, or even the right tool for the job. A lot of that comes down to the people listing the items and the people borrowing them communicating well and setting realistic expectations. CommonKit also cannot facilitate the physical exchange of items or offer any form of insurance for your property, so we recommend creating rules around who can borrow, whether you’ll collect a damage deposit, how you’ll handle late returns or damaged items, and what you expect borrowers to do to take care of your stuff.

Get started by adding a few items that your members might want to borrow, invite your members to check them out, and watch your kit grow!

Hello,

I'll give you simple ideas that you can put into practice, even if you don't own any fancy equipment and you don't know much about photography.

Each month I'll teach you a quick lesson with an example and then set you a small exercise that you can practice in your everyday life. Sometimes it will include learning a little bit about your camera settings, but sometimes it will simply be about learning to see and being creative. Don't worry though, there's no homework to hand in and there's no right way to take a photo!

This month we're going to keep things very simple. Take an everyday object that you have to hand. It could be a mug, a plant, a pair of shoes - whatever you like. Now take three photos of your object from three different viewpoints. Try taking one photo at eye level, one from above it and one from low down. Look at your photos afterwards and see how the viewpoint has changed how you see the object.

I look forward to taking you on this journey with me!

Happy photographing,

The Photography Newsletter Team

## W179 — Marketing and product writing / Comparison copy

Round 2; 282 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Compare pocket-size and desk-size versions of the same planner, with identical paper and binding. Explain portability and writing-space tradeoffs without claiming one is universally better or inventing prices. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

The pocket size planner and the desk size planner are exactly the same when it comes to paper and binding.

The biggest difference is, of course, portability. The pocket size version is much easier to throw in a small bag or even a coat pocket. It is also easier to jot down information on the go. There are also some people who have commented that they like the smaller size because there is less writing area. If you are someone who procrastinates and tends to turn simple tasks into big tasks, perhaps limiting your writing area will help you plan more effectively. Of course, if you have a lot to write about each day or each week, you might find the pocket size version to be a little constraining. You might find yourself shrinking your handwriting or using lots of abbreviations or needing more pages to write all of your tasks down.

The desk size version, on the other hand, is much easier to use when you are sitting at a desk or a table. With the larger version, there is much more writing area so you will be able to write out all of your tasks and notes without shrinking your handwriting or needing more pages. The down side to the larger version is that it is not as easy to carry with you and it takes up more desk space.

So really, it comes down to where you are when you plan and how much you need to write.

Ask yourself if you need all that extra writing area or if the smaller version would be big enough. On the other hand, maybe the larger version isn't even big enough!

### Review

## W181 — Practical guides and reviews / Travel packing

Round 2; 486 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a practical packing guide for a four-day train trip with uncertain weather. Explain rewearing, layers, and luggage limits; distinguish essentials from optional items without assuming every traveler has the same needs. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

The idea is to pack for a fairly small range of conditions. Look at the forecast before you leave, but be prepared for things to change. Also, pack to travel light. You may have to carry your bag around and up stairs and whatnot, so don't take more than you can carry easily. Ideally, you should have one suitcase or backpack (carry-on sized, if possible) and a smaller bag for your person.

Unless you're interested in wearing something new every day, you don't need that much clothing for a four-day trip. I generally pack two or three tops, two bottoms, and an extra set in case I get really dirty or wet (the extra set is entirely contingent on your activities). I'd also suggest wearing your bulkiest shoes and jacket on the trip so that you don't have to take them up and down from the overhead. I assume that you'll want clean socks and underwear for each day, but you should be able to get by with a pair of pajamas for the whole trip.

Bring a few layers, including a rain shell. You can always pack something small like a base layer, sweater or fleece, and either a heavy coat or a packable shell and hat/gloves. An umbrella may or may not be useful, depending on how you deal with inclement weather. You'll definitely want a comfortable pair of shoes if you're planning to walk a lot. Whether you need a second pair of shoes depends on your plans.

You'll also want to bring your essentials. This probably includes things like money, ID, tickets, and any medication that you need. If you wear glasses or hearing aids, don't forget them. I'm sure you'll want to charge your phone while you're on the train, so you'll need your charger. Toiletries should go in a leakproof bag. If you need any personal care items or other medical supplies, those should also go in your bag.

Finally, there are a few other items that you may or may not want to bring depending on your needs. I like to bring a water bottle and some snacks in case I get hungry. If you're likely to want to sleep at some point on the train, you might want to bring earplugs and an eyeshade. Entertainment is always a good idea. You may also want to bring a power bank in case you're away from an outlet for a long time. I don't know if you'll need a day bag or not, but bring one if you think you'll need it when you get to your destination. A small soap for doing laundry could be helpful, too. If you feel like you need anything else, that's up to you, but I'd urge you to make sure you aren't bringing anything you don't need and to leave some space in your bag for things you might buy on the trip.

### Review

{"id": "W181", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Practical rewearing, layers, carrying limits, essentials and conditional extras acknowledge differing needs. Does not explicitly advise checking train-operator luggage restrictions; repeated conversational qualifiers and undefined extra set could be tighter."}

## W182 — Practical guides and reviews / Photography tutorial

Round 2; 612 words requested 500; Pangram: AI.

### Prompt

Explain how to photograph a small ceramic bowl using a phone and window light. Establish the light direction before giving reflector-placement advice, provide clear steps, and suggest comparing results. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

First, clean the lens of your phone camera, then place your bowl on a nice simple surface near a window and switch off any room lights so that the window is the only source of light. Try not to have the sun shining directly on your bowl as it will create hard shadows and very bright highlights, but if you do want to try this then great, just see how you go. If the sun is shining through your window try putting a thin white curtain over it to soften the light. Make sure you have enough space around the bowl to move around a bit and try different things.

Next, before you bring in any reflectors etc, you need to sort out the direction of your light. For a nice clear image that shows off the shape of your bowl, the light needs to be coming from the side and slightly behind the bowl, about 45deg. So the window should be at this position in relation to the bowl, and you will be standing (or your phone will be positioned) on the opposite side of the bowl, facing the bowl. The light from this direction shows off the shape of the ceramic nicely and also gives a nice highlight on the glaze. Before taking your first photo check that you can't see the window reflecting on the glaze, if you can then move the bowl or yourself a little until it's not visible. Now, keep the direction of light the same and move on to the next step.

Now that you've got the direction of your light sorted, move the bowl a little bit away from the window and position yourself or your phone so that the camera is at the same level as the top rim of the bowl, or slightly above it if you want to show a little bit of the inside of the bowl. Make sure you use the main camera on your phone, not the zoom, and turn your grid on to make sure you keep the bowl level in your picture. Tap on the bowl on your screen to focus, and if you can, lock your focus/exposure and drop the exposure down a little bit if the glaze colour is light and looks a bit washed out. Take a picture now, with no reflector, so that you have something to compare your other shots to.

Now you can try adding a reflector if you feel the shadow on the bowl is too dark. Just get a piece of white card, foam board, even a piece of white paper and position it on the opposite side of the bowl to the window. Keep it close to the bowl but make sure it's not in the picture. Move it closer or further away until you are happy with the softness of the shadow. Be careful not to bring in too much light though, as your picture can end up looking flat if you do, so if this happens just move the reflector away or remove it all together. Also, for the moment don't try using a shiny reflector as it will reflect bright spots onto the ceramic.

Now take another picture and compare the two. You can also try taking a picture with the reflector a little further away, or turn the bowl slightly and take another picture. Just make sure you only change one thing at a time so you can tell which picture you like best. Once you've decided on your best picture just make sure there's nothing you didn't notice before creeping in at the edges of your picture and take a final shot.

### Review

{"id": "W182", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Establishes light direction before opposite-side reflector and gives controlled before/after comparisons. Some absolute composition/light recommendations and repeated now wording; 612/500 words exceeds tolerance slightly. Steps are usable with minor editing."}

## W183 — Practical guides and reviews / Gardening guide

Round 2; 331 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a beginner's guide to growing herbs on a windy balcony. Discuss observing sunlight, stable containers, drainage, and adjusting watering. Explain safe runoff management without claiming one schedule suits every plant. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Check the sunlight exposure of your potential herb garden area. Do this before you buy your plants. Most herbs love sunshine, although some can manage with less. Make sure that they are sheltered from the worst of the wind. Otherwise they will dry out quickly.

Make sure that your containers are secure and not prone to tipping over or being blown over by the wind. Heavy containers, low planters and containers with a wide base are best. If you are hanging plant boxes on your balcony railing, make sure that you secure them properly. It is a good idea to group your containers together to provide shelter for your plants.

Make sure that your containers have sufficient drainage and that you use the right type of soil -- potting soil. You will also need saucers or trays under your containers to collect the drainage. Empty these out as needed so that your plants are not sitting in water. Be considerate of your fellow apartment dwellers and make sure that your water runoff doesn't drip onto balconies below or onto walkways. Don't dump water over the edge of your balcony, of course, and observe your building's rules about drainage.

You will need to pay attention to watering. Different herbs need different amounts of water. The size of the container your herb is in will make a difference in how often you need to water. Weather conditions and the exposure of your balcony will also be factors. On a windy, sunny day, your herbs will probably need to be watered every day if they are in small pots. In larger pots, in a sheltered location, they will need less frequent watering. Stick your finger in the soil to check. If it is dry, water slowly until some of the water runs out of the drainage holes in the bottom of the pot. If it is still moist, wait a while. Get to know your plants and their needs, and check on them regularly.

### Review

{"id": "W183", "adherence": "5", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Practical sunlight, wind stability, drainage, and variable watering guidance. Explicit tray emptying and runoff handling address common ambiguity; repeated make-sure phrasing and approximate watering example need minor polish."}

## W184 — Practical guides and reviews / Home organization

Round 2; 378 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a guide to organizing a shared entryway by observing how people use it. Include a small trial, agreed locations, accessibility, and how to revise the arrangement when clutter returns. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Get started Try these steps to work out what you want to achieve with your entryway. For a few days, keep an eye on how your entryway is used. Notice when it is busiest, how each member of your household uses the space, and any differences in height, reach, mobility and routines. Talk to the people in your household about what they need and what is getting in the way.

Work out what the main functions of your entryway are. Test out your ideas for a week or two. You can make a few changes to trial without buying an organising system - you may be able to use containers or labels you already have. Try moving frequently used shoes to an easy to reach shoe rack, or putting a basket in the place where bags seem to always end up. Make your trial small so it is easy to change.

Agree where different things will be kept. It is best to put things where they are used or put away. Make sure that everyone knows where things are to be stored. Make sure everyone in the household has somewhere to put the things they use, and agree on what won't be kept in the entryway. Also make sure that you have agreed on somewhere to keep things that are used by everyone.

Think about access in your entryway. Make sure that the way in and out of your doorway is easy, particularly for members of your household who use mobility aids. Try to keep things that are used frequently within easy reach for everyone who needs to use them. Don't put heavy things overhead. Try to use hooks, shelves and containers that everyone in your household can use themselves.

At the end of your trial period, work out what is working and what isn't. If the entryway is getting cluttered up again, work out why. Maybe the place you have chosen for something isn't convenient, or there is not enough room for what you need to store, or maybe the things you are trying to store aren't supposed to be in your entryway. Change one thing at a time and agree with the household on what you are trying. After a week, see if it is working.

### Review

{"id": "W184", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Observation-based trial, shared agreement, reach/mobility access and iterative diagnosis are practical. Repeated make-sure phrases and 378/300 words exceed tolerance; no major content defect."}

### Prompt

Explain how to roast a tray of mixed vegetables when some cook faster than others. Discuss cut size, spacing, staggered additions, and checking doneness without promising a universal cooking time. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

The best way to do this is to separate all the vegetables by how long they will take to cook. If you are using potatoes, carrots or squash, cut them smaller than zucchini, peppers or asparagus. Make sure to keep each vegetable similar in size to each other.

Place all the vegetables in a single layer on a very sturdy baking tray. You may need two trays, as you don't want the vegetables touching one another. Toss them with a good amount of oil and season to your liking.

Place the slower cooking vegetables in the oven first. When they are close to being done, but still require a bit more time, add the faster cooking vegetables to the tray, or a second tray. This step will be different for everyone, depending on what vegetables you choose to roast.

Take a fork or knife and pierce the largest pieces of each vegetable to ensure they are cooked through, and the outside is slightly browned. If the quick cooking vegetables are done first, take them out and continue to roast the others until done. Keep turning and checking all the vegetables until they are done to your liking.

### Review

First puts all vegetables on tray then says start slower ones only, leaving staging slightly unclear; overall practical sequence."}

Two pieces of lightweight paper (two if you'd like it to be different colored on either side)

Pinch one of the ends of the paper together and secure with a small piece of tape or glue. Open up the other side in a fan shape. If you'd like to have handles, glue a craft stick on either side of the pinched end.

## W188 — Practical guides and reviews / Troubleshooting

Round 2; 218 words requested 150; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Give simple steps for investigating a document stuck in a printer queue. Warn against repeatedly clicking Print, distinguish checking the queue from restarting a device, and avoid assuming a particular operating system. Aim for 150 words.

### Output

1. Ensure that the printer is switched on, connected to your computer or network and isn't showing an error (paper jam, low supplies, etc.) and that you have selected the printer you wish to print to.

2. Open the Print Queue of your device (depending on the device, this may be in different locations - if you are unsure, searching for 'print queue' or 'printers' in the settings or help function of your device may help locate this). Check if there is a document stuck in the queue and check the status - if it is paused, try to resume the document. If there is an error with the print job, you may need to resolve this first before attempting to print the document again (you can delete the stuck print job from the queue and only send it through once).

Checking your print queue is different to restarting your printer, which is covered in step 3.

3. Clear your print queue, turn your printer off and on again (this is different to restarting your computer and simply involves turning your printer off, waiting around 30 seconds, and then turning it back on again). If this doesn't work, try restarting your computer or look for support on how to resolve this issue for your specific device and printer.

## W189 — Practical guides and reviews / Repair decision

Round 2; 370 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a practical decision guide for repairing or replacing a worn backpack. Turn inspection, repair cost, fit, and expected use into a clear sequence rather than only listing questions. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

When your backpack shows signs of wear and tear, empty it out and inspect the damage. Look at the fabric of your backpack and the seams, zipper tracks, buckles, shoulder straps and back of the backpack. Determine if the damage is cosmetic or severe. Scuffs or minor issues with a zipper may not be as bad as tears around a shoulder strap, or cracks in the frame or harness of the backpack. Big rips or tears in high-wear areas may render your backpack unusable, especially if it is designed to carry heavy loads. Check to see if the damage is spreading or if it is a minor cosmetic problem.

Determine if the damage to your backpack is something you can fix. You may be able to clean the backpack, fix the zipper with a brush or replace buckles and zipper pulls. Take your backpack to a gear repair shop to have seams replaced, fabric fixed or a zipper replaced. Check the warranty with the backpack manufacturer to see if they will fix any problems related to material defects or poor workmanship. Avoid using tapes and glues to fix a backpack that will be carrying heavy loads.

Compare the estimates from the repair shop to the cost of a new backpack. As a rule of thumb, if the repair costs a fraction of what it will cost to replace the backpack, go for the repair. However, if the repair will cost half of what it will to replace the backpack or if multiple parts are failing, it may be time to replace the backpack. Check the longevity of the repair and find a way to recycle the old backpack.

Determine if you are replacing the backpack, if it fits well and what you will be using it for. Try the backpack on with weight and wear it for a few minutes to see if it fits. If it never fit before, you may want to fix your old backpack. Choose repair or replacement based on how you will be using your backpack. If it will be used for everyday activities or strenuous hiking, make sure the backpack is able to perform, fits well and is the right size for your needs.

### Review

{"id": "W189", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "3", "development": "3", "notes": "Gives ordered inspection, repairability, quote and use steps, but final fit paragraph confusingly recommends fixing the old bag if it never fit. Half-price rule is arbitrary and insufficiently tied to repair life; 370/300 words slightly exceeds tolerance."}

## W190 — Practical guides and reviews / Beginner hobby

Round 2; 260 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write an introduction to collecting postcards for enjoyment. Suggest a manageable theme, storage, and recording uncertain dates; distinguish personal interest from claims about resale value. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

So you would like to collect Post Cards? Great, it is a easy and fun way to learn about different places and view points. It doesn't take a lot of room and you don't need to spend a lot of money to do it.

First, pick a theme. It should be something that you enjoy but not so specific that you will have a hard time finding cards to fill your collection. Some people collect lighthouses, some collect different buildings in their area, some collect botanical cards and others collect different places that they have been to.

Next, find a good storage container for your collection. If possible, use archival quality sleeves and a good quality box to put them in. Don't put them in direct sunlight and keep them in a place that doesn't have a lot of dampness or extreme temperatures. Handle your cards with care, don't write on them and be sure to always handle them with clean, dry hands. It is also a good idea to keep a notebook or spreadsheet of the cards that you have.

It can be difficult to find out exactly when a card was made, so note on your spreadsheet if it is not dated or if you think that it was made around a certain time. Also, keep in mind that you should collect only what you enjoy. Unless you are sure that what you are collecting is worth a lot of money, don't expect to make any money on your collection. Collect only what you enjoy and have fun!

### Review

Unless-sure-it-is-valuable caveat weakens distinction between enjoyment and assured resale returns; repetitive closing, grammar errors, and 260/200 words."}

## W191 — Civic and community writing / Policy brief

Round 2; 607 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a policy brief for a six-week trial of Sunday opening at a community center. Include baseline attendance, access for new users, incremental staffing costs, possible displacement of other services, and decision criteria. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

I assume that a six week trial period will be sufficient to demonstrate that there is a community need to have the Centre open on Sundays, and that this can be achieved at an acceptable cost, without adversely impacting on current services. To that end, the following will need to be done:

Prior to opening the Centre on Sundays for the trial period, record attendance for the previous six weeks, broken down by day of the week, type of activity, and type of user. This will form the baseline attendance upon which to measure change. Obviously, if the Centre is not normally open on Sundays, the baseline measure will be zero. However, you can measure this against attendance on Saturdays and other days, as well as other measures such as room bookings, waiting lists, requests to open on Sundays, etc. Note that you will need to record this information using the same method each time. It will also be a good idea to publish the baseline measure and opening hours for the trial period before it starts.

During the trial period, record the total number of users each Sunday, as well as the number of first time users. You will need to ask users at registration whether they have used the Centre before, and you may want to ask them where they come from and how they heard about the Centre being open. You might also want to collect information on the types of services used, user age, and any other information which might be useful to measure the success of the trial. It would be a good idea to also collect some information on why people are using the Centre, how convenient it is for them, whether it is safe, whether they like the activities being offered, etc. You will also need to record whether you turn people away, whether you have enough room to accommodate everyone who wants to use the Centre, etc. It would be a good idea to compare the attendance from each Sunday, to see whether there is any variation in attendance over the six weeks. A key measure will be the number, and percentage, of people who use the Centre who had not done so before the trial period.

Prior to commencing the trial period, you will need to calculate how much it will cost to run the Centre on Sundays. For example, if you plan to open for five hours, and it takes two people to open and close up, and you pay them $25 per hour (including on-costs), then it will cost $250 per week, or $1,500 over six weeks. You will also need to factor in other costs, such as security, cleaning, etc. Let's say this will cost $100 per Sunday - in total, the trial period will cost around $2100.

During the trial period, you will need to record whether the decision to open on Sundays has had any impact on the Centre's other services - for example, are staff working overtime, are they missing other days because they worked on Sunday, have you had to close some rooms so that they can be used on Sundays which impacts on community groups who normally use those rooms, etc.

At the end of the trial period, you will then need to decide whether to continue to open on Sundays. My suggestion is that you should continue to open on Sundays, provided it can be done within a capped dollar amount, it does not impact on other essential services, and the Centre is getting at least 20 people per Sunday, of which at least 25% are first time users.

### Review

{"id": "W191", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Covers baseline, new users, incremental cost, displacement and concrete proposed thresholds; example totals are correct. Opening assumes trial will demonstrate success, zero Sunday baseline needs counterfactual care, and repeated instructions weaken brief. 607/500 words just above tolerance."}

## W192 — Civic and community writing / Evaluation summary

Round 2; 440 words requested 500; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Summarize a fictional four-week shuttle trial that recorded 560 passenger journeys. Explain what this count shows and what it cannot establish about unique riders, car trips avoided, costs, or the case for expansion. Aim for 500 words.

### Output

This week we look at another common problem with data: failing to understand what a number actually represents. Here's the example: as part of a trial a community shuttle bus has been operating for four weeks. The operators have recorded that 560 passengers have used the shuttle service during the trial period. What can we say about the success of the shuttle service based on this information? On the face of it, 560 passengers seems like a reasonable number. The average per week is 140, which sounds like quite a few people have taken advantage of the trial. Let's have a look at some of the things we might wrongly assume about the data.

The most basic thing to remember about the 560 number is that it does not represent 560 people . Perhaps some people rode on the shuttle service multiple times (maybe they used it every day?) whilst others only rode it once. We also need to consider that the number 560 might include both sides of a round trip - something that is normally only undertaken by one person. Without further information (e.g. a unique, but anonymous ID that each rider uses) we cannot make any conclusions about the number of people who have taken up the trial.

It may also be a mistake to assume that each of the 560 passenger journeys represents a car trip that would otherwise have taken place. For each passenger, there are many alternatives: they may have walked, cycled, taken public transport, car pooled, or even not travelled at all. It's also possible that some of the 560 passengers would have travelled by car anyway, as a passenger . Without very carefully designed research (and even then), it's impossible to tell how many car trips have been avoided, or how many people have changed their travel habits because of the shuttle.

What about the cost of the shuttle service? Without knowing how many hours it's run, how much it cost to set up and operate, how much revenue it's generated, and many other factors, it's impossible to know if the trial has been a good use of money.

Finally, can we use this data to make a case for expanding the service? Perhaps we should, but not based solely on the number 560. There are too many unknowns, and too short a timeframe to know if this service is meeting a long-term need in the community. We need to look at a lot more data, including many of the items listed above, before we can reach any meaningful conclusions about the success of this trial, and whether it should be expanded.

### Review

{"id": "W192", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Correct 140 journeys weekly and distinguishes repeat riders, mode substitution, costs and expansion evidence. Initially calls journeys passengers then corrects itself; says rigorous research still cannot tell avoided trips too absolutely, and unsupported impressive-number framing weakens neutrality."}

## W193 — Civic and community writing / Consultation response

Round 2; 336 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a consultation response supporting a trial of movable seating in a town square. Address clear walking routes, different mobility needs, maintenance responsibility, and how feedback should affect the final layout. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

I support the idea of trialling some movable seating in the square.

However, I think it is vital that there are continuous routes for walking that give access to all areas of the square and to the various destinations on the square and adjacent to it. These routes need to be wide and clear of all obstructions such as seating and tables. The routes should be wide enough for people in wheelchairs, on mobility scooters, people with prams and for people who wish to walk side by side. The routes need to give access to all destinations within and adjacent to the square including any tactile paving, bus stops, road crossings, building entrances and shops. The routes should not inhibit any sight lines, emergency services access, or any other services that need access across the square. If seating is moved then there needs to be clear guidance as to where the seating can be moved to so that it does not create a hazard.

Not everybody is mobile, so as well as seating that can be moved there also needs to be some fixed seating that is stable, with a back and ideally with arms. There also needs to be space next to the seating for people in wheelchairs or on mobility scooters so that they can join others who are seated. The seating needs to be easily accessible from the main routes and ideally there should be seating in both sunny and shaded areas. All of the seating whether fixed or movable needs to be sturdy and comfortable and of a weight that can be easily manoeuvred by people.

It will be important to decide who is responsible for checking, cleaning and storing the seating - presumably the council or a contractor.

Finally, it will be important to take feedback from a wide range of people during the trial period including disabled people, and to publish the feedback. The feedback should inform the final layout of the square and be subject to change if necessary.

### Review

{"id": "W193", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "4", "development": "4", "notes": "Detailed clear routes, mobility access, seating variety, maintenance assignment and feedback. Responsibility remains presumptive and requirement that all seating be movable conflicts with requested fixed stable seats; otherwise constructive."}

## W194 — Civic and community writing / Accessibility review

Round 2; 385 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a fictional accessibility review of a meeting room with a heavy entrance door, unclear signs, movable furniture, and a hearing loop whose operation has not been checked. Offer practical next steps without claiming legal certification. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

Meeting Room Access The door at the entrance to this room is very heavy and I found it really difficult to open when I was carrying lots of things with me. The door is probably difficult for anyone in a wheelchair or anyone using walking aids. It's also very quick to shut, making it even harder to open and there isn't any evidence of a way to call for assistance if required. The staff mentioned that they would help me in if they were available but this isn't a guaranteed solution to the problem.

On entering the room, it was quite spacious although the furniture was not fixed and had been laid out differently as it was being used for a different meeting each time. On this occasion, there was a line of chairs and a table which restricted the space for me to move to some of the chairs. There was not enough space for me to bring my wheelchair or buggy with me to a few of the chairs and it wasn't obvious which chairs I could use. It would be a good idea to lay the room out in an accessible format all the time to make it easy for everyone. It's easy to make a few of the chairs accessible and to place them amongst the other chairs. The staff could easily check the layout of the room prior to each event to make sure that it's accessible. They could also ask for people's preferences when they register for each event.

There were not clear signs around the venue to indicate where the meeting room was. I ended up in the right corridor but wasn't sure which door to go into. It would be a good idea to put some directional signs up at the appropriate places to direct people to the room.

Although there is a hearing loop installed in this room, I have not checked whether it is operational or not.

It would be a good idea to: Have the door assessed and possibly fit an automatic door opener Arrange the furniture in an accessible way and keep it this way Improve the wayfinding within the venue Check whether the hearing loop is working This is not a legal document and does not confirm compliance with any legislative Acts.

### Review

## W195 — Civic and community writing / Public speech

I would like to make a few suggestions to the plans that you might be able to incorporate. First and foremost is the idea of incorporating a “quiet” space for kids. Even if it is very simple it would be great to have an area of the playground for kids who don’t prefer the high energy side of a playground. This would make the playground more accessible to kids with special needs and give the “shyer” kids an area where they feel more comfortable. It could be as simple as a few sensory toys or even just having a small nature or imagination based area.

Secondly I would like to put in a request for more shade and seating around the playground. Having some shade would be very beneficial to the park during warmer months and having benches for caregivers would be much appreciated.

I know that there is a finite space and a finite amount of funds to put towards this project and these things might not be able to be incorporated now but I would ask that you keep these requests in mind when deciding how to invest into the playground. Keeping these things in mind would go a long way towards making the playground more accessible to a greater amount of kids and their caregivers without breaking the budget. Thank you for your time.

Something you expect to spend money on regularly, for example monthly water bills, seasonal purchases of compost, annual rental fees for your garden plot, etc. This will be included in your regular operating budget.

Something you expect to spend money on just once, or very infrequently. For example, a new tool shed may be something that, once purchased, will last for many years.

Money you expect to receive, but do not yet have in hand. For example, if a local business has promised to make a donation, you may reasonably expect that donation to be forthcoming, but you do not yet have that money available to spend.

Money you have already put aside for use in the future. ## W197 — Civic and community writing / Volunteer orientation

### Prompt

Welcome volunteers staffing a neighborhood information desk. Explain greeting visitors, avoiding guesses, protecting private details, asking a coordinator for help, and leaving useful handover notes. Aim for 150 words.

Key things to remember Volunteer Information Desk Thank you for offering to work at our information desk. Please remember that as a volunteer information officer you will need to be friendly and calm. Always greet people and try and find out how you can help them.

Do not guess at information or give out information that you are not sure is correct. It is better to admit that you do not know and check with the coordinator or other appropriate resources. Equally, do not promise people anything that you are not sure you can deliver.

Remember that people may be seeking information on matters that they consider private so you need to be sensitive to their need for confidentiality. This also applies to any written information that you might collect - dispose of it appropriately.

If you are in doubt about anything, ask the coordinator for help. Finally, please make sure that you give a good handover at the end of your shift by writing notes for the next information officer. Again, keep in mind the need for confidentiality - only write down what is necessary.

## W199 — Civic and community writing / Impact report

Round 2; 368 words requested 300; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Write a report on a tool library that enrolled 160 members and recorded 710 loans in six months. Preserve those figures, distinguish activity from measured impact, and propose evidence needed to estimate household savings or waste reduction. Aim for 300 words.

### Output

In the first 6 months since our tool library launched, 160 people enrolled as members and there have been 710 tool loans. That's an average of 4.4 loans for each enrollee! How much money will the average household save? How much will waste be reduced?

Well, actually all we know is that the tool library has been loaned out 710 times. We don't actually know how much people saved (if anyone). People may have bought the tools they needed anyway. We don't know how many people actually borrowed tools. We don't know if a few people borrowed a lot of tools and the rest of them didn't borrow anything. We don't know what environmental impact, if any, our tool library has had.

To find out how much people saved, you need to talk to them. You could survey people every time they borrow a tool, or maybe once every few months, to find out if they would have bought the item, rented it, borrowed it from a neighbour or friend, or done without it. You could even ask them to bring in receipts to prove it, or at least an estimate of what it would have cost. Make sure you take into account what they paid for membership and to borrow the items, the cost of getting to the tool library, and what it cost to repair or replace items. Work out a range and report the difference between estimated and verified savings.

To find out how much waste you've reduced, you need to know how many tools people didn't buy because they were able to borrow them, and whether those tools are still in good condition or need repairing. You'll need to keep track of how many tools you get, repair, replace and discard, and how they are disposed of. You could try comparing your tool library to a group of similar people who don't have access to a tool library, or survey tool library members about how often they borrowed or bought tools before the tool library started.

So, while the 6-month results are impressive, we need to look for more evidence before we can report on the financial and environmental impacts of our tool library.

### Review

{"id": "W199", "adherence": "4", "coherence": "3", "development": "4", "notes": "Preserves 160/710 and correct rounded 4.4 average; distinguishes activity from impact and suggests counterfactual questions. Says library itself was loaned, proposes receipts to prove hypothetical purchases, and calls results impressive without benchmark. 368/300 words exceeds tolerance."}

## W200 — Civic and community writing / Community mediation

Round 2; 207 words requested 200; Pangram: Human.

### Prompt

Summarize a disagreement between residents who want outdoor music and those who want quiet evenings. State shared interests and propose a specific, time-limited compromise with a review process in a neutral third-person voice. Aim for 200 words.

### Output

On one side, you have people who want to enjoy music outside. On the other, you have people who want to enjoy their homes in quiet.

What both sides want is to be able to enjoy the outdoors in a safe and pleasant way, to treat each other well and to have some rules to follow. Since both groups want the same things, why can’t we find a solution that works for both of us? Why don’t we try, for 8 weeks, to allow outdoor amplified music on Fridays and Saturdays from 5:30 until 8:00 PM, and not any louder than X volume? If anyone wants to have music, they need to give at least 3 days notice to people, and be sure the speakers are not pointed towards anyone’s house, and the music has to be over by 8:00 PM. During the week, you’re allowed to have music outside, but not amplified, and it needs to be over by 7:00 PM.

At the end of those 8 weeks, we can all fill out a survey, meet with each other, and decide if we want to continue with this arrangement. If we don’t, it will simply expire after 8 weeks, unless there’s an agreement to extend it.

### Review

{"id": "W200", "adherence": "3", "coherence": "4", "development": "3", "notes": "Specific eight-week schedule and review/expiry provided with shared interests. Uses you/we instead of neutral third person and leaves X volume unresolved, so compromise needs completion before practical use."}
